# PiCache architecture

PiCache is a single Go binary with an embedded web UI. It combines:

- a **filtering DNS server**, the central DNS for a home or lab network: blocklists, custom rules, per-client groups, local records, conditional forwarding, encrypted upstreams (DoT, DoH, DoQ, DNS over HTTP/3, DNSCrypt) and encrypted DNS for its clients (DoT and DoH, 19);
- a **download cache** for game and OS downloads: DNS answers that point the CDN names of download services at the cache, an HTTP slice cache on :80, and SNI pass-through on :443; it works with the cache-domains lists, Steam's cache discovery and prefill tools;
- one **web UI and REST API** to operate both, including NAS-backed cache storage.

This document is the binding specification for the implementation. Where it says MUST, the code must do exactly that. The numbers and behaviours below were verified against upstream source (uklans/cache-domains, other open-source DNS filters and download caches, Linux kernel, systemd, moby) in September 2026 and then hardened by an adversarial design review.

Priorities, in this order: **security, simplicity, correctness, performance, features.**

---

## 1. Goals and non-goals

**Goals**
- One process, one config database, one UI. No nginx, BIND, dnsmasq or cron underneath.
- Deployable on Linux with systemd 247 or later (bare metal, VMs, Proxmox LXC; supported: Debian 12/13, Ubuntu 22.04+, Fedora, RHEL/Alma/Rocky 9+, Arch, openSUSE Tumbleweed and Leap 16; others get a warning) through `install.sh` or, on Debian and derivatives, a Debian package (14.1), and in Docker (also on a NAS: templates for Unraid, TrueNAS SCALE and Synology in `deploy/`), with clearly identified persistent data.
- Secure defaults: no open resolver, no open proxy, no open TLS relay, unprivileged runtime, authenticated UI, strict CSP.
- Download caching for Steam, Epic, Battle.net, Riot, Xbox/WSUS, PlayStation, Nintendo, … that works with the cache-domains lists, Steam's cache discovery and prefill tools (the heartbeat path they probe and the response header they check).
- First-class observability: who requested what, what is cached, how long it stays, how much bandwidth was saved.
- Encrypted DNS for the devices of the LAN: DNS over TLS and DNS over HTTPS with device IDs (ClientIDs), discovery of the designated resolvers (DDR), Apple configuration profiles and an optional switch that closes plain DNS, all behind the DNS ACL (19).
- Optional local DNSSEC validation (`dns.dnssecMode`, 7.6): PiCache checks the answers of its upstreams itself along a chain of trust from the built-in root trust anchors and sets AD only from its own verdict; `passthrough` keeps relying on the upstream's validator.
- Runs well on a Raspberry Pi 4 or a 1–2 GB LXC. The minimum is 512 MB of memory (256 MB is unsupported): the entry budget of the blocklists follows the memory (7.2); on armv6 (Pi Zero W, Pi 1) the download cache is not recommended and few lists are. amd64, arm64 and armv7 are supported; armv6, 386 (SSE2) and riscv64 are best effort (built, vetted and started under emulation in CI, not tested on hardware).
- Optional: a DHCP server (and IPv6 DNS announcements) for networks whose router cannot hand out another DNS server (18): available on every Linux installation, switched on in the web UI when needed (no installation option), off by default, and holding no DHCP port while it is off.

**Non-goals (for now)**: DoQ, DNSCrypt and HTTP/3 serving to clients, TLS interception of HTTPS downloads (never), in-process NAS mounts (the service never holds `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`), per-service retention, online game-name lookups, nginx cache-disk import, cloud lookups at query time (parental protection works from local tables and downloaded lists only). The data model must not prevent adding these later.

---

## 2. Runtime overview

```
                    ┌──────────────────────── picache (one process) ─────────────────────────┐
 clients ──:53/udp,tcp · :853 DoT · DoH /dns-query──▶ dnsserver ──▶ filter ──▶ upstream (DoH/DoT/DoQ/HTTP3/DNSCrypt/UDP, cache) ──▶ Internet DNS
                    │        │  ▲ clients (identity, groups)      └─▶ router resolver (LAN names)│
                    │        └─ download cache answers (services) → cache IP                  │
 clients ──:80/http─────▶ proxy ──▶ cachestore (slices on local disk / NAS) ──▶ CDN over HTTP │
 clients ──:443/tls─────▶ sni (pass-through, allowlisted SNI only) ────────────▶ CDN :443     │
 admin ──:8080/:8443────▶ api + web UI (auth, CSRF, CSP) ──▶ all components                   │
                    │  logs.db (query log, cache log, stats) · picache.db (config) · index.db │
                    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
      root helper (optional, systemd path unit): `picache storage apply-pending` → .mount units
```

The proxy and SNI server resolve CDN hostnames through `upstream.Resolver.LookupIP`, which **bypasses** local overrides, filtering and local records. This avoids resolution loops.

At start the listeners are bound first (as root), then the components start; the servers of the DNS, DoT, DoH, web, cache and SNI listeners start serving only once the filter compiled the cached copies of the enabled lists (`filter.Engine.Ready`, at most 30 s; a WARN "the blocklists were not compiled in time" otherwise). Queries that arrive meanwhile wait in the bound sockets, so a blocklist (the parental categories included) never fails open after a restart, an update or a crash; own rules and blocked services are compiled before as ever.

### Listeners (bootstrap config, applied at the next start)

| Env | Default | Purpose | Bind failure |
|---|---|---|---|
| `PICACHE_DNS_LISTEN` | `:53` | DNS over UDP and TCP (comma-separated list) | fatal |
| `PICACHE_CACHE_LISTEN` | `:80` | download cache (HTTP) | logged, health warning, download cache DNS answers disabled |
| `PICACHE_SNI_LISTEN` | `:443` | SNI pass-through (`off` disables) | logged, health warning |
| `PICACHE_WEB_LISTEN` | `:8080` | Web UI + API over HTTP | fatal only if no web listener at all |
| `PICACHE_WEB_TLS_LISTEN` | `:8443` | Web UI + API over HTTPS (certificate: below; `off` disables); DoH at `/dns-query` (19) | as above |
| `PICACHE_DOT_LISTEN` | `:853` | DNS over TLS for clients (19); bound even while `dns.encrypted.dot` is off (connections are then closed right after accept), so the switch needs no restart | logged, `listeners.failed["dot"]`; counted by the health check `listeners` only while DoT is on |
| `PICACHE_DOH_LISTEN` | `off` | DNS over HTTPS only (`/dns-query`, 19), e.g. `:4443` (`:443` is the SNI pass-through's, bound first; the bind error names the clash) | logged, `listeners.failed["doh"]`; counted only while DoH is on |
| `PICACHE_NTP_LISTEN` | `off` | NTP server (UDP only, e.g. `:123`; answers while `ntp.enabled` is on, 6.1 "NTP"); bound even while it is off (every datagram is then dropped unanswered), so the switch needs no restart | logged, `listeners.failed["ntp"]`; counted by the health check `listeners` only while `ntp.enabled` is on |
| `PICACHE_DHCP` | unset | unset: the DHCP server (18) is switched on in the web UI, and the markers of the DHCP service decide which DHCP sockets open at start (UDP 67 and 547, the raw ICMPv6 socket with `CAP_NET_RAW`); `off` (any off spelling): opt-out, no DHCP socket ever and the server cannot be switched on; `on` (the opt-in of versions before 0.8.0, kept for upgrades): every DHCP socket opens at start and the service closes what the settings do not need; anything else refuses to start | reported in the DHCP status and the health check `dhcp`; never fatal |

**Saved listeners** (System → Network, `GET`/`PUT /system/listeners`). The roles `dns`, `cache`, `sni`, `web`, `webTls`, `dot`, `doh`, `ntp` can also be saved in the web UI; they stay bootstrap configuration and apply at the next start. Files in the data directory (0640, written through a temporary file with `O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW` and a rename): `listeners.next.json` (saved by the UI), `listeners.json` (what the running process bound from a file), `listeners.failed.json` (the last saved set that could not be bound completely); schema `{"version":1, "<role>":[addr, …], …}` (an absent role is not set; `[]` = off, only for `sni`, `webTls`, `dot`, `doh`, `ntp`, and `web` while `webTls` has an address; at most 8 addresses per role, each `ip:port` or `:port`, IPv6 in brackets, no zone; decoded strictly). Precedence per role: `serve` flags > `PICACHE_*_LISTEN` > `listeners.json` > default; a role set by a flag or the environment is locked in the UI. `config.EffectiveListeners(getenv, dataDir)` computes it for the command line tools (`healthcheck`, the update helper's health wait, re-evaluated on every attempt, the API client's base URL) and the support bundle; it reads `listeners.json` with `O_RDONLY|O_NOFOLLOW|O_NONBLOCK` and `fstat` (a regular file with one link, owned by the owner of the data directory, ≤ 16 KiB; any problem: ignored with a warning that never quotes the content) and ignores the files altogether when `PICACHE_RUN_AS` is set (Docker: root binds and runs the `HEALTHCHECK`, so root never reads what the service can write). **Start**: `listeners.next.json` if it exists, else `listeners.json`, is the candidate; each candidate role that fails to bind falls back to its environment or default value for this start (a failure of that fallback keeps the rules above: DNS fatal, web fatal only without any web listener), so a value from a file is never a reason to exit. After the data directory is prepared, `listeners.json` is written with the candidate minus the failed roles, `listeners.next.json` is removed, and when a role failed the candidate is kept as `listeners.failed.json` with the ERROR "listener <role> from the saved listeners could not be bound (<error>); using <fallback>", `listeners.failed[<role>]` and a failing health check `listeners` until a start binds everything. `PUT /system/listeners` checks the syntax, the locks, that every address is one of this machine's (loopback included), port clashes per protocol (TCP: dns, cache, sni, web, webTls, dot, doh; UDP: dns, ntp), a DNS and a cache address, a web listener on a wildcard or loopback address (the command line tools use it) and that the local address of the request's own connection stays served by `web` or `webTls`; then the current password. Docker: read-only (the compose file sets the listeners). Recovery from the host: `picache listeners --reset` (removes `listeners.json` and `listeners.next.json`).

**Web access (web ACL).** Both web listeners are wrapped (stage 1): a connection whose peer is not allowed is closed right after accept, before TLS and HTTP. Then the connection caps apply (`app.webLimiter`, shared by the HTTP and HTTPS web listeners): 64 concurrent connections per client key of the TCP peer (`netutil.ClientKey`), 256 for the IPv6 addresses of one /64 whose client keys are /128 (on-link, ULA and link-local addresses: a host cannot multiply its 64 with addresses it gives itself) and 1024 in total; `web.trustedProxies` count only toward the total (a reverse proxy carries the connections of every client); loopback counts toward the total and may use a reserve of 64 connections beyond it, so this machine's health checks (the Docker `HEALTHCHECK` with `picache healthcheck`, the health wait of an update, which rolls the update back when it fails) and the CLI still connect when hosts of the network filled the total. A browser keeps at most 6 HTTP/1.1 connections per host and port, its event streams included, or one HTTP/2 connection, so several browsers, tabs and scripts behind one address stay far below 64; the total and the reserve bound the memory of idle connections (about 45 KiB each over HTTP/2, so about 48 MiB). A host of the allowed networks can still fill the total with IPv4 aliases and keep other devices from opening the web UI while it holds the connections; it cannot lock out this machine. A connection over a cap is closed at accept (before TLS) with the WARN "web connection limit reached" at most once a minute. The web servers read the headers within 5 s (which also bounds the TLS handshake), a request within 60 s and write a response within 120 s (event streams and the long operations of 12 lift these per request) and close idle connections after 120 s, like the cache listener; DoH on the web listeners is bounded per request (19), and every web server allows at most 32 concurrent HTTP/2 streams per connection. The API middleware checks the effective client of every request again (stage 2, 12), so a keep-alive connection from an address that was just removed is refused from its next request. Allowed are always loopback and this machine's own addresses (`netutil.LocalAddrs`, re-read on every tick); while `web.restrictToNetworks` is on (the default of new installations; settings v5 turns it off for documents of earlier versions) also `netutil.PrivateLANPrefixes`, `netutil.ConnectedSubnets` (public on-link prefixes included), `dns.allowedNetworks`, `web.allowedNetworks` (≤ 64, at least /8 or /32) and `web.trustedProxies`; with it off everyone. `dns.allowAllNetworks` and `dns.trustConnectedNetworks` do not change it. The set (`netutil.WebACL`, held by `netutil.WebAccess`) is immutable and swapped atomically on every settings change and on every **tick** (the app's 60 s maintenance loop, also run at once on SIGHUP: web ACL, web access reset marker, certificate checks). Both stages count into one counter (`webRefused` of `GET /system/info`) and log at WARN at most once per address per 10 minutes (256 addresses tracked). Recovery from the host: `picache web-access --reset` (4, 6.1).

**Trusted reverse proxies.** `web.trustedProxies` (≤ 16 addresses or CIDRs of at least /24 or /64) are the only peers whose `X-Forwarded-For` and `X-Forwarded-Proto` are read (`Forwarded`, `X-Real-IP` and the like never are). `netutil.ForwardedClient`: the header lines joined with `,`, entries trimmed, empty ones skipped, at most the 16 right-most inspected; from the right, trusted entries are skipped and the first other entry is the effective client (`ip`, `ip:port`, `[ipv6]`, `[ipv6]:port`, zone removed, canonical); any other entry reached before the client fails the request with 400 (fail closed); all trusted: the left-most inspected entry; no header: the peer. The effective scheme is https over TLS, or for a trusted peer whose right-most `X-Forwarded-Proto` value is `https`.

**HTTPS certificate** (`internal/app` `webTLS`, `api.WebTLS`): the certificate of every TLS listener — the web UI over HTTPS, DoT and the dedicated DoH listeners (19) share it (`GetCertificate` whatever the SNI; ALPN `h2`/`http/1.1` for the web UI and DoH, `dot` for DoT, so a DoT client that offers ALPN without `dot` fails the handshake). Sources in this order: `files` (`PICACHE_WEB_TLS_CERT`/`PICACHE_WEB_TLS_KEY`), `uploaded` (`<data>/tls/uploaded.pem`, `PUT /system/tls`), `local-ca` (`<data>/tls/cert.pem` and `key.pem` issued by PiCache's local CA), `self-signed` (the certificate of 0.10 or older until it is due for renewal, or an in-memory certificate when nothing else could be loaded or written). A configured source that is unusable serves its last good certificate (after a failed reload) or else the local CA's (created when needed); `GET /system/tls` then reports `fallback`/`error` and the health check `tls` fails. No TLS listener is disabled for a certificate problem (encrypted DNS counts as not serving while the certificate is a fallback or expired, 19). `GetCertificate` returns an atomically swapped certificate (leaf and intermediates); `GetConfigForClient` picks one of two fixed configurations by `web.tlsMinVersion` (`1.2` or `1.3`), so a change applies to the next handshake. On every tick the certificate files are read (at most 1 MiB each) and loaded when their SHA-256 differs from the last good pair (a failure keeps the current certificate, is logged once per content and retried; a deploy hook that writes key and certificate in two steps is picked up on the next tick); the local CA's leaf is re-issued 30 days before it expires or, at most hourly, when the wanted SANs changed (a change of `dns.encrypted` and an address of this machine the CA covers but the leaf lacks, e.g. the network came up after the start, at the next tick, without the hourly limit; while the machine has no address besides loopback outside a bridge network, the leaf keeps its addresses instead of dropping them); a certificate that is not valid yet (issued while the clock was far ahead) is re-issued at the next tick, with a new CA when the CA is not valid yet either (the health check `tls` warns "… is not valid until …" meanwhile); a legacy self-signed certificate is replaced by a leaf of the local CA 30 days before it expires. Uploads, deletes and a new local CA through the API take effect at once. The local CA: ECDSA P-256, 10 years, path length 0, key usage CertSign and CRLSign only, subject `PiCache local CA <host name> <instance>`, **critical name constraints**: PiCache's own names (`localhost`, `picache`, the host name (not in a Docker or Podman container whose host name is its default, 12 hex digits of the container ID: a recreated container gets a new one), `dns.serverNames`, single-label ones also with the local and search domains, and `dns.encrypted.serverName`, whose constraint permits every name below it (the DoT ClientIDs) unless it equals or is a parent of the local domain, a search domain or an entry of `web.allowedHosts`/`PICACHE_WEB_HOSTS`: then it is left out and reported not covered; never the local domain, a search domain or a parent of one, never `web.allowedHosts`) and its own stable addresses (loopback and the addresses of up, non-virtual interfaces without link-local, temporary, deprecated, tentative and public IPv6 addresses; the container's own addresses are left out in a bridge network), each as /32 or /128; a family without an address is excluded as a whole. The leaf (a new P-256 key each time, 825 days but never beyond the CA, CN the host name) carries the own names and addresses within the constraints, and `*.<serverName>` while `dns.encrypted.dot` is on (`hostsCovered`/`hostsNotCovered` list it, checked with `VerifyHostname` on a probe label); names and addresses outside them (a new server name, renumbering) are reported (`localCa.renewalNeeded`, health warning) until a new CA is created (`POST /system/tls/local-ca`; devices must trust it again). The CA key stays in the data directory (6.1). Nothing is created or loaded while no TLS listener (`web-tls`, `dot`, `doh`) is bound; `api.TLSStatus.listener` reports that one is (so a DoT listener alone creates the local CA at the first start).

**Bootstrap switches.** `PICACHE_CONFIG_LOCKED` (default off): configuration changes by interactive sessions are refused with `config_locked` (12); admin API tokens still write; for infrastructure as code, not an access control against admins. `PICACHE_DESTRUCTIVE_API` (default on): off refuses the destructive routes (restore, DHCP reset and lease wipe, purges, storage init and delete, deleting scheduled backups, accounts and the uploaded certificate) for every principal. An invalid value of either refuses to start. `PICACHE_LOG_FILE` (a `<name>.log` file below `/var/log/picache`, directly in the data directory or below its `logs/`) and `PICACHE_LOG_SYSLOG` (`udp://` or `tcp://host:port`) add sinks to the application log (6.1 "Log sinks"); `PICACHE_PPROF=on` serves the Go profiles on `/debug/pprof/` to admins on this machine (6.1 "pprof"); `PICACHE_INITIAL_CONFIG` is a settings document applied once on the first start (no account, app_meta without `config.initial_applied`: strict decoding on top of the current document, `settings.Store.Update` with the normal validation, before any listener serves and before `PICACHE_ADMIN_PASSWORD_FILE` is provisioned; recorded as `{sha256, appliedAt}`; ignored with a WARN on a later start; `cache.activeStoreId` in it is ignored; an unreadable, oversized or invalid file refuses to start with its field). An invalid value of any of them refuses to start.

All listeners and the DHCP sockets the markers ask for are bound **before** privileges are dropped (section 6.2; the DHCP service opens UDP 67 and 547 later itself when DHCP is switched on while it runs, 18.1). TCP listeners for DNS, DoT, the dedicated DoH listeners, :80 and :443 are wrapped with `netutil.LimitListener`: connections from clients outside the ACL are closed at accept (for DoT and DoH before TLS, so scanners outside get no certificate); concurrent connections are capped per client key (`netutil.ClientKey`: /32 for IPv4; /128 for loopback, link-local, ULA and on-link IPv6; /64 for other IPv6) and in total (DNS, DoT and DoH 32/1024, cache and SNI 256/4096; the web listeners 64/1024 with their own limiter, above). DNS over TCP, DoT and the dedicated DoH listeners use `netutil.LimitDNSListener`: at the total, a newcomer whose client key would then hold fewer connections than the key holding the most is admitted and the oldest connection of that key (of several such keys, the oldest connection) is closed; a newcomer that would hold as many is refused; loopback may use 64 connections beyond the total and is never closed for a newcomer. One host with many source addresses (up to 32 per key) could otherwise hold all 1024 connections with a query every few seconds and keep every other client, this machine's resolver included, from TCP DNS (answers truncated over UDP failed).

---

## 3. Persistent data

Everything persistent lives in exactly two places plus optional NAS mounts.

| Path (bare metal / LXC) | Docker | Contents | Backup? |
|---|---|---|---|
| `/var/lib/picache` (`PICACHE_DATA_DIR`) | `/data` | `picache.db` (configuration: settings, users, lists, rules, clients, groups, parental controls, local records, DHCP static leases and leases, services, storage targets with sealed NAS passwords, notification channels with sealed secrets, the sealed sync token and proxy password (`settings_secrets`), audit log); `logs.db` (query log, cache events, sessions, statistics, evictions, seen clients, warning history; `logs.db.cleared` while the rows of a cleared query log are deleted, `logs.db.damaged` after SQLite reported damage, 11); `cache-index/<store-id>.db`; `lists/`; `cache-domains/`; `tls/` (0750: `ca.crt` 0644 and `ca.key` 0600 (PKCS#8) of the local CA, `cert.pem` 0644 and `key.pem` 0600 of its leaf or of the legacy self-signed certificate, kept current so a rolled-back 0.10 serves the same certificate, `uploaded.pem` 0600: the uploaded key first, then its chain, re-encoded; every write goes through a temporary file created with `O_EXCL`/`O_NOFOLLOW`, synced and renamed, a key before its certificate; a symbolic link in place of the directory is refused); `web-access.reset` (the empty marker of `picache web-access --reset` until the service applied it); `listeners.json`, `listeners.next.json`, `listeners.failed.json` (the listeners saved in the web UI, 2); `picache.db.restore` (a staged restore until the next start, 15.3); `keys/master.key` (0600; created when missing: when the configuration then holds secrets sealed with the previous key (settings secrets, notification channel secrets, NAS passwords), an ERROR is logged and app_meta `secrets.key_replaced_at` makes the health check `master-key` warn while stored secrets cannot be decrypted with the new key: each evaluation opens every sealed secret, so an edit that keeps a secret (a renamed notification channel) does not end the warning, only entering or deleting it does); `instance-id` (written at the first start; while it exists, a missing or empty `picache.db` stops the start instead of becoming a new installation: `checkExistingInstallation`, deleting `instance-id` starts one on purpose); `setup-token` (until setup is done); `backups/` (automatic pre-upgrade copies, newest 3; a failed update puts back the one made during its run); `backups/scheduled/` (scheduled backups, 15.2); `storage-requests/` (mount requests for the root helper); `update-requests/` (update request and `status.json` of the root update helper, 14.4); `dhcp.sockets`, `dhcp.ra` (empty markers of the DHCP service: which DHCP sockets open at the next start, 18.1); `picache.db.before-restore` (after a restore) | `picache.db` (UI download, scheduled backups (15.2) or file copy while stopped); everything else is rebuildable. `keys/master.key` separately if stored NAS passwords and notification secrets should survive a move to another machine. |
| `/var/cache/picache` (`PICACHE_CACHE_DIR`) | `/cache` | The built-in **local** cache store (slice files). Large. | No |
| `/srv/picache/<id>` (`PICACHE_MOUNT_ROOT`) | `/srv/picache` (bind, `rslave`) | NAS cache stores. The only place outside the cache dir where stores may live (the only NAS path writable inside the sandbox). | No |
| `/etc/picache/picache.env` | environment | Bootstrap settings. Read by systemd **and by every CLI command** (for both, the last line of a key listed twice wins; for the CLI a variable set in the environment wins over the file). No longer the only source of the listeners: the web UI can save them in the data directory (2); the environment wins. | Yes |
| `/etc/picache/credentials/<id>.cred` | – | NAS credentials written by the root helper (0600, root). | – |
| `/etc/picache/nightly.enabled` | – | Root-owned marker of `install.sh --nightly`: the update helper may install nightly builds (14.4). | – |
| `/var/log/picache/` (`LogsDirectory=picache`, 0750) | `/data/<name>.log` or below `/data/logs` | `PICACHE_LOG_FILE` (off by default), rotated at 10 MiB with 5 gzip generations. | No |

Rules:
- SQLite databases MUST live on local disk. PiCache refuses to open a DB on NFS/CIFS.
- Only slice files may live on a NAS.
- A broken `logs.db` is moved aside (`logs.db.broken-<ts>`, with `logs.db-wal.broken-<ts>` and `logs.db-shm.broken-<ts>`) and recreated; only the newest broken copy is kept (the older ones are deleted first: each can be as large as `logs.maxDbSizeMiB`); if that fails, logging is disabled but DNS keeps running. A `logs.db` (or its `-wal`/`-shm`) this user may not write, or on a read-only file system, is never moved aside: it is intact, so logging is disabled with the cause and the owner (`writableHint`, as for `picache.db`). SQLite's SQLITE_CORRUPT or SQLITE_NOTADB on a write batch, pruning or a read of the query log or the statistics marks the store damaged (`logs.Metrics.damaged`: the health check `logs` warns; the first such error makes the app evaluate the health checks at once, `logs.Store.OnDamage`) and writes `logs.db.damaged`, and such a read answers 503 (5); the next start runs `PRAGMA quick_check` on a `logs.db` with that marker (at most 5 minutes) and moves it aside unless it passes, then removes the marker. The health check separates events dropped under load from events of batches that could not be written (`logs.Metrics.writeFailed`). A broken cache index is moved aside and rebuilt from the self-describing slice headers.
- UI backups and scheduled backups never contain accounts (users with password hashes and TOTP secrets, sessions, API tokens); sealed NAS passwords and notification secrets only on explicit opt-in. A restore (browser session + current password) replaces the configuration, never the accounts: on the next start the running instance's users, API tokens and audit log are copied into the restored database and all sessions end. Uploads with triggers, views, virtual tables, generated columns, tables or indexes the live database does not have, indexes defined differently, or altered account tables are refused (at upload and again at start). PiCache creates no triggers or views; any found in `picache.db` are dropped at start (WARN), by `picache reset-password` and in every backup copy.
- Schema changes are append-only component migrations (5), run at start after the pre-upgrade copy (14.4); a restored backup of an older version is migrated at the start that applies it. v0.2.0 renamed the download cache identifiers: settings v2 moves the old download cache section to `downloadCache` (a `downloadCache` section that is already there wins), clients v2 renames the bypass column of `client_clients` to `download_cache_bypass`, and logs v2 renames the matching rollup column of `logs_dns_minute`/`logs_dns_hourly` to `override` and rewrites the status of logged queries to `override` (the old names are in the migration code only). Backups made by v0.2.0 are refused by v0.1.x (newer schema); audit entries keep the action they were written with.
- v0.9.0: settings v4 changes a stored `dns.upstreams` that equals the old default (`["https://dns.quad9.net/dns-query","https://cloudflare-dns.com/dns-query"]`, compared with `json()`) to `["https://dns.quad9.net/dns-query"]`, and gives a document without `dns.fallbackUpstreams` whose `dns.upstreams` differs from that list `fallbackUpstreams: []` (an admin's own resolver never starts sending queries to another operator; only installations on the defaults get the default fallback); documents that are not valid JSON are left alone. dns v2 creates `dns_forwarder_domains(forwarder_id REFERENCES dns_forwarders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE, position, domain UNIQUE, PRIMARY KEY (forwarder_id, position))` with every domain of every forwarder (position 0 = `dns_forwarders.domain`) and back-fills it from `dns_forwarders`, whose table and indexes stay unchanged. logs v3 adds `upstream_ede_code INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT -1` (−1 = no EDE), `upstream_ede_text` and `ecs` to `logs_queries` (no table rewrite). A version before 0.9.0 refuses the migrated `picache.db` (newer schema): downgrading needs the pre-upgrade copy (14.4), and an older version sets the newer `logs.db` aside (query log and statistics start fresh).
- v0.11.0: settings v5 sets `web.restrictToNetworks` to `false` in every stored document that lacks it (a missing or `null` `web` object becomes one; documents that are not valid JSON are left alone), so an upgraded or restored older configuration keeps the web UI open to every address; new installations get `true` from `Defaults()`; the other new members (`allowedNetworks: []`, `trustedProxies: []`, `tlsMinVersion: "1.2"`) decode from the defaults. auth v2 adds `role TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'viewer' CHECK (role IN ('admin', 'viewer'))` to `auth_users` and makes every existing account an admin (an insert that forgets the role creates a viewer; PiCache always inserts the role). `CarryOverAccounts` copies columns by name, so a restore keeps the roles of the running instance (the accounts of a live database from before roles stay admins), and `CheckBackupSchema` accepts auth v1 and v2 backups. A version before 0.11.0 refuses the migrated `picache.db` (newer schema) and does not start: downgrading needs the pre-upgrade copy (14.4). `picache reset-password` migrates the auth schema too, so when it runs before the new version's first start it makes the same copy first and records the new version (`app.PreUpgradeBackup`).
- v0.12.0: logs v4 adds `upstream_answer TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` to `logs_queries` (no table rewrite), the warning history `logs_events` (15.1) and the daily top tables `logs_dns_top_daily` and `logs_cache_top_daily` (the columns and primary keys of the hourly tables, `WITHOUT ROWID`, 11); the top kinds `qtype` and `unique` are rows of new kinds in the hourly tables, which 0.11 ignores. clients v3 adds `ignore_stats INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` to `client_clients` and sets it to `ignore_logs` (the meaning of the single flag before 0.12.0). Settings keep their version: the new members (`logs.hideDomains`, `logs.statsEnabled`, `logs.ignoredDomains`, `logs.statsOnlyAddressQueries`, `logs.flushSeconds`, the derived `logs.privacyLevel`) and the section `health` decode from `Defaults()`, so every 0.11 combination of the query log and anonymisation switches keeps its behaviour ((on, off) is `full`, the others `custom`); 0.11 ignores the new members and section, a save by 0.11 drops them and a re-upgrade restores their defaults. A version before 0.12.0 refuses the migrated `picache.db` (clients v3): downgrading needs the pre-upgrade copy (14.4). 0.11 sets the newer `logs.db` aside as `logs.db.broken-<ts>` (query log, statistics and the warning history start fresh); after upgrading again the admin can stop PiCache and move that file (with `-wal`/`-shm`) back by hand. Backups made by 0.12 are refused by 0.11 (newer schema); 0.11 backups are accepted and migrated at the start that applies them (`ignore_stats` back-filled).
- v0.13.0: filter v3 adds `format` (`'domains'`) and `name_auto` (0) to `filter_lists`, the rule modifiers `qtypes` (`'[]'`, a JSON array), `qtypes_negate`, `reply`, `reply_ipv4`, `reply_ipv6`, `denyallow` (`'[]'`) and `invert` to `filter_rules`, and the tables `filter_ip_rules` (`UNIQUE (action, pattern)`) and `filter_ip_rule_groups` (7.2 "IP rules"); dns v3 adds `scope` (`'all'`) and `other_family` (`'nodata'`) to `dns_records` and the table `dns_record_groups`; clients v4 adds `upstreams` (`'[]'`), `upstream_preset` (`''`) and `device_client_id` (NULL, `REFERENCES client_clients(id) ON DELETE SET NULL`) to `client_groups`. The three steps only add columns, tables and indexes: `db.Migrate` runs each step in a transaction on connections with foreign keys on, so a rebuilt `filter_rules`, `filter_lists` or `client_groups` would delete their group links through `ON DELETE CASCADE`, and a rebuilt `filter_lists` could reset its AUTOINCREMENT sequence (the matcher and the cached copies refer to list IDs). Every rule, list and record keeps its group links, the list IDs and the `sqlite_sequence` row of `filter_lists` stay, every record keeps serving everyone (scope `all`) and every group uses the default upstreams. Settings keep their version: `dns.localRecordsEnabled` (`true`), `dns.localizeRecords` (`first`, which reorders multi-address records, 7.1 step 7) and `dns.serverNameAddresses` (empty) decode from `Defaults()`. `logs.db` has no migration: `blocked-ip` is a new status string, counted as blocked by the `blocked-` prefix (0.12 shows such rows with the raw status). Backups made by 0.12 are accepted and migrated at the start that applies them. A version before 0.13.0 refuses the migrated `picache.db` (clients v4, filter v3, dns v3) and does not start: downgrading needs the pre-upgrade copy (14.4); `logs.db` stays readable.
- v0.14.0: logs v5 adds `dns_client_id TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` to `logs_queries` (one append-only step, no table rewrite). `picache.db` has no schema step: ClientIDs are rows of `client_identifiers` with the kind `clientid` and the value `clientid:<ClientID>`, and settings keep their version: `dns.plainDns` (`true`) and `dns.encrypted` (`{dot:false, doh:false, serverName:""}`) decode from `Defaults()`; 0.13 ignores them, a save by 0.13 drops them and a re-upgrade restores the defaults. The ClientIDs of the seen data stay in memory (`clients_seen` is unchanged). Visible after the upgrade: `:853` is bound (closed at accept while DoT is off; a clash is reported only while DoT is on), and an installation with `PICACHE_WEB_TLS_LISTEN=off` gets the local CA at the first start (the DoT listener is a TLS listener). A downgrade to 0.13 needs no pre-upgrade copy: 0.13 sets the newer `logs.db` aside as `logs.db.broken-<ts>` (it can be moved back by hand after upgrading again, while PiCache is stopped); plain DNS is on and DoT/DoH are gone; 0.13 skips `clientid:` identifiers (a client with only ClientIDs never matches) and refuses to save such a client until they are removed; `clientid:` entries of `dns.blockedClients` and upstreams with `quic://`, `h3://` or `sdns://` make the stored settings invalid for 0.13 (Open keeps them with a warning, every settings save is refused until they are removed) and 0.13 skips those entries, so a default set, forwarder or group set with only such upstreams has none (SERVFAIL; group sets fail closed). Backups made by 0.14 are accepted by 0.13 with the same effects.
- v0.15.0: auth v3 rebuilds `auth_tokens` for the scope `sync` (copy, drop, rename, indexes re-created; the result equals a fresh v3 schema; nothing references `auth_tokens`, so nothing cascades; `CheckBackupSchema` and `CarryOverAccounts` accept the `auth_tokens` of auth v1–v3); settings v6 creates `settings_secrets(name TEXT PRIMARY KEY, sealed BLOB NOT NULL, bound TEXT NOT NULL, updated_at INTEGER NOT NULL)` and sets `updates.channel` in stored documents without it (`includePrereleases` true → `beta`, else `stable`; a missing or `null` `updates` becomes `{"channel":"stable"}`). The identifier kinds `iface` and `host` are rows of `client_identifiers` (no step). New members and sections decode from `Defaults()`: `clients`, `sync`, `network`, `ntp`, `logs.seenRetentionDays`. `logs.db` has no step (interfaces are computed when read; the WHOIS cache is memory only). A version before 0.15.0 refuses the migrated `picache.db` (auth v3, settings v6) and does not start: downgrading needs the pre-upgrade copy (14.4), which the automatic rollback uses, so `iface:`/`host:` clients, sync tokens and settings secrets never reach 0.14. 0.14 ignores `listeners*.json` and binds the environment and default listeners (when another service took such a port meanwhile, 0.14 exits on the DNS bind: free the port or set `PICACHE_DNS_LISTEN`). Backups of 0.14 are accepted and migrated at the start that applies them (channel derived, no secrets); backups of 0.15 are refused by 0.14. New files: `listeners.json`, `listeners.next.json`, `listeners.failed.json`; app_meta keys `config.initial_applied` and `sync.state` (`restore.sections` and `restore.by` only inside staged files). Units: `picache.service` gets `LogsDirectory=picache`, `LogsDirectoryMode=0750` and `SystemCallFilter=adjtimex` and loses `ProtectClock=yes` (6.2); the 0.15 binary starts with the 0.14 units (a log file below `/var/log/picache` then cannot be opened, stderr continues and `logging` warns; the clock state is unknown, NTP answers unsynchronised and `ntp` warns) until the one-line installer or the updater's unit step installs the new units.
- v0.16.0: no schema step (`picache.db` and `logs.db` keep 0.15.0's versions, so 0.15 opens them). `web.onboardingDone` decodes from `Defaults()` as `true` (upgraded installations and restored older backups never show the getting-started checklist; a fresh installation stores `false`, 13); 0.15 ignores it and drops it when it saves the settings (after upgrading again it reads `true`). Units: only comments change, the 0.16 binary runs with the 0.15 units. armv7 installations stay on armv7 (the updater reads GOARM from the build, 14.3); the Debian package is new (14.1).
- v0.17.0 (local DNSSEC validation, 7.6): settings v7 sets `dns.dnssecMode` in every valid stored document whose `dns` is an object without it, from `json_type(doc, '$.dns.dnssec')` (`true` → `passthrough`, anything else → `off`; documents that are not valid JSON are left alone, like v2–v6), so an upgrade never switches validation on; `DecodeStored` mirrors it (15.3). dns v4 adds `validate INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0` to `dns_forwarders` (no forwarder validates after the upgrade). logs v6 adds `dnssec_status TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` to `logs_queries` (older rows read as not validated); the top kind `dnssec` needs no step (11). Every step only adds a column or sets a missing member. A version before 0.17.0 refuses the migrated `picache.db` (settings v7, dns v4) and does not start: downgrading needs the pre-upgrade copy (14.4; the update helper's rollback uses it, Docker users restore it before starting the older image, `.deb` users stop PiCache, restore the copy, then install the older package with `PICACHE_ALLOW_DOWNGRADE=1`); the DNSSEC mode and the forwarder flags set since the upgrade are lost. 0.16 sets the newer `logs.db` aside as `logs.db.broken-<ts>` (it can be moved back by hand after upgrading again). Every document 0.17 writes keeps `dns.dnssec` with 0.16's meaning (`dnssecMode != "off"`). Backups of 0.16 and earlier are accepted and migrated at the start that applies them (a partial `settings` restore uses the `DecodeStored` mirror); 0.16 refuses 0.17 backups. Followers are upgraded first: a 0.16 follower refuses a 0.17 primary's export (newer schema); a 0.17 follower of a 0.16 primary maps `dns.dnssec` through the alias (15.4). Units, `install.sh` and the packages are unchanged.
- v0.10.0: filter v2 adds `category` and `catalog_key` to `filter_lists` (`TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT ''`); at every start the lists with an empty category get the category and key of the catalogue entry with exactly the same URL, else `other` (`allow` for allowlists, `abused-tlds` for an own blocklist whose cached copy blocks mostly whole TLDs, which the TLD guard of 7.2 would otherwise drop; idempotent, only rows with an empty category), so an existing HaGeZi DoH/VPN/TOR/Proxy Bypass list becomes a protection list (7.2). parental v2 adds `pause_until INTEGER NULL` to `parental_groups` (16). `logs.db` has no migration: the statistics purposes are rows of a new kind in `logs_dns_top_hourly` (11), which 0.9.0 ignores. A version before 0.10.0 refuses the migrated `picache.db`: downgrading needs the pre-upgrade copy (14.4); `logs.db` stays readable.

---

## 4. Package layout and dependency rules

```
cmd/picache/                 CLI: serve (default), version, healthcheck, reset-password [--admin], users, web-access --reset, listeners --reset, setup-token, storage apply|apply-pending, update [--check] [--channel] | apply-pending, restore, db check|salvage; API client: logs tail|export, status [--watch], pause, resume, explain, lists update, allow, deny, query, config get|set|apply
internal/version/            build info (ldflags)
internal/config/             bootstrap config from env/flags, derived paths
internal/db/                 SQLite (modernc) writer/reader pools, per-component migrations, read-only open, schema registry
internal/apperr/             typed user-facing errors (NotFound, Invalid, Conflict, …)
internal/listing/            generic page type
internal/settings/           typed runtime settings (one JSON document in picache.db), validation, pub/sub, query-type names (QTypeName/ParseQType), the family resolver presets of client groups (UpstreamPresets), the upstream syntax and the DNS stamp parser (stamp.go), ClientIDs
internal/secrets/            master key + AEAD seal/open for stored secrets
internal/oui/                MAC vendor lookup (the IEEE MA-L/MA-M/MA-S registries embedded as oui.bin, binary search over the bytes; `make oui` refreshes it)
internal/netutil/            ACL, web ACL and forwarded client (trusted proxies), IP classification (incl. rebind targets), SSRF-safe dialer, rate limiter (RateKey), client lists (IP/CIDR/MAC), LimitListener, host normalisation, gateway (IPv4/IPv6)/resolv.conf detection, interface addresses with state and lifetime, route snapshot (the interface of an address, `iface:`), tunnels through the outbound proxy (HTTP CONNECT, SOCKS5 to IP literals)
internal/auth/               users and roles, argon2id, sessions, API tokens, TOTP, login throttling, setup token, audit log
internal/clients/            clients, groups (incl. their upstreams), identity resolution (IP/CIDR/MAC; ClientIDs of encrypted DNS), kernel neighbour table (netlink, IPv4 + IPv6; /proc/net/arp fallback), client names, device groups ("Only for this device"), batch changes, the ClientIDs seen since the start (memory only), identification by interface (`iface:`) and host name (`host:`), name sources (DHCP lease, PTR, hosts file), WHOIS (RDAP) annotations, forgetting seen data, the follower sync of groups and clients
internal/dns/upstream/       upstream transports (UDP/TCP/DoT/DoH, DoQ and DoH over HTTP/3 with quic-go as a client only, DNSCrypt; DNS stamps with certificate hashes), modes (incl. fastest address), fallbacks, group sets (upstreams per client group), response cache, serve-stale, classification of answers blocked by the upstream, client subnet, bypass LookupIP, clock guard, DNSSEC modes and validation (the fetch's verdict in the response cache, chain lookups and their flights, the global chain rate limit, the DNSSEC probes, the injected clock state, validating forwarder sets, the DNSSEC test)
internal/dns/dnssec/         DNSSEC validator (7.6): built-in root trust anchors, algorithm policy, RRset, key and denial (NSEC/NSEC3) checks, the zone-cut walk over a lookup function it is given, key and failure caches, budgets; imports no PiCache package (`dnssec/dnssectest`: signed test zones for the tests of `dnssec` and `upstream`, imported by tests only)
internal/dns/filter/         blocklists (fetch, parse, compile, titles), list catalogue and categories, category switches, custom rules (query types, replies, denyallow, inverted expressions; import and export), lists of answer addresses and IP rules, matcher, protection lists, explain, search, batch changes
internal/dns/parental/       parental controls: service catalogue, schedules, overrides, safe search, group pauses, hot-path check
internal/ntp/                NTP server (the server side of SNTP on PICACHE_NTP_LISTEN; the host clock's state via adjtimex, read only)
internal/dns/server/         DNS listeners (UDP, TCP, DoT) + request pipeline, DoH (ServeDoH), plain-DNS switch, DDR, local records (typed, per group, hosts import), conditional forwarders (incl. import), router resolver, pause, blocked clients, rebinding protection, response-IP check
internal/dlcache/services/   cache-domains source, service registry & matcher, custom services, content grouping, labels
internal/dlcache/store/      slice store + index DB + eviction + verify/rebuild + store marker   (package cachestore)
internal/dlcache/proxy/      HTTP cache proxy (:80)
internal/dlcache/sni/        TLS SNI pass-through (:443)
internal/storage/            storage targets, capability detection, mount guard, store init/adopt, host-apply root helper, snippets, speed test
internal/update/             releases: check (GitHub API), SemVer, signature check (compiled-in keys), install + rollback, update requests of the root helper, release asset names per architecture (AssetName, BuildGOARM, DebianArch), package mode (the Debian package's marker)
internal/notify/             notification channels (webhook, ntfy, Gotify), sealed secrets, per-channel queues with retries and rate limit, delivery log
internal/dhcp/               optional DHCP server: sockets, safety gates, probe, DHCPv4 (packets, allocation, leases), lease names, router advertisement and DHCPv6 loops
internal/dhcp/ra/            router advertisements (RDNSS/DNSSL only): encoding, solicitation check, timing
internal/dhcp/dhcpv6/        stateless DHCPv6: information requests and replies
internal/logs/               logs.db: query log, cache events, sessions, rollups (hourly and daily top lists, unique-name sketches), evictions, live subscriptions, clearing, export reads, warning history
internal/applog/             application log: slog handler around the stderr handler, redaction for both sinks, ring buffer (2000 records) with privacy masking, runtime debug level, streams, log file and syslog sinks
internal/hostinfo/           host resources (load, uptime, memory, cgroup v2 memory, temperatures, model, disks; /proc and /sys below an injectable root), network interfaces (sysfs) and the health check host
internal/api/                REST API + SSE, middleware, one routes_<domain>.go file per domain, openapi.json (the OpenAPI 3.1 description, embedded, GET /openapi.json)
internal/webui/              go:embed of the built frontend (internal/webui/dist)
internal/app/                wiring, lifecycle, privilege drop, store switching, health, backup/restore, scheduled backups, notification events and the warning history (emit), network check, certificate of the TLS listeners (sources, local CA, reload), the DoT and DoH listeners and the state of encrypted DNS, maintenance tick (web ACL, web access reset), host sampling, database sizes, support bundle, picache.db schema of every component (MigrateConfigDB), saved listeners, partial restores, PICACHE_INITIAL_CONFIG, the follower sync, the outbound proxy's tunnels, the NTP server's wiring
web/                         Svelte 5 + Vite SPA (builds into internal/webui/dist)
deploy/                      docker/ (compose files: host, bridge, macvlan), systemd/, lxc/, debian/ (the package's control template and maintainer script fragments), unraid/, truenas/, synology/ (NAS templates), install.sh
docs/                        this file, API.md, DESIGN.md, DEPLOYMENT.md, SECURITY.md, ROUTERS.md, GUIDES.md, DEVICES.md, TRANSLATING.md
```

Dependency rules:
- Foundation packages (`version`, `config`, `db`, `apperr`, `listing`, `settings`, `secrets`, `netutil`, `oui`) import only each other (`settings` → `db`, `apperr`; `netutil` → `settings`; `oui` imports nothing). `clients` and `dhcp` use `oui` for the vendors of MAC addresses.
- Domain packages import foundation packages and each other only along these edges: `dnsserver` → {`upstream`, `filter`, `parental`, `clients`, `logs`} (types only; collaborators are consumer-side interfaces); `parental` → {`clients`} (the group type; group names through a consumer-side interface); `proxy` → {`cachestore`, `clients`, `logs`, `services` (pure functions GroupFor/IsBypassPath/constants only)}; `sni` → {`clients`, `logs`}; `storage` → {`cachestore`} (store marker; slice sampling and page-cache dropping for the speed test); `upstream` → {`dnssec`} (the validator; `dnssec` imports no PiCache package and nothing but `upstream` imports it). `filter`, `services`, `upstream` (apart from `dnssec`), `clients`, `logs`, `auth`, `cachestore`, `update`, `notify`, `dhcp`, `ntp` import no other domain package (`ntp` gets the DNS ACL, the limiter settings and the clock reader from `app`; `upstream` gets the same clock reader through `SetClockReader` for the DNSSEC time checks) (`update` imports only `version`; `dhcp` only its own `dhcp/ra` and `dhcp/dhcpv6`, which import nothing of PiCache). The DNS server reads lease names through a consumer-side interface (`dnsserver.LeaseNames`), the clients registry through a function (`SetLeaseNames`), and `dhcp` reads the neighbour table through a function in its `Deps`; `app` wires them. The CLI never writes `picache.db` for `web-access --reset` (the running service keeps the settings in memory, and its next save would undo such a write): it creates the marker `<data>/web-access.reset` (`O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW`, 0600; as root after switching to the owner of the data directory), which the service applies with `lstat` (a regular file only) at start before the web listeners serve, on every tick and on SIGHUP: through `settings.Store.Recover` (an internal recovery update: the configuration lock does not apply, and stored settings that are invalid in another member, which Open keeps, do not refuse it) `web.restrictToNetworks=false`, `web.trustedProxies=[]`, `web.tlsMinVersion="1.2"`, the uploaded certificate deleted, the audit row `web.access_reset` (username `cli`), WARN "web access was reset from the host", the marker deleted (one that cannot be deleted is applied once per process start, and again after it was removed by hand). `cmd/picache` subscribes to SIGHUP before `app.Run` binds anything (Go's default would end the process); SIGHUP never stops PiCache. Events reach `notify` from `app`: `auth` reports lockouts through a callback (`OnLockout`), health, store, update and backup events are raised by `app` itself.
- `applog` and `hostinfo` import no PiCache package. The redacted attribute names of `applog` equal the audit log's (`auth.redactedNames`, kept equal by a test). `cmd/picache` builds the logger (`applog.New` around the stderr handler, created at debug level); `app` finds the application log through the logger's handler and wires `logs.anonymizeClientIps` and `logs.hideDomains` into it as functions.
- The CLI commands that use the API (`logs tail`, `logs export`, `status`, `pause`, `resume`, `explain`, `lists update`, `allow`, `deny`, `query`, `config`; `cmd/picache/apiclient.go` with `get`, `post`, `put`, `patch`): base URL `--url`, else `PICACHE_URL`, else the effective local web listener (`config.EffectiveListeners` like `healthcheck`: the first `web` address on all addresses or loopback, else the first such `webTls` address, reached on loopback; only without one the first `web`, else `webTls`, address as it is); the token from `PICACHE_TOKEN` of the process environment or `--token-file` (a regular file opened without following a link, at most 4 KiB, trimmed; a warning when group or others can read it), never from `picache.env` (the line is skipped with a warning). The token is sent only to a loopback `http` URL or to an `https` URL verified against the system roots plus `<data>/tls/ca.crt` when it is readable (a loopback `https` URL without a readable CA is not verified, like `healthcheck`); anything else exits 1 with "refusing to send the API token to <url>". No proxy, no redirects. A read token is enough for the read commands (`logs`, `status`, `explain`, `query`, `config get`); the others need an admin token (a 403 prints "this command needs an admin token", a 401 "the API token is missing or invalid (PICACHE_TOKEN or --token-file)"). Every string from the API is printed through `escapeControls`; `--json` prints the answer as it is, with the C1 and bidi controls escaped too. `config set|apply` read JSON from a file (regular, not a link, ≤ 1 MiB) or stdin, never from an argument. `picache restore` and `picache listeners --reset` work on the data directory directly (as root after switching to its owner), never through the API. Exit codes: 0 ok, 1 error, 2 usage. Output files are created with `O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW`, 0600.
- `picache db check` and `db salvage` switch to the owner of the data directory first and read `picache.db` read-only (`db.OpenReadOnly`); check also decodes the settings document with `settings.DecodeStored` (a damaged document passes SQLite's integrity check but stops the start; `settings.Open` then names `picache restore`), and salvage reports the lost rows of a table whose largest rowid cannot be read as unknown (`?`) instead of counting its failed rowid probes; salvage builds the new file's schema with `app.MigrateConfigDB` (the migrations of every `picache.db` component in start order, starting nothing) and never writes the source (DEPLOYMENT "Recovering a damaged picache.db").
- Routes that span packages are orchestrated by `api`, never by a domain package: "Only for this device" (`POST /filter/rules/device`: `clients.EnsureDeviceGroup` in one clients transaction, then `filter.AddRuleForGroup` in one filter transaction; when the rule step fails, `clients.RevertDeviceGroup` undoes the clients change exactly) and the checks of group upstreams against the DNS settings (a group's upstreams against `dns.bootstrap` and `dns.localDomain` when they are saved, the settings against the groups in `updateSettings`). `app` wires the reloads: a clients change (`clients.Registry.OnChange`) reloads the record groups (`dnsserver.ReloadRecords`) and the group upstream sets (`upstream.SetGroupUpstreams` with `clients.GroupUpstreamConfigs`); a forwarders change passes the target lists of the validating forwarders (`dnsserver.Deps.ValidatingForwarders`) to `upstream.SetValidatingForwarders`. The preset table of the family resolvers lives in `settings`, which both `clients` (validation) and `upstream` (the sets) import.
- `dnsserver`, `proxy` and `sni` declare **consumer-side interfaces** for their collaborators (see their `Deps`) so they can be tested with fakes.
- `api` imports domain packages; domain packages never import `api`. `app` imports everything and is imported only by `cmd`.

Third-party dependencies are limited to: `github.com/miekg/dns v1.1.73`, `github.com/quic-go/quic-go v0.63.0` (with `github.com/quic-go/qpack v0.6.0` for its `http3` package; the DoQ and DNS-over-HTTP/3 upstream clients only, never a QUIC listener; the quic-go server is used in tests only), `modernc.org/sqlite v1.59.0` (with `modernc.org/libc v1.75.7` pinned exactly; never bump libc alone), `golang.org/x/{crypto,sys,sync,net,time}` (pinned in `internal/deps`; `x/net/proxy` for the SOCKS5 tunnels of the outbound proxy; the DNSCrypt client uses `curve25519`, `salsa20`, `chacha20`, `poly1305` and `nacl/secretbox` of `x/crypto` and `crypto/ed25519`). Adding any other module needs an explicit decision.

---

## 5. Conventions

- Go 1.27, `CGO_ENABLED=0`, static binary. Release targets: linux/amd64, linux/arm64, linux/arm (v7 and v6), linux/386 (`GO386=sse2`) and linux/riscv64 (14.1).
- Logging: `log/slog`, attribute `component`. Never log secrets, passwords, tokens or CDN query strings. Repeated errors are logged when they change or at most hourly.
- JSON: `encoding/json/v2`; lowerCamelCase field names; API decoding rejects unknown members.
- Time: stored as INTEGER unix **milliseconds** (UTC). API entities use RFC 3339; time series use unix **seconds**.
- IDs: SQLite `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` for rows; service IDs are the cache-domains names; storage target and store IDs are 32 hex characters (the built-in target is `local`).
- **Table names are prefixed with their component** (`dns_records`, `filter_lists`, `client_groups`, `auth_sessions`, `storage_targets`, `logs_queries`, `proxy_noslice_hosts`, …). Each component migrates only its own tables via `db.Migrate(ctx, "<component>", steps)`; steps are append-only.
- Errors: domain packages return `apperr` errors for user-facing conditions; everything else is a 500 with a generic message, except a full data disk (SQLite's `SQLITE_FULL` or `ENOSPC`): 503 `unavailable` "the data disk is full: free space on it (System → Health & about)"; a sign-in and the setup, which must store a session, say so themselves ("cannot sign in: PiCache's data disk is full (a sign-in stores a session); free space on the host — signed-in browsers and API tokens keep working"). A read of `logs.db` that hits a damaged page (`SQLITE_CORRUPT`, `SQLITE_NOTADB`) is 503 `unavailable` "logs.db is damaged: restart PiCache; it moves the file aside (System → Health & about)".
- Lifecycle: every `Start(ctx)` **blocks** until ctx is done and all goroutines it started have exited; after it returns the component no longer touches its DB. The app runs each Start in a tracked goroutine and waits for them on shutdown before closing databases.
- Shutdown order (`app.shutdown`; on SIGTERM, SIGINT, a requested restart or a fatal error), chosen so a restart or an update interrupts DNS as briefly as possible: (1) the API's event streams end (`api.Server.EndStreams`; `http.Server.Shutdown` neither cancels request contexts nor ends a stream, so an open application-log stream used to hold the stop for the whole grace), and the web and DoH servers stop accepting and get 12 s to finish their requests, the download cache's server 3 s (a download that is cut off is resumed by its client with a range request), then they are closed; DNS (UDP, TCP, DoT), NTP, the SNI pass-through and the background components keep running, because they run in their own context, which a cancelled parent (the signal) does not cancel; (2) that context is cancelled: DNS and the other servers stop, the components flush and stop (5 s); (3) the listeners are closed, the cache store (at most 10 s) and the databases. DNS is therefore down only for the components' flush, the closing of the databases and the next start; the stop takes well under Docker's default 10 s unless a request needs the grace, and at most about 30 s (the compose files' and the Unraid template's stop timeout).
- Concurrency: hot-path structures are immutable snapshots behind `atomic.Pointer`. 64-bit counters use `atomic.Int64`/`atomic.Uint64` (never `atomic.AddInt64` on plain fields: 32-bit ARM alignment).
- OS-specific code lives in `*_linux.go` with portable `*_other.go` fallbacks so `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` work on Windows/macOS.
- Tests: table-driven unit tests per package, `testing/synctest` for time, `httptest` for HTTP; no network access in unit tests.
- SQL: parameterised statements only; sort columns from an allowlist.
- Bounds everywhere: every cache, map, queue, list and upload has an explicit size limit. The bounds of 0.15.0: route snapshot 4096 routes; hosts file 1 MiB, 10 000 lines, 4 KiB per line; WHOIS cache 4096 networks, queue 64, 1 lookup per 10 s and 100 per day, answers ≤ 256 KiB; NTP limiter 16 384 keys; log sink queues 1024 records each; network interfaces 64 (64 networks each); export ≤ 64 MiB (one at a time); sync answers ≤ 64 MiB; listeners files ≤ 16 KiB, 8 addresses per role; `PICACHE_INITIAL_CONFIG` and `picache config` files ≤ 1 MiB.
- Schema steps are append-only **from the moment they are merged to `main`**, not from the release: a nightly build (14.1) may already have applied them.

---

## 6. Security model

### 6.1 Threats and controls

| Threat | Control |
|---|---|
| Open DNS resolver / amplification | ACL: loopback, RFC 1918, ULA, link-local, CGNAT and **private** directly connected subnets plus user CIDRs (each at least /8 IPv4, /32 IPv6; `allowAllNetworks` is the explicit dangerous switch). Opt-in `dns.trustConnectedNetworks` (default off) also trusts every on-link prefix of this machine, public ones included (`netutil.ConnectedSubnets`: interfaces that are up, without loopback and the virtual bridges and tunnels of 7.1 step 9, IPv4 ≥ /8, IPv6 ≥ /48), so a LAN's global IPv6 prefix follows provider renumbering; off by default because a cloud server's on-link network can contain other tenants. The ACL is rebuilt every 60 s and on settings changes. UDP from others is dropped; TCP is closed at accept. `ANY` → NOTIMP; CHAOS class and `version.bind`/`id.server`/`hostname.bind` → REFUSED. Rate limit per rate-limit key (`netutil.RateKey`: LAN sources per address — IPv4 loopback, RFC 1918, CGNAT, link-local and on-link sources /32, IPv6 loopback, link-local, ULA and on-link sources /128 — other sources per `dns.rateLimitIpv4Prefix` (8–32, default 32) and `dns.rateLimitIpv6Prefix` (32–64, default 64), so the defaults are the keys of `netutil.ClientKey`; only the DNS limiter uses `RateKey`, login throttling, fill caps and connection limits keep `ClientKey`), default 50 qps burst 200; loopback, the router resolver (all its addresses, 7.1 step 6), local PTR upstreams, forwarder targets and the trusted EDNS forwarders (`dns.ednsClientTrusted`: a whole LAN behind one source) are exempt automatically. Every limiter operation is O(1): buckets are kept in least-recently-seen order and the oldest is evicted when the table (100 000) is full; limits change in place (`Reconfigure`). Only **private** connected subnets are trusted automatically (a public IPv6 LAN prefix needs `dns.allowedNetworks` or `dns.trustConnectedNetworks`). EDNS capped at 1232. DoT and DoH are behind the same ACL (19): DoT and the dedicated DoH listeners close other sources at accept, DoH on the web listeners answers them 403 (and needs the web ACL too); nothing is offered to sources outside the ACL. |
| Unwanted clients inside the allowed networks | `dns.blockedClients` (≤ 256 IP addresses, CIDRs of at least /8 or /32, MAC addresses or `clientid:<ClientID>`) get no DNS answer at all: source addresses and the ClientIDs of DoT and DoH queries at 7.1 step 2a (UDP dropped before parsing, TCP and DoT closed, a DoH stream reset), MAC addresses and addresses derived from EDNS after identification (end of step 5). Nothing is logged, recorded as seen or counted as refused. DNS only: the ACL, the connection limits, the cache proxy (:80) and the SNI pass-through (:443) are unchanged. A new address of a device without a known MAC (a fresh IPv6 privacy address) passes until the neighbour table has its MAC. Safety net at run time: loopback, this machine's addresses and MACs, the router's addresses and MACs (every IPv4 and IPv6 default gateway and the neighbour addresses with their MACs, in all modes of `dns.routerResolver`; the gateways from the start, the last known ones until a changed `routerResolver` is detected again, new ones before the router's DNS probe) and trusted EDNS forwarders (their addresses and neighbour-table MACs) are never dropped; a MAC entry matches a MAC from the neighbour table only when the address it belongs to is not protected either (so it never drops a trusted forwarder's own queries), a MAC a trusted forwarder sends in EDNS unless the MAC itself is protected. A matching entry is logged at WARN at most once per entry and hour, also after a restored backup or a changed router. The API refuses entries that equal or contain them (plus the container network's gateway in a bridge network; MACs of trusted forwarders read from the neighbour table at that moment); with `device:true` the address itself is checked before its MAC is taken. |
| DNS rebinding through PiCache's answers | `dns.rebindProtection` (default on, 7.1 step 14c): answers of the default set (default upstreams, clock guard, fallbacks, `default` forwarders) or of a group set (7.4) that point a name at 0/8, 10/8, 100.64/10, 127/8, 169.254/16, 172.16/12, 192.168/16, `::`, `::1`, fc00::/7, fe80::/10 or 64:ff9b:1::/48, or at an IPv6 address carrying such an IPv4 address (IPv4-mapped, IPv4-compatible, 6to4, NAT64 64:ff9b::/96 and the configured DNS64 prefix), are blocked (`blocked-rebind`), also while blocking is paused; target hints of HTTPS/SVCB records and target records of the additional section are removed. Exempt: the local domain, `home.arpa`, search domains, `dns.rebindAllow` (default `plex.direct`), the names of `web.allowedHosts` and names a user allow rule applies to. Local records, lease names, forwarders with explicit targets and the router are trusted as configured. The router's own rebind protection no longer sees the queries once devices use PiCache. **Residual risk: global IPv6 addresses of the LAN.** A public name may resolve to a global (GUA) address inside the LAN's own /64 — the router's, a NAS's, a printer's, or the visitor's own address, which a web page learns from its IPv6 source address — and the answer passes; browsers treat GUAs as public, so a page can reach such a service over IPv6 when it accepts foreign `Host` headers. These addresses are not rebind targets because a public name pointing at a LAN host's GUA is also the common, legitimate way to reach a self-hosted service (dynamic DNS for a NAS, the only way in on DS-Lite), which a default rule would break without notice. Mitigations: an IP rule or `ips` list for a static delegated prefix (7.1 step 14d), services that check `Host` and require authentication, or binding services to IPv4/ULA addresses (SECURITY "DNS rebinding"). |
| Hostile lists of answer addresses | Lists of the format `ips` and user IP rules block answers by the addresses in them (7.1 step 14d). **IP guard** (7.2): a list block broader than /16 (IPv4) or /32 (IPv6), one overlapping 0/8, 10/8, 100.64/10, 127/8, 169.254/16, 172.16/12, 192.168/16, 224/4, 240/4, `::`, `::1`, fc00::/7, fe80::/10 or ff00::/8, or an IPv6 block overlapping a prefix that embeds IPv4 (inside it or around it, like `64:ff9b::/32`) is ignored (counted as `ipBlocksIgnored`, health warning), so a hostile or broken list (`0.0.0.0/0`, `::/0`, a list of private ranges) can never block every answer or the LAN's own names; IP rules need at least /8 or /32. This machine's addresses are never blocked; answers of forwarders with explicit targets, the router and `localPtrUpstreams` (the admin's own network) are never checked; at most 256 addresses per answer are examined. |
| Family resolvers per group | The upstreams of a client group (a preset or its own list, 7.4) answer its clients instead of the default set and fail closed: a set that cannot be built or that no upstream answers gives SERVFAIL, never the default upstreams, the fallbacks or the (unfiltered) bootstrap servers; while the clock guard is active only the group's own plain upstreams given by IP address (a preset's plain addresses) are asked, else SERVFAIL. No client subnet is sent. A global disable or pause of blocking and a group pause do not switch them off (content protection, 16); disabling the group or removing the client does. Residual: a device that asks another resolver bypasses them (16 "Limits"). |
| Untrusted names | Device names (DHCP, PTR, lease and hosts-file names), MAC vendor names (cleaned by the OUI generator) and WHOIS (RDAP) names that become client and group names ("Only for this device", 7.2) and list titles read from `! Title:` lines (7.2) are untrusted: invalid UTF-8 and every character of the Unicode categories Cc and Cf (controls, bidi controls and other format characters) removed, white space collapsed, cut to 59 (group names) or 100 (titles) characters; the UI renders every name as text. A device group is reused only when it was created for that client (`device_client_id`) and the client is its only member; a new one gets " (2)" … " (99)" on a name collision, so a device named "Kids" never joins the admin's group "Kids". |
| Client identity claims via ClientID | A ClientID (the first label of a DoT SNI `<ClientID>.<serverName>`, the path of DoH `/dns-query/<ClientID>`) identifies but never authenticates: DoT sends it in cleartext (SNI), anyone can guess or copy one, and a device PiCache does not identify by address or MAC can claim any ClientID it knows and get that client's groups (like a forged MAC). A device identified by address, interface or MAC can never leave its client by sending a ClientID (7.1 step 5), so parental controls on such devices hold; a known ClientID wins over a `host:` identifier (the last rule); a ClientID can never unblock (the blocked checks use the source and its MAC; `clientid:` entries of `dns.blockedClients` can only drop more). |
| Client identity claims via interface | `iface:<name>` identifies the **transport source** by the output interface of the most specific non-default route to it (`netutil.InterfaceOf`: an in-memory snapshot of the main routing table, rebuilt every tick and on SIGHUP; Linux only), never by the destination address (Linux's weak host model lets a sender choose it) and never by the ingress interface of `IP_PKTINFO` (UDP only: a device could switch to TCP, DoT or DoH). An IPv6 link-local source (`fe80::/10`, on every interface, so no route names it) is identified by the zone the kernel reports with its address for UDP, TCP, DoT and DoH alike (`sin6_scope_id`: the interface it arrived on, which its reply must leave by; `netutil.LinkLocalZone`); a link-local source whose interface is unknown never reaches the MAC steps while `iface:` identifiers exist (only its address can identify it). Like a CIDR it wins over the MAC identifiers of the devices behind it, so a device on a guest VLAN or `wg0` cannot leave its client by copying a trusted device's MAC: use `iface:` for networks whose devices are all one client (guest VLAN, VPN), CIDRs or MACs for the main LAN. It applies to UDP, TCP, DoT, the dedicated DoH listeners, DoH on the web listeners without a trusted proxy and `POST /dns/lookup`, never to an address derived from EDNS or the effective client of DoH through a trusted proxy. Residual: a query with a source address of another interface's networks than the one it arrived on has a forged source; its reply goes to that other network, and TCP, DoT and DoH cannot complete a handshake, so it gains nothing an IP identifier would not already give. In a container bridge network every source arrives through the bridge. |
| Client identity claims via host name | `host:<name>` is spoofable by design (opt-in per identifier; the UI asks to confirm "A device chooses its own name: any device can claim this identity"): it matches an address whose own cached name (PiCache's DHCP lease name, PTR name or hosts-file name, each only while its name source is on; never the name of another address with the same MAC, never WHOIS) equals the name. A PTR name counts only for a LAN address (not public unicast, or inside a connected network): the reverse zone of a public address belongs to whoever holds it, who could otherwise claim a `host:` identity from the internet (e.g. over DoT); the residual is a LAN device choosing its own DHCP name or registering its own PTR name. It is the last rule of 7.1 step 5 (after ClientIDs), creates no learned MACs, is not accepted by `dns.blockedClients`, and the query path never triggers a lookup: an address whose name is not cached yet does not match, so its first queries get the next rule (usually Default). |
| WHOIS privacy | `clients.nameSources.whois` (off by default) sends data out: the masked network (IPv4 /24, IPv6 /48) of public source addresses to the RDAP servers of the IANA bootstrap files; never the host address, never an address inside `netutil.PrivateLANPrefixes`, `netutil.ConnectedSubnets`, the neighbour table, an entry of `dns.allowedNetworks` of /24 or /48 or longer, a trusted EDNS forwarder or the /48 of one of this machine's global IPv6 addresses, never while client addresses are anonymised. At most 1 lookup per 10 s and 100 per day, a queue of 64, an LRU of 4096 networks (positive 7 days, negative 24 h, memory only); https only, TLS 1.2+ against the system roots, 10 s, ≤ 256 KiB, at most 3 redirects to https and public addresses, never through a proxy; `org` and `country` cleaned as untrusted names. A separate annotation, never a name. |
| NTP | `PICACHE_NTP_LISTEN` (off by default) plus `ntp.enabled` (off): only client requests (mode 3, version 1–4, ≥ 48 bytes) are answered, with exactly 48 bytes (never larger than the request, so no amplification); other modes and malformed packets are dropped, no Kiss-o'-Death. Behind the DNS ACL (refused sources counted like DNS) and a separate limiter (`netutil.RateKey`, 4 packets/s, burst 8, 16 384 keys). With `dns.allowAllNetworks` it is an open NTP server (the UI and DEPLOYMENT warn). The clock state is read with `adjtimex` and modes 0 (6.2); an unsynchronised or unreadable clock answers with LI 3 and stratum 16. |
| Outbound proxy | `network.proxy` for the traffic `network.proxyFor` switches on (list and cache-domains downloads, the release check, notifications); never RDAP, the follower sync, DNS upstreams, the download cache or SNI. The targets are resolved and checked exactly as without a proxy (`netutil.SafeDialer.Filter`, incl. the `WithAllowPrivate` rule), then the proxy is asked for a tunnel **to the checked IP literal** (HTTP `CONNECT <ip>:<port>` with Basic credentials when set, answer headers ≤ 16 KiB, only 200; or SOCKS5 CONNECT with an IPv4/IPv6 address, never a domain), so it can never resolve a name to an address the SSRF rules refuse; TLS runs through the tunnel with the original host name; every redirect hop dials again. The proxy itself is resolved by the host resolver; link-local, multicast and unspecified addresses are refused, and so is this machine on one of PiCache's own listener ports. Errors name the proxy (`scheme://host:port`), never the credentials. Residual: the proxy sees every target address and host name (SNI) and, for plain http, the content; HTTP Basic and SOCKS5 credentials cross the network unencrypted. The password is sealed (`settings_secrets`, AAD `picache/settings/network.proxy.password`) and kept only while the proxy's origin and user name stay. Update downloads use only `PICACHE_UPDATE_PROXY` of the root-owned `picache.env` (the root helper never parses service-writable settings or unseals their secrets). |
| Sync credential | A sync token (scope `sync`, created by admins with the current password) reaches only the configuration export (`permExport`, 12): every other route answers 403. The export holds only data the R routes return (never notifications, storage targets, sealed values, accounts, tokens, the audit log, TLS material or runtime state). The follower seals the token (`settings_secrets`, AAD `picache/settings/sync.token`, kept only while the source's origin stays), sends it only after a TLS handshake verified against `sync.caPem` (trust anchors; a self-signed leaf is trusted as itself) or the system roots, never over a redirect or a proxy, and refuses sources that resolve to link-local, multicast, unspecified, loopback or this machine's addresses. Synced sections are read-only for every principal (409); no web or DNS access member is ever synced, so a sync can never lock the admin out of the follower. |
| pprof | `PICACHE_PPROF` (off by default): the handlers use `runtime/pprof` directly (never `net/http/pprof`, whose `init` registers on `http.DefaultServeMux`; a test checks that no server uses it), are registered through `s.route` behind the whole middleware, permission A, and answer only an effective client that is loopback or one of this machine's addresses (`ssh -L` from elsewhere); everyone else, and everyone while it is off, gets 404. One profile at a time, audited `system.pprof`. |
| Log sinks leaving the host | `PICACHE_LOG_FILE` and `PICACHE_LOG_SYSLOG` receive exactly the records stderr receives, after the same redaction (secret names, URL reduction) and the ring's privacy masking (`logs.anonymizeClientIps`, `logs.hideDomains`); the setup token (stderr only) is redacted there. The file is opened `O_WRONLY|O_APPEND|O_CREAT|O_NOFOLLOW`, 0640, only as a `<name>.log` file below `/var/log/picache`, directly in the data directory or below its `logs/`: never one of PiCache's state files (databases, `keys/`, `tls/`, the listeners files, the root helper's request directories), which the sink would append to, rename away and compress. Syslog is plain text on the network (no TLS). Each sink has a bounded queue (1024, overflow dropped and counted) and never blocks a log call. |
| Encrypted DNS listeners | DoT (`PICACHE_DOT_LISTEN`) and the dedicated DoH listeners (`PICACHE_DOH_LISTEN`): the DNS ACL at accept (before TLS), 32 connections per client key and 1024 in total, closed at accept while the protocol is switched off and closed at once when it is switched off; the handshake runs in the connection's goroutine with a 10 s deadline, DoT answers the queries of a connection one after another (no buffers for queries sent ahead), 1024 queries per connection, idle 30 s; DoH: 64 concurrent requests per client key (4096 keys), POST bodies ≤ 64 KiB, 32 HTTP/2 streams per connection, the dedicated server serves only `/dns-query` (no UI, API, cookies or health endpoints). Every query is checked again (ACL, blocked sources and ClientIDs, rate limit) and shares the 4096 queries in flight. The certificate is the one of the web UI; DoT/DoH count as serving only while it is usable. No access from outside the ACL is offered: devices away from home use a VPN. |
| Client identity claims via EDNS | Only sources in `dns.ednsClientTrusted` (≤ 16 single addresses) may name their client: exactly one ECS option with a full-length unicast address and/or exactly one option 65001 with a unicast MAC (7.1 step 4a); everything else is ignored. The derived identity decides groups, parental controls, rules, blocked identities and the query log; the source stays the transport address for the ACL, the blocked sources, the rate limit, the loop guard and this server's names, so a trusted forwarder cannot defeat the loop guard and a derived loopback address gets no loopback-only answers.
