**Categories**: every list has a `category`: one of the catalogue categories `general`, `security` (phishing, malware, threat intelligence, crypto-mining), `privacy` (trackers, telemetry of TVs and vendors), `adult`, `gambling`, `dating`, `piracy`, `social`, `doh-vpn-bypass`, `abused-tlds`, `url-shorteners`, `stalkerware`, `regional`, `allow` (small curated allowlists), or `other` (the user's own lists). `allow` is stored exactly for allowlists (a list whose kind becomes `allow` gets it, one whose kind becomes `block` without a category gets `other`); on create an absent category takes the catalogue entry's (same URL), else `other`; on update an absent category keeps the stored one. `catalogKey` is the key of the catalogue entry whose URL equals the list's URL exactly (`""` for own lists), set on create and whenever the URL changes; `catalogAliases` in `catalog.go` keeps an old key recognised when a release replaces a bound entry. On create an empty `kind` with a catalogue URL takes the entry's kind, and a kind that contradicts the entry is refused (field `kind`).

**Protection lists**: an enabled list of the categories `adult`, `gambling`, `dating`, `piracy` or `doh-vpn-bypass` is a protection list, whatever group it is assigned to and however it was added. `Filter.CheckProtection(qname, qtype, groups)` applies the list precedence (steps 6–9 and 11) over only the enabled protection lists that share a group with the client (a second source→groups table of the snapshot in which every other list has no groups; lock-free, allocation-free, at once when no protection list is enabled): user rules and other lists are not evaluated, so an allowlist does not lift a protection-list block, but the list's own `@@` entries apply. The DNS server enforces it like parental controls (7.1 step 7a). Protection lists also stay in `Filter.Check`. `POST /filter/explain` evaluates this section only (its `decision` can say `allow` for a name a protection list blocks at 7a; `POST /dns/lookup` shows the real outcome). A list decision carries its list's category (`Decision.Category`, the statistics purpose, 11).

**Known trackers** (no external tracker database: size and licensing): `filter.Match.category` is the list's category (`""` for rules). The query panel shows "Known tracker (<list names>)" in the explain result for the block matches of lists of the category `privacy` (an exception of such a list, action `allow`, does not count), whether or not they apply to the client. Explain runs only when the user presses "Explain" (never automatically), reads only the lists stored locally and enabled, through its semaphore and timeout, and never downloads a catalogue list; nothing is shown for a hidden query name (`logs.hideDomains`).

**Search** (`GET /filter/search`): the user rules and IP rules whose pattern contains the text (id order), then the local copies of the enabled lists (id order, one file at a time with Explain's parser; never downloaded), at most 200 hits; it shares Explain's semaphore (tried without waiting: 503 when busy) and its 10 s budget (`timedOut`, the hits so far are returned).

**Import and export of rules** (`/filter/rules/import`, `/filter/rules/export`): the text form of user rules (the list syntax plus `$dnstype`, `$denyallow`, `$reply`, `$dnsrewrite` with an address or NXDOMAIN/REFUSED/NOERROR, `$invert` and the `;querytype=`, `;reply=` and `;invert` suffixes of regular expressions), imported all or nothing in one transaction (API.md); the export writes what the import reads back. Batch changes of rules, IP rules and lists are one transaction each and recompile once.

**Catalogue** (`catalog.go`, 68 entries, first the default list HaGeZi Multi NORMAL): `{key, name, description, descriptionDe, url, kind, category, plainDomains, recommended, entries, maintainer, license, homepage}`, grouped in the category order. Every URL was verified for the release (HTTP 200, parsed by this package with the TLD guard, entries > 0, invalid and unsupported lines at most 1 % of all); `entries` is the count of that run. Invariants (tested): keys and https URLs unique, the category never `other`, kind `allow` ⇔ category `allow`, allowlists never recommended, never bound to a switch and at most 10 000 entries, lists above 1 000 000 entries ("large") never recommended or bound, both descriptions set. `recommended` = suggested for its category. PiCache ships no list content; the lists are downloaded from their maintainers. The category switches of the parental controls (`categoryPresets`, 16) bind `oisd-nsfw`, `hagezi-gambling-medium`, `shadowwhisperer-dating`, `hagezi-anti-piracy` and `hagezi-doh-vpn-bypass`.

**Entry budget**: one entry costs about 24 bytes (at most 16 in the matcher, about 8 in the kept parse result), plus the peak while a list is parsed and while a new matcher is built next to the old one. Measured in a 1 GiB container with `GOMEMLIMIT` 600 MiB (the default on such a host): 4 000 000 entries in four lists hold about 80 MiB of heap and peak at about 250 MiB RSS across a recompile (8 000 000: 155 MiB heap, 480 MiB peak). The budget follows the memory PiCache may use (0.16.0): `filter.BudgetFor(limit)` = 4 000 000 (`filter.MaxEntryBudget`) for an unknown limit or 1 GiB and more, else 4 000 000 × limit / 1 GiB rounded down to a multiple of 100 000, at least 500 000 (512 MiB: 2 000 000; 256 MiB: 1 000 000). `limit` is `cmd/picache`'s `budgetMemory()` (the lowest memory limit of PiCache's own cgroup and its ancestors as it is: cgroup v2 `memory.max` along the path of the `0::` line of `/proc/self/cgroup`, so a `MemoryMax=` of `picache.service` counts as well as a container's limit, else cgroup v1 `memory.limit_in_bytes` along the memory controller's path; else `MemTotal` rounded up to the next multiple of 128 MiB by `filter.NominalMemory`: the kernel and the GPU keep part of the memory, so a 1 GB board reports about 926 MiB and a 512 MB one about 430 MiB, and their budgets follow the nominal 1 GiB and 512 MiB), read at start into `config.Config.MemoryLimit` and set on the engine (`filter.Engine.SetEntryBudget`). Measured for 0.16.0 in a 512 MiB container (`docker run --memory 512m`, the default `GOMEMLIMIT` of 307 MiB): 2 000 000 entries in two lists peak at about 130 MiB RSS across recompiles (about 68 MiB without lists; the cgroup, page cache included, peaked at about 245 MiB). The health check `blocklists` warns above the budget ("the blocklists hold N entries; a small host may run short of memory", the hint names the budget of this host). IP entries and modified list entries count too; a modified entry costs about 64 bytes plus 8 per denyallow domain (at most about 2 MiB at the 20 000 cap). Enabling lists with `POST /filter/lists/batch` is refused with 409 (field `force`) when the current entries plus the estimates of the lists being enabled (their stored count, else the catalogue entry's) exceed the budget; `force: true` enables them anyway.

### 7.3 Blocking replies

| Mode | A | AAAA | Other |
|---|---|---|---|
| `null` (default) | `0.0.0.0` | `::` | NODATA + SOA |
| `nxdomain` | NXDOMAIN + SOA | NXDOMAIN + SOA | NXDOMAIN + SOA |
| `nodata` | NODATA + SOA | NODATA + SOA | NODATA + SOA |
| `refused` | REFUSED | REFUSED | REFUSED |
| `custom_ip` | `blockingIpv4` | `blockingIpv6` (or NODATA + SOA) | NODATA + SOA |

**Per-rule replies**: a user block rule's `reply` (`null`, `nxdomain`, `nodata`, `refused` or `custom_ip` with `replyIpv4`/`replyIpv6`; `""` = the blocking mode) replaces the mode in the answers that rule decides: steps 8 and 11 (the block of the 7c guard included) and `blocked-cname` when it blocks a CNAME target. The rows above apply (`custom_ip`: A → `replyIpv4`, AAAA → `replyIpv6`, else NODATA + SOA), with `blockedTtl`, the synthetic SOA and EDE 15; the status stays (`blocked-rule`, `blocked-regex`, `blocked-cname`: a rule is blocked traffic whatever its reply). Every other decision keeps the global mode: 7a (parental controls, protection lists), 10, 13a, 13b, 14c, 14d and every list entry. **`self`**: `filter.blockingIpv4/Ipv6` (mode `custom_ip`) and a rule's `replyIpv4/Ipv6` accept `self`, the first address of the family that a server-name answer (7.1 step 6) would give this client (`dns.serverNameAddresses`, in bridge mode the configured cache addresses, else the address on the client's subnet or the primary one; loopback for loopback clients; never IPv6 link-local); none → NODATA + SOA. A browser sent to this server's address meets the download cache (:80, 403 with `Connection: close`) and the SNI pass-through (:443). The name of a download service (`MatchDNS`) never gets this server's address, `self` or an explicit address of this machine, a server name or the download cache: the proxy and the SNI relay serve those names without asking the filter, so the blocked content would be fetched anyway; that family answers NODATA + SOA (traced `download service <id>: no reply with this server's address …`).

TTL `filter.blockedTtl` (default 10 s). Synthetic SOA: `picache.invalid. hostmaster.picache.invalid. 1 1800 900 604800 <ttl>`. **Pause**: global, optional duration up to 7 days (the UI offers 30 s, 5 min, 1 h, until the next 06:00 on the host's clock, a custom duration of 1 minute to 7 days, and until re-enabled; `BlockingStatus` reports the host's `timeZone` and `utcOffsetMinutes` for it), persisted, auto re-enabled. **Pause per group** (16): the lists and rules of one group, up to 7 days. Neither pause lifts parental controls, safe search or protection lists (content protection, 7.1); the security lists of the paused scope pause too.

### 7.4 Upstreams and cache

- Upstream syntax: `host[:port]` (plain UDP), `udp://host[:port]`, `tcp://host[:port]`, `tls://host[:port]` (DoT, ALPN `dot`), `https://host[:port]/path` (DoH, RFC 8484 POST, ID 0, HTTP/2; never switched to HTTP/3), `quic://host[:port]` (DoQ, RFC 9250, default port 853, ALPN `doq`), `h3://host[:port]/path` (DoH over HTTP/3, default port 443) and `sdns://…` (a DNS stamp, below; the scheme case-insensitive, kept byte for byte). Plain, `tcp://`, `tls://` and `quic://` entries also take the port as `host#port` (`127.0.0.1#5335`, as other DNS software writes it; `url.Parse` makes it a fragment, so `settings.ParseUpstream` reads it as the port); anything else after `#`, `#port` next to `:port`, and a fragment in `https://`/`h3://` URLs are refused. Stored values that versions before 1.0.0 accepted with such text (they ignored it) are read with their old meaning, the text after `#` dropped (`settings.LegacyUpstream`: the default upstreams, fallbacks and local PTR upstreams when the document is loaded or decoded for a restore, with a warning in the log; forwarder targets and group upstreams when their rows are read, logged once per forwarder domain or group name when they are loaded: at the start and after a sync or restore brought them; all of them in the export of such a primary that a follower applies, 15.4); the next save stores the used value, new input with such text is still refused. The health check `upstreams` fails when no default upstream could be built ("no usable upstream DNS server is configured"; an empty default set counted as healthy before). Every save (the settings, forwarders and their import, groups, the follower sync) stores a valid `host#port` as `host:port` (`settings.CanonicalUpstream`: an IPv6 address in brackets, the scheme as typed): versions before 1.0.0 ignore `#port` and would ask the default port after going back. A `host#port` that a release candidate stored as typed is read with its port and stored as `host:port` at the next save. Other entries are kept and shown as typed. DoT, DoH, DoQ, HTTP/3 and plain upstreams given by name and stamps without an address are resolved via the bootstrap IPs only, with the TTL refresh of the bootstrap cache.
- **DNS stamps** (`settings` `stamp.go`, fuzzed): at most 1024 characters, base64url without padding, parsed strictly (every length checked, no trailing bytes); DNSCrypt (0x01), DoH (0x02), DoT (0x03) and DoQ (0x04) only (plain, oblivious DoH and relays are refused). The properties are ignored; the address (`IP[:port]`, IPv6 in brackets) is dialled directly, the host name is the TLS name, the port is the address's, else the host name's, else the default; the stamp's bootstrap addresses are ignored. Certificate hashes (SHA-256 of a certificate's TBS part) are enforced in addition to the normal verification against the system roots (one certificate of the verified chain must have a listed hash; never `InsecureSkipVerify`). Stamps are named `sdns:<dnscrypt|doh|dot|doq>:<host>` (the provider name for DNSCrypt) in the status, logs, metrics and block reasons, never by the stamp, its address or a DoH path (`UpstreamSpec.Display`, `upstream.UpstreamStat.name`).
- **DoQ and HTTP/3** (quic-go as a client only): idle connections close after 60 s, no keep-alives, the handshake bounded by the attempt, no 0-RTT (queries are replayable), no incoming bidirectional streams (DoQ: no incoming streams at all; HTTP/3 accepts the server's control and QPACK streams, at most 8), no qlog; TLS 1.3 with the system roots. DoQ keeps one connection per upstream (dialled again after it failed or idled out), one stream per query (2-byte length prefix, ID 0, the send side closed), at most 64 streams at a time, replies ≤ 64 KiB. HTTP/3: an `http3.Transport` per upstream, response headers ≤ 16 KiB, bodies ≤ 64 KiB, no redirects, no proxies. quic-go's receive-buffer warning is switched off before the first QUIC socket. A QUIC connection whose path died silently ends with the idle timeout (at most 60 s).
- **DoH connections** (`https://`): at most 4 connections per upstream, dials in progress included (further requests wait for one instead of dialling on their own; HTTP/2 multiplexes the queries on one); every dial is bounded by the attempt timeout (3 s for all addresses of the host; net/http dials detached from the request, so a black-holed upstream would otherwise leave a socket and a goroutine per request pending for minutes); each address but the last gets an equal share of the time left (at least 750 ms) and the last one the rest, so an address that drops the packets silently (a broken IPv6 path with `dns.bootstrapPreferIpv6`, a filtered anycast address) does not keep every dial from reaching the next one (DoT and DoQ share their attempt among the addresses the same way); an HTTP/2 connection that received no frame for 10 s is pinged and closed when the ping gets no answer within 5 s, so a silently dead connection (lost NAT state, a router reboot) is replaced within about 15 s instead of after the server's stream limit of cancelled requests; idle connections close after 60 s. **Resend on a closed connection or a cut handshake** (`exchangeHTTP`, also HTTP/3 for the first case): a request that fails before any response on a connection that carried a request before (the server closed or reset it, sent GOAWAY, or closed an idle HTTP/1.1 connection while the query was on its way: dns.quad9.net closes HTTP/2 connections after seconds to minutes without GOAWAY, and a query in flight then failed with "unexpected EOF") is sent again once, at once, after the idle connections are closed, so it goes over a fresh connection, within the attempt's context. net/http and quic-go resend a POST only when it surely was not processed; a DNS query is idempotent. Never after a response (any HTTP status, a malformed or oversized body), after the context ended, or for a failure of a new connection other than a cut handshake. A request whose new connection had its TLS handshake cut by the server (EOF or a connection reset, reported by `httptrace.TLSHandshakeDone`: dns.quad9.net closes or resets a share of new handshakes on its DoH and DoT ports) is sent once more too, at once, within the context; certificate and verification errors, alerts, timeouts and refused connections are not. At most one resend per query in total: a query already resent after a closed reused connection fails when the handshake of its new connection is cut. HTTP/3 has no TCP handshake to cut. DoT resends the same way: once after a stale pooled connection, or once when the handshake of a new connection was cut (`cutHandshakeError`, marked at the dial so a bootstrap lookup error never counts).
- **DNSCrypt** (protocol 2, es-versions 1 X25519-XSalsa20Poly1305 and 2 X25519-XChaCha20Poly1305): the certificates come from a TXT query for the provider name sent directly to the stamp's address (UDP, TCP when truncated; at most 8 records of exactly 124 bytes, signed with the provider key, valid now; es-version 2 preferred, then the highest serial), refreshed hourly and when the certificate expires (a failed refresh keeps the current one until it expires; an undecryptable reply requests an early refresh at most once a minute); a new X25519 key pair per certificate; queries padded to multiples of 64 bytes (at least 256 over UDP), UDP first and TCP when the reply is truncated; replies must carry the resolver magic and the query's nonce prefix (otherwise discarded like a mismatched reply), ≤ 64 KiB.
- **PiCache as its own upstream**: an entry of `dns.upstreams`, `dns.fallbackUpstreams`, a forwarder target or a group upstream whose host (a stamp's host) is `dns.encrypted.serverName`, a name `<label>.<serverName>` or an entry of `dns.serverNames` (also with the local domain) is refused ("an upstream must not be PiCache itself"). An upstream given by one of PiCache's own addresses is accepted (a local resolver on another port of this machine is legitimate); such a loop is bounded by the in-flight de-duplication and the upstream timeout. A plain upstream given by name (in `dns.upstreams`, `dns.fallbackUpstreams` and forwarder targets; `localPtrUpstreams` and `routerResolver` stay IP-only) must be a public fully qualified name: at least two labels, not equal to or below the local domain, `home.arpa`, `localhost`, `test`, `invalid`, `onion`, `local`, `internal` or `arpa`, and for forwarder targets not below a resolv.conf search domain ("plain DNS upstreams given by name must be public DNS names; use an IP address for local servers"). Only its resolved addresses that are public unicast and not this machine (the SafeDialer's filter) are dialled; if none remain the attempt fails ("resolves to no public address"), so a named target is never loopback, PiCache itself or the router and needs no loop guard or rate-limit exemption. Every target given by name needs bootstrap servers (settings validation for upstreams and fallbacks, forwarder validation for targets). A local resolver (a Docker service, a NAS) is configured by IP address.
- Defaults: Quad9 DoH (`https://dns.quad9.net/dns-query`, which filters malware) as the only default upstream, and Cloudflare's malware-filtering DoH (`https://security.cloudflare-dns.com/dns-query`, another operator whose blocks — 0.0.0.0/`::` and EDE — are detected like Quad9's) as the fallback: load-balancing Quad9 with an unfiltered resolver made its blocking random, and the fallback gives the provider redundancy; bootstrap `9.9.9.9`, `149.112.112.112`, `1.1.1.1`, `1.0.0.1`, `2620:fe::fe`, `2606:4700:4700::1111` (settings migration v3 appends the two IPv6 addresses to a stored list that equals the old IPv4-only default exactly; settings migration v4 replaces the old default upstream list, 3). Bootstrap servers are asked in the configured order and the resolved addresses are dialled IPv4 first, or IPv6 first with `dns.bootstrapPreferIpv6` (IP literals are unaffected); without a route for a family the attempt fails at once and the next address is tried.
- Modes: `load_balance` (default; weighted by EWMA RTT and recent failures, fail over on error), `parallel`, `strict`, `fastest_addr`. Per attempt 3 s, total `upstreamTimeoutMs`. `fastest_addr` asks the default set like `parallel` and, on a cache miss, orders a NOERROR A or AAAA answer with at least two addresses of the type: the first 8 addresses that pass the SafeDialer's filter (public unicast, not this machine, embedded IPv4 judged) are probed by a TCP connect to 443 (80 only after 443 failed; connect and close, no data, no TLS) within 300 ms per answer; the address with the fastest connect moves to the front of its records (the others keep their order; nothing succeeds → unchanged); the reordered answer is cached and hits are not probed; results (RTT or failure) are cached 10 minutes per address (LRU, 4096); at most 32 probe dials at a time and 100 new targets per second (token bucket, burst 100) — an answer whose probes cannot all get a slot and a token is ordered by cached results only (fail open, no waiting). ResolveVia sets use `load_balance` while `fastest_addr` is set.
- **Default set and ResolveVia sets**: the default set is what `Resolve` answers with: the default upstreams, the clock-guard set while the clock guard is active, and the fallbacks. Queries reach it at step 13 and through forwarders with the target `default`. Everything else (forwarders with explicit targets, `(unqualified)`, the router resolver, `localPtrUpstreams`) is a ResolveVia set: no fallbacks, no client subnet, no classification of blocked answers, no fastest-address order.
- **Group sets** (upstreams per client group, a third kind): a client group has an `upstreamPreset` (`cloudflare-family`, `opendns-familyshield`, `cleanbrowsing-family`: `settings.UpstreamPresets`, each a DoH URL and its plain addresses for the clock guard, sources cited in the code) or its own `upstreams` (≤ 8, the syntax and rules of `dns.upstreams`), never both and never on the Default group. `SetGroupUpstreams` (called by `app` at start and on every clients change; the sets are also rebuilt when the bootstrap servers change) builds one set per distinct list (a preset counts as its list; at most 16 lists and 64 distinct upstreams across them, 409), shared by the groups that name it, in its own registry: built on configuration changes, never on the query path, never evicted by queries; a replaced set is closed after the swap. Selection (`GroupFor`): among the client's enabled groups (pauses do not matter), a group with a preset wins, else one with its own list, the lowest id among equals. `ResolveGroup` answers with the set's own cache namespace and in-flight de-duplication, the classification of blocked answers and the fastest-address order like the default set; no fallbacks and no client subnet. Fail closed: a set that cannot be built (e.g. a name without bootstrap servers after a restore) or a fetch that no group upstream answered is an error (SERVFAIL, 7.1 step 13), never the default set or the fallbacks. While the clock guard is active only the plain UDP/TCP entries of the group's own list given by IP address (a preset's plain addresses) are asked; none → error, never the global guard set or the (unfiltered) bootstrap servers. `LookupIP` (list and cache-domains downloads, the proxy) and client-name PTR lookups stay on the default set. `GET /dns/upstreams` reports the sets (`groups`); health check `upstreams`, after the checks above: a set that could not be built or has no healthy upstream → warn "the upstreams of group <names> are not answering: their clients get SERVFAIL".
- **Fallbacks** (`dns.fallbackUpstreams`, ≤ 4, same syntax as `dns.upstreams`; `[]` = off): used only by fetches of the default set (client queries at step 13, `default` forwarders, DNS64's A lookups, `LookupIP`), never while the clock guard is active; they see exactly the names the default upstreams would see (private reverse zones, the local domain, `home.arpa`, search domains, special-use names, names of explicit forwarders and — while `dns.domainNeeded` is on — single-label A/AAAA/HTTPS/SVCB/ANY queries never reach them). Trigger: every primary attempt of a fetch ended without any DNS reply (transport errors, timeouts); a reply of any rcode (SERVFAIL — often a DNSSEC-bogus verdict of a validating upstream —, REFUSED, NXDOMAIN) never triggers them, nor does a reply with EDE 15–17. **Hedge**: while every default upstream is unhealthy (3 failures in a row each), a fetch still asks them but also asks the fallbacks once none of them replied within 500 ms (at once when they all failed sooner); the first good reply wins and ends the other phase (its attempts are not counted), so a dead default upstream costs each fetch 0.5 s instead of the whole attempt, and its first reply (within 0.5 s) makes it healthy again. Budget: with fallbacks configured the primary phase is bounded by min(`upstreamTimeoutMs`, 7 s) and the fallback phase (inside the same in-flight call) asks all fallbacks in parallel for at most 2.5 s (first good reply wins; SERVFAIL/REFUSED are soft failures), so one fallback round ends within the 10 s query timeout; without fallbacks nothing changes. The answer is cached under the default set's key and records `fallback` (`Info.Fallback`, also on hits); classification, ECS and the fastest-address order apply to it. If the fallbacks fail too, nothing is cached and the client gets SERVFAIL (status `error`). The fallbacks keep their own statistics (`GET /dns/upstreams` `fallbacks`) and the time one last answered (`fallbackLastUsed`). Health check `upstreams` (first match): clock guard → warn; no default upstream healthy and no fallback healthy (or none configured) → fail; no default upstream healthy → warn "fallback DNS in use: the upstream DNS servers are not answering"; the fallbacks answered at least 3 fetches within the last 5 minutes → warn "fallback DNS in use: the upstream DNS servers left several queries of the last 5 minutes unanswered" (`Resolver.FallbacksSince`: a ring of the times of the last 8 fallback answers, atomics only; one or two fallback answers, e.g. after a TLS handshake the provider cut, do not warn); else ok.
- **Answers blocked by the upstream** (default set only, classified at fetch time where the answering upstream is known; the first match wins): `ede` — the reply's OPT carries an EDE with code 15 (Blocked), 16 (Censored) or 17 (Filtered); `null-ip` — an A/AAAA NOERROR answer with at least one record of the type in which every A record is 0.0.0.0 and every AAAA record `::` (CNAMEs may precede them); `block-page` — the same shape with every address a Cisco Umbrella (OpenDNS) block page (146.112.61.104–110 and their IPv4-mapped IPv6 forms); `nxdomain-no-ra` — NXDOMAIN with RA=0 from one of Quad9's filtering endpoints (`dns.quad9.net`, `dns9.quad9.net`, `dns11.quad9.net`, `9.9.9.9`, `149.112.112.112`, `9.9.9.11`, `149.112.112.11`, `2620:fe::fe`, `2620:fe::9`, `2620:fe::11`, `2620:fe::fe:11`, so the plain IPs of the clock-guard set are covered). Only the first 16 options of the upstream OPT are examined; the logged EDE is the first one with code 15–17, else the first EDE; its text is cut to 200 bytes at a rune boundary, made valid UTF-8 and stripped of control and bidi characters. The EDE of every fetch (all sets) is logged. A classified answer is cached with `Info.Block` even when it would not be cached otherwise (e.g. NXDOMAIN without SOA), for `dns.upstreamBlockedTtl` (10–86400 s, default 300), which replaces the normal or negative TTL, the TTL clamp and the 1 h negative cap (at most the 7-day hard cap); serve-stale applies as usual; with the cache disabled nothing is stored. `LookupIP` shares the fetches and the cache of the default set and ignores the classification. The DNS server turns such answers into `blocked-upstream` (7.1 step 13a). Filtering by the upstream is best effort while the clock guard is active: the plain bootstrap servers (e.g. 1.1.1.1) do not filter.
- **Client subnet** (`dns.ecs`, RFC 7871, off by default): `client` sends the source's /24 (IPv4) or /56 (IPv6) when the source is a public unicast address (nothing otherwise), `custom` sends `dns.ecs.customSubnet` (a public IPv4 /8–/24 or IPv6 /32–/56 network, host bits masked); SCOPE 0, the address masked to the source prefix; only with client queries answered by the default set (step 13, `default` forwarders, DNS64's A lookups, the targets of local CNAMEs resolved upstream), never to ResolveVia sets, the bootstrap servers or with `LookupIP`. A reply whose ECS option differs from the sent one in family, source prefix length or address is discarded like a malformed reply (RFC 7871 7.3); an ECS option in a reply to a query without one is ignored. Quad9's default endpoint and Cloudflare ignore ECS (an ECS-enabled endpoint such as `dns11.quad9.net` is needed).
- Plain UDP retries over TCP on TC. Replies must match the question or are discarded. Upstream queries are built fresh (RD=1, AD=1 (RFC 6840 5.7), our OPT 1232, the client subnet of `dns.ecs`), no client EDNS options. Queries to encrypted upstreams (DoT, DoH, DoQ, HTTP/3) also carry an EDNS Padding option (RFC 7830) that makes them a multiple of 128 bytes (RFC 8467), so their length does not tell the length of the name and the resolver pads its reply; the padding of the reply is removed before it is cached. Plain DNS, the bootstrap servers and DNSCrypt (its own padding) get none. **DO** by `dns.dnssecMode`: `off` sends DO=1 only when the client set DO; `passthrough` and `validate` send DO=1 on every upstream query. The client's CD bit never reaches an upstream in any mode (a validating upstream stays a second layer; only the DNSSEC test sends CD=1, 7.6).
- **AD**: in `off` and `passthrough` the upstream's AD is passed on (step 15 decides whether the client gets it). In validate mode every message the upstream package returns (`Resolve`, `ResolveGroup`, `ResolveVia`, `ResolveValidating`; validated or not) carries AD exactly when its verdict is `secure` and it is not stale; the upstream's AD is discarded on every route.
- **Verdicts** (validate mode, 7.6): a fetch of a validated route is validated once, after classification and before the fastest-address order (which skips `bogus` answers), and its verdict (`Info.DNSSEC`, an `upstream.Verdict{Status, Zone, Reason, EDE}`; nil = not validated) is cached with the answer; hits return it without any verification, stale hits are `indeterminate` (`stale answer`). Rcodes other than NOERROR and NXDOMAIN and answers classified as blocked by the upstream are not validated. The chain lookups (DS and DNSKEY, DO=1, CD=0, a fresh ID, no client subnet or client options; never filtered, logged, counted in the query statistics or rate limited; counted in the upstream statistics; classified only to detect an upstream block) go through the route that answered the fetch and never another: the default route (the default set with its fallbacks, the clock-guard set while it is active), the same group set (fail closed) or the validating forwarder's targets. The chain lookups of a fetch that the fallbacks answered (none of the default upstreams replied) go to the fallbacks at once, so a dead default upstream does not use up the validation's 4 s before they are reached. They have their own in-flight map and cap (7.6), separate from the client fetches.
- **DNSSEC probes** (validate mode): every upstream of a validated route (the default set, the fallbacks, every group set, every validating forwarder's targets and, while the clock guard is active, the clock-guard set and the group sets' guard parts: otherwise they are no route, and probing them over plain DNS would only give someone on that path a say in the time checks and the health check) is probed with `. DNSKEY` and `. SOA` (DO=1, CD=0, 2 s each, straight through its transport: not counted in its statistics, never cached) at the start, after every rebuild of its set, on the switch to `validate`, every 30 minutes, every 30 s while its state is `unknown` or its probes get no reply, and (at most once a minute) after a query got a root DNSKEY RRset without an anchor match from it; at most 16 probes at a time. States (`UpstreamStat.dnssec`): `capable`, `no-dnssec` (`dnssecError` says why), `anchor-mismatch`, `unknown` (7.6). A change to `capable` empties the response cache.
- **Flushes**: switching `dns.dnssecMode` empties the response cache, the `LookupIP` cache and the key and failure caches (and starts the probes on the switch to `validate`); resuming time checks empties the response cache and the key and failure caches; `POST /dns/cache/flush` empties all of them.
- Response cache: key (lower qname, qtype, qclass, DO sent upstream, upstream-set id, the client subnet sent — none for most queries —, `val`: the fetch validates, so an entry whose fetch started before a mode switch and finished after the flush is never served after it); LRU of `cacheSize` entries; TTL clamp; negative caching per RFC 2308 (≤ 1 h; also for NODATA/NXDOMAIN at the end of a CNAME chain; negative answers without SOA are not cached, except answers blocked by the upstream); SERVFAIL 5 s; serve-stale (TTL 30, background refresh, one per key). A cached answer keeps its block classification, the upstream's EDE, whether a fallback answered and its DNSSEC verdict. A `secure` entry lives at most until the earliest RRSIG expiration of its RRsets (`dns.cacheMinTtl` never raises it beyond); a `bogus` entry lives min(its TTL, 30 s) after a cryptographic failure and 5 s after a lookup failure or an exhausted budget, is never served stale, and a lookup-failure `bogus` never replaces a servable (fresh or stale) entry, like a SERVFAIL. Replies keep the client's question case and Id; TTLs are decremented by the cached age.
- Duplicate records (same owner name, class, type and RDATA; RFC 2181 §5, e.g. Docker's embedded DNS repeats every A/AAAA record) are removed from each section of an upstream reply before it is cached or answered, keeping the first copy with the lowest TTL of its copies; sections of more than 256 records (pairwise check) are passed on unchanged.
- In-flight dedup: identical queries (the same cache key, so two client subnets never share an exchange) share one exchange; each waiter gets its own copy.
- **Clock guard**: while the clock is before the binary's build date, encrypted upstreams are skipped and plain DNS to the configured plain upstreams given by IP address and to the bootstrap IPs is used (logged once, health warning); plain upstreams given by name and the fallbacks are left out. `quic://`, `h3://` and every DNS stamp count as encrypted (DNSCrypt certificates depend on the clock too). Certificate errors alone never trigger this.
- **Stale clock** (a device without a real-time clock whose time was restored from its last shutdown, after the build date, while its upstreams renewed their certificates): when the TLS handshake with an encrypted upstream (DoT, DoH, DoQ, HTTP/3) fails because a certificate is **not yet valid** by the host clock, the host clock is readable and not synchronised (the NTP server's adjtimex reader, `SetClockReader`), and the chain verifies against the roots for the upstream's name at the start of that certificate's validity, that start becomes a lower bound of the real time (a CA never dates a certificate ahead), and the attempt is repeated at once: certificates of the encrypted upstreams are then checked at that time while the host clock is behind it (`tls.Config.Time`; logged once at WARN). Encryption and authentication stay; nothing falls back to plain DNS. A later clock never accepts a certificate that has expired by the host clock; a synchronised or unreadable clock (containers), an expired certificate or an unverifiable chain change nothing. So a host that resolves its NTP server names through PiCache gets its time again. DNSCrypt certificates are not covered.
- `LookupIP(host, want6)` resolves via the default set (with the fallbacks, without a client subnet) with caching and never consults local data. In validate mode its answers are validated like client fetches: `bogus` is an error (`*net.DNSError` "DNSSEC validation failed: <zone>: <failure>", not cached), every other verdict gives the addresses; no log or health side effects.

### 7.5 IPv6 answers: disableAAAA and DNS64

Both are off by default and cannot be on together (settings validation, field `dns.disableAAAA`).

- **`dns.disableAAAA`** (networks with broken IPv6): an AAAA query that reaches the forwarding stage (after special-use names, local records, parental controls, download cache answers and filtering; step 12a) is answered NOERROR/NODATA with the synthetic SOA (TTL 60), status `special`, reason `aaaa-disabled`; nothing is forwarded. `ipv6hint` is removed from forwarded HTTPS/SVCB answers and AD is then cleared (step 14a). Local AAAA records, this server's names and the local zones (router resolver) keep answering AAAA.
- **DNS64** (`dns.dns64 {enabled, prefix}`, RFC 6147/6052, for IPv6-only networks with a NAT64 gateway; `prefix` default `64:ff9b::/96`, only /96 prefixes outside IPv4-mapped, IPv4-compatible, link-local and multicast space are accepted, field `dns.dns64.prefix`; host bits are masked): for an AAAA query that was forwarded and allowed (status `forwarded`, `cached` or `stale`, after CNAME inspection) whose NOERROR answer contains no AAAA record (NODATA, also at the end of a CNAME chain), the A records of the chain's final name are resolved through the same upstreams (the conditional forwarder or the default upstreams) and synthesised as AAAA = prefix + IPv4, owned like the A records; CNAMEs of the A answer are appended; the authority section is dropped. TTL = min(A TTL, the AAAA answer's negative TTL: min(SOA TTL, SOA minimum)) when it carried a SOA, else the A TTL. Never synthesised: IPv4 addresses in `0.0.0.0/8`, `127.0.0.0/8`, `169.254.0.0/16`, `255.255.255.255/32` (RFC 6147 5.1.4; if only such addresses exist the NODATA stays), names with an existing AAAA record (answered as they are), NXDOMAIN and failed A lookups (the original answer stays), and names that would be blocked for the client (filtering groups): the final name if it differs from the query name, and (with `cnameInspection`) CNAME targets in the A answer, both unless blocking is paused or the query name is allowlisted. Synthesised answers never carry AD; the status stays, the log reason is `dns64`. A query with DO and CD set gets no synthesis (RFC 6147 5.5; every DNSSEC mode): the AAAA answer is returned as it is. In validate mode a `bogus` A lookup for a CD=0 client makes the answer SERVFAIL (7.1 step 13v) instead of keeping the NODATA answer, and the logged `dnssecStatus` is the worse of the two fetches. Never synthesised from an A answer the default upstream blocked itself (7.4). The synthesised AAAA records are then checked by the rebind protection (step 14c, `dns.rebindProtection`): an embedded IPv4 address that is a rebind target (e.g. A 10.0.0.1 of a public name → `64:ff9b::a00:1`) makes the answer `blocked-rebind`; the configured prefix is judged by its embedded address whether DNS64 is on or not. PTR queries for addresses inside the prefix (step 5a) are answered from the PTR of the embedded IPv4 address: rewritten to its `in-addr.arpa` name, resolved through steps 6–14 (private IPv4 reverse zones stay local), then every record owned by that name is renamed back; AD cleared; reason `dns64` unless the answer has another one (e.g. a blocking reason).

### 7.6 DNSSEC validation (`internal/dns/dnssec`, `dns.dnssecMode`)

**Modes** (`dns.dnssecMode`, trimmed and lower-cased; 400 `dns.dnssecMode` "must be off, passthrough or validate"; default of new installations `validate`, 13; upgraded installations keep their behaviour, 3):

| Mode | Upstream queries | AD to clients | Local validation |
|---|---|---|---|
| `off` (0.16 `dns.dnssec:false`) | DO=1 only when the client set DO | the upstream's AD (step 15 rules) | no |
| `passthrough` (0.16 `dns.dnssec:true`) | DO=1 always | the upstream's AD (step 15 rules) | no |
| `validate` | DO=1 always | only PiCache's own `secure` verdict of a fresh answer | the validated routes (below) |

In every mode the client's CD bit never reaches an upstream and upstream queries carry AD=1 (7.4). **Alias** `dns.dnssec:bool` (API clients, scripts, `PICACHE_INITIAL_CONFIG`, 0.16 primaries; like `updates.includePrereleases`): `reconcileDNSSEC` runs in `Store.update` next to `reconcileChannel`, so PATCH/PUT `/settings`, the initial configuration and the follower sync get it: `dnssecMode` `""` keeps the stored mode; a changed `dnssecMode` wins, and a `dnssec` changed in the same write that contradicts it (`dnssec != (dnssecMode != "off")`) → 400 `dns.dnssecMode` "dnssec contradicts dnssecMode: send dnssecMode only"; a changed `dnssec` alone maps `false` → `off` and `true` → `passthrough` when the stored mode was `off` (`passthrough` and `validate` stay); every save writes `dnssec = (dnssecMode != "off")`. Switching the mode empties the response cache, the `LookupIP` cache and the key and failure caches; switching to `validate` starts the probes.

**Statuses** (the verdict of a validated answer; an answer that is not validated has none):

| Status | Exactly when | Reply |
|---|---|---|
| `secure` | every RRset of the answer and of its denial proof verified along a chain of trust from a built-in anchor while time checks are active | the answer; AD per step 15 |
| `insecure` | a validated proof that the answer lies below an insecure delegation: a signed NSEC/NSEC3 no-DS proof at a delegation (NS bit, no DS bit), an NSEC3 opt-out span covering the name, or a validated DS set whose entries all have unsupported algorithms, digest types or keys; or NSEC3 parameters above the limit | the answer, AD clear; EDE 1, 2 or 27 (EDNS clients) when that was the cause |
| `bogus` | inside a zone proven signed (every zone is: the root is anchored) anything that cannot be verified: a missing, invalid, expired or not yet valid RRSIG; no DNSKEY matching the DS; a DS or DNSKEY lookup that timed out, answered SERVFAIL or REFUSED, or came back malformed or unsigned; a missing or invalid denial or wildcard proof; a signer that is not the zone; a chain loop; an exhausted budget or limit | SERVFAIL with the EDE below (client CD=0, step 13v); a CD=1 client gets the data, AD clear |
| `indeterminate` | only (a) the answer or one of its chain lookups came from an upstream whose probe state is `no-dnssec` or `anchor-mismatch`; (b) time checks are suspended and the verdict would be `secure` or `insecure`; (c) the answer is served stale | the answer, AD clear, never SERVFAIL |

- **Failure policy**: no other degradation exists; a lookup that fails or cannot be proven is `bogus`, never `insecure` or `indeterminate`. Not validated (no status): rcodes other than NOERROR and NXDOMAIN, answers the upstream classified as its own block (`Info.Block`; they stay `blocked-upstream`, 7.1 step 13a), queries of type RRSIG, NSEC and NSEC3 (AD clear); ANY answers are validated per RRset.
- **Failure caching** (RFC 9520): a `bogus` answer is cached under its key for min(its TTL, 30 s) after a cryptographic failure and 5 s after a lookup failure or an exhausted budget; a chain failure is cached per (route, zone) in the failure cache for the same times. Neither is ever served stale. A lookup-failure `bogus` never replaces a servable (fresh or stale) entry, like a SERVFAIL; a cryptographic `bogus` replaces it. A lookup refused by a local limit (the chain rate limits, the flight cap) says nothing about the zone: that `bogus` answer and the step are cached nowhere.
- **EDE** (RFC 8914) of a `bogus` reply, EDNS clients only, text `<zone>: <failure>` (the zone lower-case without the trailing dot, the root as `.`), at most 128 bytes of valid UTF-8 without control or bidi characters: 7 Signature Expired, 8 Signature Not Yet Valid, 9 DNSKEY Missing (no DNSKEY matches the DS; the DNSKEY lookup failed or was empty), 10 RRSIGs Missing, 11 No Zone Key Bit Set, 12 NSEC Missing (a missing or invalid denial or wildcard proof), 6 DNSSEC Bogus for everything else (a bad signature, a failed DS lookup, a signer that is not the zone, a chain loop, a limit). Failure phrases (fixed English, compared by tests): `signature expired`, `signature not yet valid`, `bad signature`, `no RRSIG`, `no DNSKEY matches the DS`, `DNSKEY lookup failed (<error>)`, `DS lookup failed (<error>)`, `no zone key bit`, `missing or invalid NSEC proof`, `signer <name> is not the zone`, `chain loop`, `validation limit reached`. Reasons of `insecure`: `no DS`, `NSEC3 opt-out`, `unsupported algorithm` (EDE 1), `unsupported digest` (EDE 2), `unsupported key` (EDE 1), `NSEC3 iterations above 50` (EDE 27); of `indeterminate`: `<upstream> returns no DNSSEC data`, `the trust anchors do not match the root zone`, `time checks suspended`, `stale answer`.

**Algorithm.**
- Standards: RFC 4033–4035, 5155 (NSEC3), 6672 (DNAME), 6840, 8624 (algorithms), 9276 (NSEC3 parameters), 9520 (failure caching). **Out of scope for 0.17.0**: RFC 5011 rollover tracking (the anchors change only with releases and are never fetched at runtime), RFC 8198 aggressive use of NSEC/NSEC3 (nothing is synthesised from cached proofs, never with opt-out), negative trust anchors.
- **Trust anchors** (built in, `internal/dns/dnssec/anchors.go`, copied from IANA's `root-anchors.xml`): the DS records (algorithm 8, digest type 2) of KSK-2017 `20326 8 2 E06D44B80B8F1D39A95C0B0D7C65D08458E880409BBC683457104237C7F8EC8D` (validFrom 2017-02-02) and KSK-2024 `38696 8 2 683D2D0ACB8C9B712A1948B27F741219298D0A450D612C483AF444A4C0FB2B16` (validFrom 2024-07-18). A root DNSKEY is trusted only when the SHA-256 digest over its owner name and RDATA (flags included) equals an anchor's digest (never by key tag), its zone-key and SEP flags are set, its REVOKE flag is clear and its algorithm is 8; the root DNSKEY RRset is trusted when an RRSIG of a trusted key over it verifies. KSK-2024 signs the root DNSKEY RRset from 2026-10-11; KSK-2017 gets the REVOKE bit on 2027-01-11 (its changed RDATA then matches no digest, so it is never used). A later rollover needs a PiCache update: first the warning "a new root key is published" (a root SEP key, not revoked, of a supported algorithm in a validated root DNSKEY RRset that matches no anchor, judged only while the RRset's signature is valid now: `newRootKey`; an old key set replayed to a probe does not count), then the probes report `anchor-mismatch`, answers are `indeterminate` and the health check fails.
- **Algorithms**: supported 8 RSASHA256, 10 RSASHA512, 13 ECDSAP256SHA256, 14 ECDSAP384SHA384, 15 ED25519. Unsupported (never `bogus` by themselves): every other algorithm, including 5 RSASHA1, 7 RSASHA1-NSEC3-SHA1 (SHA-1 is not trusted) and 16 ED448; an RSA key with a modulus below 1024 or above 4096 bits or an exponent above 2³¹−1, an ECDSA or Ed25519 key of the wrong length (classified before verifying). **DS digest types**: supported 2 SHA-256 and 4 SHA-384; 1 SHA-1 is never used (ignored next to a SHA-256/384 DS, RFC 4509; alone it counts as unsupported); all others unsupported.
- **A zone's keys** (zone Z with a secure parent P): the DS RRset of Z, verified with P's keys, keeps the records with a supported digest and algorithm; DS present but none kept → Z `insecure` (EDE 2, or 1 when the algorithms are the cause). Otherwise Z's DNSKEY RRset must hold a key that matches one of them (digest over owner and RDATA; algorithm and key tag equal), is supported, has the zone-key flag (else EDE 11) and no REVOKE flag, and whose RRSIG over the DNSKEY RRset verifies; every DS-matched key unsupported → `insecure` (EDE 1); no match → `bogus` (EDE 9). Afterwards every key of the set with the zone-key flag, without REVOKE and supported may sign Z's data; one valid RRSIG of any supported algorithm is enough (RFC 6840 5.11).
- **RRset verification**: an RRSIG counts only when its type covered, class and owner match the RRset; its signer name equals the zone (case-insensitive) and the owner is equal to or below the zone on label boundaries (`dns.IsSubDomain`: `www.devil.com.` is not below `evil.com.`; miekg's `RRSIG.Verify` checks only a string suffix, so the validator checks it first); its labels field is at most the owner's label count (fewer, not counting a leading `*` of the owner, RFC 4035 5.3.2: a wildcard expansion, which needs the no-closer-match proof, else EDE 12); its algorithm and key tag name a usable key of the zone; and, while time checks are active, its validity period (serial-number arithmetic, RFC 4034 3.1.5) contains now with a tolerance of 1 h at both ends (else EDE 7 or 8). The RRset's TTLs are reduced to at most the RRSIG's original TTL and the time left until the earliest RRSIG expiration (RFC 4035 5.3.3).
- **Zone-cut discovery** (a forwarding validator sees no referrals): the zone of a signed RRset is its RRSIG's signer name; RRsets of one answer may have different signers (a CNAME chain across zones) and are validated each in their own zone. The **walk** establishes the key state of a zone S, or proves an insecure cut above it: it starts at the deepest ancestor of S that has a key-cache entry for the route (the root is established from the anchors on first use); an `insecure` ancestor ends the walk (`insecure`); from a secure ancestor Z, for each label L from Z's child down to S, top-down, chain lookup `L DS`: a DS RRset signed by Z → a secure delegation: chain lookup `L DNSKEY`, keys as above, Z := L; NODATA with a valid denial proof from Z whose NSEC/NSEC3 record of L has the NS bit and no DS bit → an insecure delegation, `insecure` (cached for L); an NSEC3 opt-out span covering L → `insecure`; no NS bit (L is no zone cut: a name inside Z or an empty non-terminal) → the next label, Z unchanged; NXDOMAIN with a valid proof → nothing exists below L, the answer must be a signed denial from Z; a CNAME at L (validated with Z's keys) → L is no zone cut, the walk stops with Z; anything else (unsigned, no proof, SERVFAIL, REFUSED, timeout, malformed, over budget) → `bogus`. A chain reply the upstream classified as its own block (default and group routes) makes the client query `blocked-upstream` with that reply's `Info.Block`, never `bogus`. An RRset without RRSIG needs the walk down its owner name until an insecure cut is proven; reaching the owner without one → `bogus` (EDE 10); a negative answer without RRSIGs likewise (EDE 12).
- **Scrub**: before validation the Answer section keeps only the answer to the question, class IN: along the chain from the question name (at most 8 hops) the data of the type (any type for ANY) or the CNAME at each chain name, the closest DNAME above a chain name (with the CNAME it synthesises at the chain name), and their RRSIGs. Everything else (an RRset of another zone or class appended by an upstream or on the path) is removed for every verdict, so it neither changes the verdict nor reaches the client.
- **Answer shapes**: positive answers: every RRset of the scrubbed Answer section; CNAME chains: each CNAME RRset in its zone and the final RRset in its zone; a DNAME RRset is validated and the unsigned CNAME it synthesises is accepted only when it equals the substitution (RFC 6672); a wildcard expansion needs the proof that no closer name exists (NSEC, or NSEC3 covering the next closer name); NXDOMAIN: an NSEC covering the name and one covering the wildcard (`*.` when the closest encloser is the root: a name below a TLD that does not exist), or the NSEC3 closest-encloser proof with the next closer name and the wildcard covered (a next closer name covered by an opt-out span → `insecure`); NODATA: an NSEC/NSEC3 matching the name without the type and without CNAME, the wildcard NODATA proof, or for DS queries an NSEC3 opt-out span. The zone of the denial is the deepest signer of an NSEC, NSEC3 or SOA RRset of the Authority section that is an ancestor of the name at the end of the chain (for DS a proper ancestor); proofs of other zones there (the no-closer-name proof of a wildcard CNAME) belong to their own parts, and without such a signer the name must lie below a proven insecure cut (else EDE 12). The verdict of an answer with several parts is the worst part (`bogus` > `indeterminate` > `insecure` > `secure`). **DNSKEY and DS answers to clients** use the answer itself (no second lookup of the same RRset, no wait on the client's own fetch): a DNSKEY answer at Z is checked against Z's DS from the parent, a DS answer for L against the keys of L's parent zone. Every walk carries a visited set of (zone, type); a repeat → `bogus` (EDE 6, `chain loop`).
- **Sections of a `secure` answer**: RRsets of the Authority and Additional sections that were not validated are removed (a negative answer keeps its validated SOA and proof), so AD covers the whole message. Other answers keep their sections as received.
- Cryptography only through miekg's `RRSIG.Verify`, `DNSKEY.ToDS` and `HashName` (NSEC3 iterations and salt are checked before any hash is computed; `HashName` itself accepts 65 535 iterations) and the standard library. No new dependency.

**Upstream capability and the clock.**
- **Probes** (7.4): `capable` = the DNSKEY answer holds a key matching an anchor, an RRSIG by it over the set verifies, and the SOA answer carries an RRSIG that verifies with a key of that set; `no-dnssec` = a reply without DNSKEY records or without RRSIGs, or signatures that do not verify (`dnssecError`, ≤ 200 bytes, sanitised, says which); `anchor-mismatch` = DNSKEY records and RRSIGs present but no key matches a built-in anchor; `unknown` = no probe answered yet (transport errors, timeouts). Data (an answer or a chain lookup) from a `no-dnssec` or `anchor-mismatch` upstream is `indeterminate`; `unknown` and `capable` upstreams are validated normally (fail closed; the first probes run at the start). Only a probe sets `anchor-mismatch`: during a query, a root DNSKEY RRset without an anchor match from an upstream in any other state is `bogus` (EDE 9) and triggers its re-probe. The answering upstream is known per reply, so a set mixing capable and incapable upstreams (`load_balance`, fallbacks) behaves per reply. A state change logs once (WARN for `no-dnssec` and `anchor-mismatch`, INFO for `capable`).
- **Time checks**: RRSIG validity periods are checked while the clock is trusted. They are **suspended** while (1) the clock guard is active (7.4), (2) the host clock state is readable and not synchronised (`ntp.ReadClock`, read at most once a second; `app` injects it through `upstream.SetClockReader`; an unreadable state, EPERM in a container or a system other than Linux, suspends nothing), or (3) the host clock state cannot be read and the probes of the capable upstreams agree that the root zone's signatures are outside their period now: the last answered probe of at least one of them found an anchored root DNSKEY RRSIG that verifies but whose validity period (with the 1 h tolerance) does not contain now, and none found one that does (the period is kept and judged at the current time, so a corrected clock counts at once; an upstream whose last three probes got no reply no longer counts). Old root signatures replayed to the probe of one plain path therefore never suspend the time checks while another upstream or a synchronised host clock contradicts them. While suspended, a signature that fails only on its dates counts as valid; cryptography, chain and denial are still checked (forged data stays `bogus`), and a verdict that would be `secure` or `insecure` is `indeterminate` (never cached as `secure`). Time checks resume when none of 1–3 held for 60 s; resuming empties the response cache and the key and failure caches, and key states validated while suspended are dropped. So a Raspberry Pi without RTC that boots with an old clock keeps resolving (its NTP server names included) until time synchronisation sets the clock, and a PiCache that is the host's and the LAN's resolver never locks itself out.

**Where validation runs.** Inside the upstream fetch: exchange → duplicate removal → EDE parse → classification (default and group routes) → **validation** (validate mode, a validated route, rcode NOERROR or NXDOMAIN, not classified as a block) → fastest-address order (not for `bogus`) → cache store with the verdict. Validation runs once per fetch, never per hit; the DNS server applies the verdict at 7.1 step 13v.

| Route | Validated | Chain lookups through |
|---|---|---|
| Step 13 default set (`Resolve`), forwarders with the target `default`, `LookupIP`, `POST /dns/lookup`, the fetches of 5a/7/7c/14b that reach the default set | yes | the default route (the default set with its fallbacks; the clock-guard set while it is active) |
| Group sets (`ResolveGroup`, every fetch the default set would make for a group client) | yes | the same group set (or its guard part), never the default set (fail closed) |
| Forwarders with explicit targets and `validate:true` (step 12; also 5a and local CNAME targets routed to them) | yes (`ResolveValidating`) | that forwarder's targets only (they must be recursive resolvers) |
| Forwarders with `validate:false`, `(unqualified)`, the router resolver, `localPtrUpstreams`, everything answered in step 6 (special-use names, the local and reverse zones, forwarders reached there) and 11a (single-label names), steps 7–10 (local records, DHCP lease names, server names, download-cache answers, special domains), client-name PTR lookups (`LookupPTR`), bootstrap resolution, `POST /dns/upstreams/test`, health probes | no (no status; the upstream's AD is discarded) | – |

- **Forwarder flag** `validate:bool` (dns v4 column `dns_forwarders.validate`, default `false`): allowed only on a forwarder with explicit targets whose domains are all outside the locally served zones of step 6 (special-use names, the local domain, `home.arpa`, the search domains, the locally served reverse zones) and not `(unqualified)`: 400 `validate` "only forwarders with explicit targets outside the locally served zones can validate". A validating forwarder reached in step 6 or 11a after a later settings change is not validated there. The flag acts only in validate mode. The target sets of the validating forwarders are registered with the upstream package whenever the forwarders change (`app` wires `dnsserver.Deps.ValidatingForwarders` to `upstream.SetValidatingForwarders`; the sets are pinned outside the 64-set LRU of `ResolveVia`, probed and rebuilt with the bootstrap servers). The import keeps the flag of an updated forwarder (new lines get `false`; an updated line whose new domains or targets break the rule is an error line with `field:"validate"`); the follower sync carries it.
- **CD**: the reply echoes the client's CD bit (every mode). In validate mode CD decides only whether a `bogus` answer is served (CD=1: the data, AD clear) or SERVFAIL (CD=0); the cache is shared, so a CD=1 fetch never serves unvalidated data to a later CD=0 client.
- **AD**: 7.4. Steps that change an answer keep clearing AD (14a, 14b, 14c when it removes records, 7c, 5a, local CNAME answers); the logged `dnssecStatus` stays the verdict of the fetched data (`dnssec` records the AD actually sent); an answer combined from several fetches (7c with its target, DNS64 with its A lookup, a local CNAME with its target) logs the worst verdict of its fetches.
- **Chain lookups and coalescing**: chain steps (DS or DNSKEY of a zone, per route) have their own in-flight map and cap (1024), separate from the client fetches' flights: a chain flight never waits on a client fetch; parent zones are strictly shorter and the visited set breaks loops, so no wait cycle exists. Every wait for a chain flight is bounded by the waiting context's deadline, never by the resolver's lifetime alone; the flight itself runs detached (bounded by its own budget), so its result fills the key cache for the client's retry. The walk of one context is top-down, one chain lookup at a time. Validated states enter the key cache (`secure` with the zone's usable keys, or `insecure` with the reason) for min(the TTLs of the DS/DNSKEY RRsets or of the proof, their RRSIGs' original TTLs, the time until the earliest RRSIG expiration, `dns.cacheMaxTtl` when set, 24 h); `dns.cacheMinTtl` never raises it. The key and failure caches work whether `dns.cacheEnabled` is on or off and are keyed by route, so a split-horizon forwarder's view never affects the default route.
- **LookupIP** (list and cache-domains downloads, the proxy, update checks): the same verdicts; `bogus` → an error (7.4); every other verdict → the addresses.

**Bounds** (a validation context = the validation of one fetched answer, or of one chain step, with its own budget):

| Bound | Value | Beyond it |
|---|---|---|
| Chain lookups per validation context (every step that is not a key-cache hit, coalesced waits included) | 32; one at a time (the walk is top-down) | `bogus`, EDE 6 `validation limit reached` (cached 5 s) |
| New chain exchanges (global) | 50 per second, burst 200 | the lookup waits for its turn within its context's deadline, else it fails at once → `bogus` as a lookup failure, a local refusal (cached nowhere; no per-zone WARN, one WARN per 10 minutes) |
| New chain exchanges per device (a source the DNS rate limit applies to; the device whose query started the fetch: the MAC the neighbour table knows for the source, else its DNS rate-limit key, so all addresses of a device — IPv6 privacy addresses, extra IPv4 aliases — share one) | 25 per second (half the global rate: one device leaves the other half to the rest), burst 200 (a device alone may use the whole global burst: a cold burst of about 200 new zones is not delayed); 4096 devices tracked (least recently used evicted, idle ones forgotten after a minute) | the lookup waits for its turn within its context's deadline like the global rate (a burst is delayed, not refused), else it fails at once → `bogus`, a local refusal (cached nowhere; no per-zone WARN, one WARN per device per 10 minutes naming its source address, never its MAC, with the `dns.rateLimitExempt` hint) |
| Chain flights in progress (global) | 1024 | as above |
| Time of a validation context (after the answer arrived) | 4 s; the client waits at most until its own deadline (10 s query timeout), the validation continues detached until its 4 s are used up | `bogus` as a lookup failure (5 s) |
| RRSIGs considered per RRset | the first 8 | the rest are ignored |
| DNSKEYs tried per RRSIG (same key tag and algorithm) | 4 | the rest are ignored |
| Records in a DNSKEY RRset / a DS RRset | 32 / 8 | `bogus`, EDE 6 `validation limit reached` |
| Signature verifications per validation context | 16, of them at most 2 failed | `bogus`, EDE 6 `validation limit reached` |
| NSEC3 iterations / salt length | 50 / 64 bytes (RFC 9276 3.2) | the proof's zone is `insecure` (EDE 27); no hash is computed |
| NSEC3 hash computations per validation context | 256 | `bogus`, EDE 6 |
| NSEC/NSEC3 records in one proof | 16 | `bogus`, EDE 6 |
| CNAME/DNAME hops validated | 8 (as local CNAMEs) | `bogus`, EDE 6 |
| Concurrent signature verifications (global semaphore) | `GOMAXPROCS` | waits within the context's deadline (cached and local answers never wait) |
| Key cache: validated zone states per (route, zone) | 16 384 entries and 8 MiB (packed wire size of the kept DNSKEY RRsets plus the names), LRU; an RRset above 16 KiB is not kept (validated on each use); `CacheStat.validation` | LRU eviction |
| Failure cache per (route, zone) | 4096 entries, 30 s (cryptographic) / 5 s (lookup, limit); never a local refusal | LRU eviction |
| Probes | 2 queries per upstream per 30 min (30 s while `unknown` or while its probes get no reply), 2 s each, 16 at a time | – |
| BOGUS log lines | 1 WARN per zone per 10 minutes, 1024 zones remembered | not logged |

A LAN client (a hostile web page) can make PiCache spend at most 16 signature verifications and 256 NSEC3 hashes per query of a zone it controls; the per-client rate limit and the semaphore bound the total, and cached and local answers keep flowing (6.1; KeyTrap CVE-2023-50387, NSEC3 CVE-2023-50868).

**Observability.**
- Query log `dnssecStatus` (logs v6), the filter `dnssecStatus`, the CSV column, the top kind `dnssec` and `GET /stats/dnssec` (11); `dnsserver.Stats.dnssec` and `picache_dns_dnssec_total{status}` (in-memory counters since the start, never filtered); `GET /dns/upstreams` (the probe states, the time checks, `newRootKey`, the validating forwarders, `cache.validation`; validate mode only); the Lookup trace (7.1 step 13v).
- **Health check `dnssec`** (validate mode only; the first match wins): fail "the DNSSEC trust anchors of this version do not match the root zone: update PiCache" (an upstream of a validated route is `anchor-mismatch`); warn "DNSSEC time checks are suspended: <the host clock is not synchronised | the system clock is before the build date | the host clock disagrees with the root zone's signatures>"; warn "<upstream> does not return DNSSEC data: its answers are not validated" (the default set, the fallbacks, the clock-guard set while the clock guard is active, or a validating forwarder as "forwarder <domain>: <upstream>"; the first one, then "(and N more)"; group sets are left out, a family preset without DNSSEC data is a deliberate choice); warn "a new root key is published: update PiCache before it is used"; warn "<n> of <m> validated answers in the last hour failed DNSSEC validation" (n ≥ 20 and n > 1 % of m, from in-memory one-minute buckets); ok.
- **Application log**: a `bogus` verdict logs at most one WARN per zone per 10 minutes ("DNSSEC validation failed", `domain` = the zone, hidden while domains are hidden, `reason` = the failure phrase without names, `ede` = the code).
- **Test DNSSEC** (`POST /dns/dnssec/test`, admins, lock class exempt, audited `dns.dnssec.test` with `{passed, failed, inconclusive}`): only on request, in every mode (in `off` and `passthrough` it shows whether `validate` would work). It probes the default set's upstreams now, then, through the default route, for each fixed name an A query with DO=1 and CD=1 that bypasses the response cache (neither read nor written) and is validated locally with the normal chain path (key cache, coalescing, budgets, CD=0 chain lookups), plus a second uncached query with CD=0 (`upstreamRefused`: the upstream's own validator answered SERVFAIL). Names (checked on 2026-09-29; a name that stops behaving is replaced in a release): `example.com` (expect `secure`), `google.com` (`insecure`), `dnssec-failed.org` (`bogus`, EDE 9), `sigfail.ippacket.stream` (`bogus`, a bad signature behind a CNAME). Verdict `pass` when the status equals the expectation, `inconclusive` when it is `indeterminate` or `error` (no reply, SERVFAIL to the CD=1 query, NXDOMAIN), else `fail`. One test at a time (409), one start per 10 s (429), at most 10 s in total (unfinished checks end as `error`). The caller's group upstreams are never used; nothing is written to the query log or the statistics.

---

## 8. Download cache

### 8.1 Services and domain lists

- Source: `uklans/cache-domains` (`cache_domains.json` + every file in `domain_files`), fetched over verified HTTPS through the bypass resolver, refreshed every `downloadCache.updateIntervalHours` (default 24), snapshot in `<data>/cache-domains/` (written through `os.Root`, temp + rename). Offline start uses the last snapshot; without a snapshot, overrides stay inactive and health says why.
- **Untrusted input rules**: `domain_files` names must match `^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._-]{0,63}\.txt$` and are joined with `url.JoinPath`; `cache_domains.json` ≤ 1 MiB and ≤ 128 services; each `.txt` ≤ 4 MiB and ≤ 50 000 lines; service IDs `^[a-z0-9][a-z0-9_-]{0,31}$`; no redirects to other hosts, never private destinations.
- `.txt` parsing: trim, lowercase, skip empty and `#` lines, accept CRLF, strip trailing dot. `*.example.com` = any depth below, **not** the apex; plain = exact. Every pattern (source, custom, extra) passes `ValidatePattern`: valid A-labels, ≥ 2 labels, not `*`, base not a public suffix. Rejected patterns are listed in the source status.
- The download cache is **off by default**. Enabling it in the UI shows the effective cache IP, the store path, its filesystem, free space and warnings (SD card, Docker bridge). All services are enabled by default except `test`. Users can disable services, add custom services and extra hosts.
- `lancache.steamcontent.com`, the hostname Steam uses to discover a download cache, is answered whenever `steam` is enabled.
- Matching is anchored and case-insensitive (exact map + suffix walk).

### 8.2 HTTP cache proxy (:80)

Order per request:
1. **Client ACL** (also enforced at accept) → 403. While `downloadCache.enabled` is false every request except the heartbeat (step 2) gets 403.
2. **Heartbeat** (the path prefill tools and monitoring probe): `GET|HEAD|OPTIONS /lancache-heartbeat` for any Host (incl. IP literals) → `204` with `X-LanCache-Processed-By: <instanceID>` (the response header prefill tools check), `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *`, `Access-Control-Expose-Headers: *` (+ `Access-Control-Allow-Private-Network: true` on OPTIONS). Not logged as a download.
3. **Loop detection**: an incoming processed-by header containing our instance ID → 508.
4. **Host**: lowercase, strip port and trailing dot. IP-literal Host → 400.
5. **Classify** (`Classify(host, ua, path)`): User-Agent ending in `Valve/Steam HTTP Client 1.0` **and** path `^/depot/[0-9]+/` or `/server-status` **and** GET/HEAD → `steam` (Steam sends the real CDN host). Otherwise by host. Unknown → 403 (Steam-UA refusals are counted and shown so the admin can add the host). Known host of a disabled service → pass-through uncached. Residual risk: a client can poison `/depot/…` keys from its own public host; Steam verifies chunk SHA-1, so the effect is a denial of service, not code execution.
6. **Special paths** (pass-through uncached): `/server-status`; `^.+(releaselisting_.*|.version$)`; prefix `/latest64`; `(?i)(authrootstl|pinrulesstl|disallowedcertstl)\.cab$`.
7. **Method**: `GET`/`HEAD` → cache path. Others → pass-through uncached (bounded bodies, no Range injection, client's Accept-Encoding kept).
8. **Canonical path and key**: if `r.URL.EscapedPath()` contains `//`, a `.`/`..` segment, `%2F`/`%2f`, `%5C`, `%00`, `\`, or percent-encodes an unreserved character, the request is passed through and never stored. Otherwise key = `service + "\x00" + keyPath` (no query), where keyPath is the decoded path except that percent-encoded reserved characters (`!$&'()*+,;=:@[]`) and `%` itself stay encoded (upper-case hex), so two different upstream resources never share a key, object ID = first 32 hex chars of SHA-256(key). The upstream request target is the client's escaped form byte for byte (`u := *r.URL; u.Scheme = "http"; u.Host = host`), including the query.
9. **Bypass**: `?nocache=<non-empty, not "0">` from a client in `downloadCache.nocacheClients` → skip cache reads, refetch and overwrite (logged BYPASS). From others it is ignored.
10. **Slicing** (slice size S from the store marker, default 1 MiB): slice `i` covers `[i·S, min((i+1)·S, total))`; fills request `Range: bytes=i·S-(i·S+S-1)` with `Accept-Encoding: identity` (never the client's).
    - Valid slice response: `206` with `Content-Range: bytes a-b/total`, `a == i·S`, `b+1 == min(a+S, total)`, total known and ≤ 1 TiB (larger objects are passed through), no Content-Encoding other than identity.
    - (a) A `200` with Content-Length ≤ S is the complete object: stored as slice 0, not a range failure. (b) A `200` with Content-Length > S, or a mismatching `206`, is a **range failure**: stream it from its actual start, store complete slices only when total is known and the encoding is identity, count one failure for the host. (c) A `200` without Content-Length is served, never stored. (d) A host is marked `noSlice` after 3 range failures on distinct objects within 24 h (persisted, resettable); a later valid 206 resets it.
    - A response with a non-identity Content-Encoding is streamed uncached (PASS).
    - Upstream `416` for slice i>0 of a recorded object → invalidate and abort; for slice 0 → retry once without Range and pass through uncached; never send 416 to a client that sent no Range.
    - A different total than recorded → the store increments the object generation (old slices discarded); a response whose headers were sent with the old length is aborted.
    - **Stored headers**: every upstream header except Content-Length, Content-Range, Content-Encoding, Transfer-Encoding, Accept-Ranges, ETag, Set-Cookie, Age, Date, Expires, Cache-Control, Pragma, Vary, Alt-Svc, Strict-Transport-Security, Connection, Keep-Alive, Trailer, Upgrade, Via, Server-Timing and names starting with X-Cache, CF-, X-Amz-Cf-, X-LanCache- (the processed-by header family), X-Upstream-; values with CR/LF/NUL dropped; ≤ 4 KiB.
11. **Serving**: the handler parses Range itself. With an unknown total and an If-Range, suffix or multi-range request it fetches slice 0 first. It writes 200, 206 or 416 (`Content-Range: bytes */total`); multi-range (≤ 16 ascending, non-overlapping ranges, else 200 full) as `206 multipart/byteranges` with a precomputed Content-Length; If-Range (dates vs stored Last-Modified; any ETag form → 200 full); If-Modified-Since → 304; HEAD without body. Cached slices are written with `SliceReader.WriteRange` to the unwrapped `ResponseWriter` so net/http uses sendfile(2). WriteRange never holds a store I/O slot while writing to the client: it uses sendfile while one of a separate pool of stream slots is free, otherwise it copies 32 KiB chunks, holding the I/O slot only for each file read. Each slice open has a time limit; a slow store makes the request fetch upstream instead. The object is marked in use for the whole request (`Store.Use`), so eviction never removes data being served. Response headers: stored headers, `Accept-Ranges: bytes`, the processed-by header, `X-Upstream-Cache-Status: HIT|MISS|PARTIAL|BYPASS`; never ETag or Set-Cookie. A 60 s write deadline is set before each client write.
12. **Mid-stream failure** of a later slice: retry once, then fetch the remaining bytes of the current range directly (uncached) and keep streaming; abort only if that fails too.
13. **Status handling**: only 200/206 are stored. `301/302/307/308` followed server-side (≤ 5 hops, Range kept, SSRF re-check, `https` with verified TLS); content stored under the original key. `426` → retry once as `https://<host><RequestURI>` (verified TLS, SSRF-guarded), host remembered as https-only for 24 h. `4xx/5xx` passed through unmodified and never stored (403 must pass through for prefill tools). On `404` from one IP, retry once on the next A record.
14. **Fills and collapsing**: in-flight fills live in a `map[sliceKey]*fill` with a sync.Cond-signalled growing buffer; concurrent readers stream from it as it grows (not x/sync/singleflight). Buffers come from a free list of size S. Global slots `min(maxConcurrentFills, 1 GiB / S)`; per client key `maxFillsPerClient` (incl. read-ahead). A demand fill waits ≤ 2 s for a slot, then streams its slice directly uncached (PASS). A slot is released after the buffer was renamed into the store or discarded; store writes wait ≤ 10 s for the NAS I/O semaphore, then the buffer is discarded. Stalled fill (no bytes for 15 s) → a waiting reader may fetch directly. Fills continue after the client disconnects; no new slices are started for a gone client. While the store is full (`StoreFull`), no new fills are stored. Hosts without range support are collapsed too: one request leads and stores each complete slice as a fill that other requests stream from (within 8 slices of the leader; they fetch on their own if the leader stalls for 15 s or stops capturing). Late requests follow the leader as well, also one that finds the object already recorded: the leader is announced before the first fill leaves the fill table, and while an object has a leader no request starts a fill that fetches it (demand, read-ahead or whole-object fill; decided atomically with the fill table insert).
15. **Read-ahead**: `readAheadSlices` (default 2) following uncached slices, started only after the client consumed one full slice of the current range, `TryAcquire` only; none for an object with a leader (step 14).
16. **Upstream transport**: HTTP/1.1 keep-alive (`MaxIdleConnsPerHost` 32, `IdleConnTimeout` 90 s, no env proxy, `DisableCompression`), `netutil.SafeDialer` over `LookupIP` (IPv4), connect 10 s, response header 15 s, idle read 60 s. Forward end-to-end client headers except hop-by-hop, `Range`/`If-*`, `Cookie`, `Accept-Encoding` (fills); add the processed-by header. No `X-Forwarded-For`.
17. **Accounting**: bytes served from cache vs fetched upstream per slice, bytes stored; one cache event per client request; live transfers and aggregated active downloads (per client + content, 10 s rate window).

### 8.3 SNI pass-through (:443)

Accept (ACL + caps at accept) → read the ClientHello: 5-byte record header (type 0x16, length ≤ 16384), then the full record, reading further 0x16 records until the handshake message is complete (16 KiB, 5 s) → extract SNI without terminating TLS → must match an enabled service (or the Steam trigger) → `LookupIP` → SSRF guard → dial :443 (10 s) → replay the buffered bytes → relay with `io.CopyN(dst, src, 4 MiB)` loops on the raw `*net.TCPConn` pair (splice), refreshing a 5 min idle deadline per chunk; max lifetime 24 h. No SNI or not allowed → close. One event per connection (client, SNI, service, bytes, duration). The UI flags services whose traffic is mostly pass-through (HTTPS bypass, e.g. Epic launcher ≥ 20.0.3).

### 8.4 Content grouping ("what was downloaded")

| Service | Rule | Group key | Label |
|---|---|---|---|
| steam | path `^/depot/(\d+)/(?:chunk/[0-9a-fA-F]{40}|manifest/(\d+)/\d+(?:/\d+)?|[^/]+/)` | `steam:depot:<depot>` | user label → "Steam depot <depot>" |
| blizzard | path `^/(tpr|cortez)/([^/]+)/(config|data|patch)/` | `blizzard:<lower(cdnpath)>` (`configs` → shared) | built-in product map (wow, ovw, fenris, diablo3, hs, sc2, …) |
| epicgames | `^/Builds/Org/(o-[a-z0-9]+)/([0-9a-f]{32})/` or `^/Builds/(.+?)/CloudDir/` | `epic:<org>/<build>` or `epic:<app>` | user label → "Epic item <build[:8]>" / humanised app |
| riot | host `^([a-z0-9-]+)\.(?:secure\.)?dyn\.riotcdn\.net$` | `riot:<prod>` | lol → League of Legends, valorant → VALORANT, ks-foundation → Riot Client |
| xboxlive / wsus | package file name `(.+)_(\d+(?:\.\d+){3})_(x64|x86|arm64|neutral)__([a-z0-9]{13})\.(msixvc|xvc|appx|appxbundle|msix|msixbundle|eappx|eappxbundle)$`; KB `(?i)(?:^|[-_/])kb(\d{6,8})(?:[-_.]|$)`; DO `^/filestreamingservice/files/<guid>`; Office `^/pr/<guid>/Office/Data/<ver>/` | `xbox:<pkg>`, `win:kb<n>`, `win:do`, `win:office:<channel>` | humanised |
| sony | `/((?:CUSA|PPSA)\d{5})_00/` | `psn:<title>` | title id |
| nintendo | `^/c/([csa])/([0-9a-f]{32})` | `nintendo:switch` | "Nintendo eShop content" |
| uplay / origin | `^/uplaypc/downloads/([^/]+)/`, `^/eamaster/s/shift/([^/]+)/` | `ubi:<g>`, `ea:<g>` | humanised |
| wargaming | host `^dl-(wot|wows|wowp)-` | `wg:<g>` | World of Tanks/Warships/Warplanes |
| fallback | — | `<service>:<host>` | "<Service> · <host>" |

Query strings are never stored or displayed. Users can set labels for any group key (e.g. name a Steam depot).

---

## 9. Cache store

### 9.1 On-disk format (per store root)

```
<root>/.picache-store          JSON {"storeId","format":1,"sliceSize","createdAt"} (≤ 4 KiB, validated)
<root>/tmp/                    temp files (same filesystem → atomic rename)
<root>/slices/<h0h1>/<h2h3>/<objectId>.<sliceIndex>
```
Slice file = magic `PCS1` + header length (uint32 LE, ≤ 64 KiB) + JSON header `{"o","i","s","h","p","t","z","c","k","m"}` (object id, index, service, host, path, total, slice size, created ms, **crc32c of data**, headers) + data.
