| `backup.failed`, `backup.succeeded` | error, info | the backup scheduler after each run (scheduled, catch-up or "run now") |
| `security.lockout` | warning | `auth` throttling (`OnLockout`): a new lockout of a client, a device or a session's password confirmations, or the start of a username delay; once per lockout; names the client key, never the username or the session |
| `notify.test` | info | the test button |

**Warning history.** Every event the app emits goes through one `app.emit`, which records it in the warning history and then calls `Emit`, independent of channels: exactly the events of the table above (`health.*` at the health watch's debounced moments), never `notify.test` or `notify.dropped`. Table `logs_events` (`logs.db`): an unacknowledged entry with the same event and title is updated (`count` + 1, last time, newest message) instead of adding one; at most 200 entries per area (`health`, `storage`, `update`, `backup`, `security`; the oldest last time dropped first), entries whose last time is older than 90 days removed; never size-trimmed, never cleared with the statistics. While `logs.db` is disabled an in-memory ring of 200 entries with the same rules (lost at restart). Writes are small direct transactions (5 s timeout, failures logged rate-limited). `GET /system/events` lists it newest first (principals without admin scope never see `security.*` entries or `acknowledgedBy`); `POST /system/events/{id}/ack` and `/ack-all` acknowledge (admins); `/system/overview` `events.unacknowledged` counts the unacknowledged warnings and errors the caller may see from memory.

**Messages** are English, a title (one line, ≤ 200 characters) and a message (≤ 2000 characters, line breaks kept), with control characters removed. They may contain storage and check names, versions and client addresses for security events, never secrets, passwords, tokens, session data or usernames.

### 15.2 Scheduled backups

**Settings** (section `backups`): `enabled` (false), `schedule` (`daily`|`weekly`), `time` (`HH:MM`, 00:00–23:59, local time of the host; `03:30`), `weekday` (0 = Sunday … 6, weekly only; 0), `keep` (1–90; 7), `destination` (`local` or a storage target id; `local`), `includeSecrets` (false). The API refuses a changed destination that is not an existing storage target, and a storage target that is the destination cannot be deleted (409).

**Destination.** `local` = `<data>/backups/scheduled/` (the pre-upgrade copies in `<data>/backups/` are not affected). A storage target = `<its store root>/picache-backups/`; the target must be online (mount guard; this implies an initialised store), otherwise the run fails with the target's reason. The built-in cache target (`local`) cannot be chosen: `local` means the data directory. The directory is created with 0750; a symbolic link in its place is refused, and it is used as an `os.Root` that must be the directory checked before opening.

**Run.** File name `picache-backup-<instanceId>-<YYYYMMDDTHHMMSSZ>.db` (UTC start time; the instance id of `<data>/instance-id` with characters outside `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` replaced). Content = `App.Backup` (the UI backup: no accounts, sealed secrets only with `includeSecrets`). Written as `<name>.tmp` (`O_CREATE|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW`, 0640), synced, renamed; leftover `.tmp` files of this installation are removed first. Retention after a successful run: this installation's backups in the destination (regular files whose names match the pattern exactly), newest first by the time in the name; the ones beyond `keep` are deleted. No other file is ever touched. One run at a time; `POST /system/backups/scheduled/run` uses the same code path (409 while a run is going). The result `{time, ok, error?, file?, sizeBytes?, destination}` is kept in memory and in app_meta `backups.last` (with the time of the last success) and sent as `backup.succeeded`/`backup.failed`.

**Timing.** The scheduler checks every minute. For each local date (weekly: each date with the weekday) the run time is `time.Date(date, HH:MM, time.Local)`; a run starts when a run time lies between the previous check and now, so every run time is due exactly once, also when the clock is set back. When the clocks go forward and the time does not exist, it is moved forward by the gap (02:30 → 03:30); when they go back and it exists twice, `time.Date` picks one of the two. Catch-up: when scheduled backups are enabled at start and the last successful run is older than the interval (24 h or 7 days) + 1 h, or there was none, a run is made 5 minutes after the start (so the mount guard has checked the NAS), unless a run succeeded meanwhile. At shutdown a run is cancelled and waited for at most 3 s.

**Secrets.** A backup without `includeSecrets` has the sealed columns cleared and every `settings_secrets` row deleted (`scrubBackup`, the sync token and the proxy password too); with it they stay sealed with the master key, which is never part of a backup.

**Files.** The overview lists this installation's backups in the current destination (newest first, ≤ 10 000 directory entries read, 5 s; one listing at a time, a hung NAS is reported in `filesError`). Download and delete accept only names that match the pattern of this installation, regular files in the current destination; downloads are opened with `O_NOFOLLOW`.

---

### 15.3 Partial restore

`POST /system/restore?sections=a,b` and `picache restore <file> [--sections a,b] [--force]` restore selected sections (absent = everything, as before). The selection travels inside the staged file (app_meta `restore.sections`; `restore.by = "cli"` for the CLI, audited as `system.restore` with username `cli` by the start that applies it; any such key in an upload is overwritten) and applies at the next start. Every `picache.db` table is in exactly one section (`internal/app/restore.go`; a test fails for a table of `MigrateConfigDB` missing from the map):

| Section | Tables | Settings members | Restore | Export / sync |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `settings` | `settings`, `settings_secrets` | all except `dhcp`, `cache.activeStoreId`, `backups.destination` | yes | no (sync uses `dns-settings`) |
| `clients-and-groups` | `client_groups`, `client_clients`, `client_identifiers`, `client_memberships` | – | yes | yes |
| `lists-and-rules` | `filter_lists`, `filter_list_groups`, `filter_rules`, `filter_rule_groups`, `filter_ip_rules`, `filter_ip_rule_groups` | – | yes | yes |
| `local-dns` | `dns_records`, `dns_record_groups`, `dns_forwarders`, `dns_forwarder_domains` | – | yes | yes |
| `parental` | `parental_groups` | – | yes | yes (configuration only, never overrides and pauses) |
| `dhcp` | `dhcp_static`, `dhcp_leases` | `dhcp` | yes | never |
| `download-cache` | `services_custom`, `services_extra_domains`, `services_labels` | – | yes | never |
| `notifications` | `notify_channels` | – | yes | never |
| `storage` | `storage_targets` | `cache.activeStoreId`, `backups.destination` | yes | never |
| `dns-settings` | – | the syncable members of `dns` and `filter` (15.4) | no (part of `settings`) | yes |
| never (kept live) | `auth_users`, `auth_sessions`, `auth_tokens`, `auth_audit`, `app_meta`, `schema_migrations`, `proxy_noslice_hosts` | – | – | never |

Group links are the only references between sections. **Dependency rule** (restore and sync): `clients-and-groups` requires `lists-and-rules`, `local-dns` and `parental`; a group-linked section without it keeps the live groups and maps its links by group name (case-insensitive; Default always 1 → 1), and a link to a name that does not exist live refuses the whole restore (400 `sections`). At start (`applyStagedRestore`, before any component opens `picache.db`): the staged file is validated as for a full restore, a copy of it migrated (`MigrateConfigDB`), a `VACUUM INTO` copy of the live database gets, in one transaction with foreign keys on, per section its parent rows deleted and the backup's rows inserted by column name (links remapped), the section's settings members merged (for `settings` the backup's document except `dhcp` and the storage members, a member it lacks, as in an older version's backup, taking its default as after a full restore (the members the settings migrations derive are already in the migrated copy: `updates.channel` from `includePrereleases`, `dns.dnssecMode` from `dns.dnssec`, so a 0.16 backup gets `off` or `passthrough`, never `validate`; the checks of the upload decode the backup's document the same way with `settings.DecodeStored`), plus `settings_secrets` replaced as a whole, so a live member never outlives its secret), then `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` and the storage references (`cache.activeStoreId`, `backups.destination`: `local` or a target of the result); then it replaces `picache.db` like a full restore (`picache.db.before-restore` kept). Any failure: the staged file becomes `picache.db.failed-restore-<ts>` and the live database stays. The upload and the CLI run the same merge as a dry run, so their errors come at once. Partial restores keep sessions; `webAccessWarning` and `dnsWarning` are computed only when `settings` is selected. A restore on a follower is allowed and makes it forget the last applied export (the next sync applies the primary's configuration again).

### 15.4 Follower sync (`internal/app/sync.go`)

A pull-based, read-only follower; it never pushes. **Primary**: `GET /system/export?sections=…` (permission X; the five exportable sections of 15.3; JSON `api.ConfigExport` with `formatVersion` 1, the primary's version and component schema versions, `contentSha256` = the SHA-256 of the deterministic JSON of the sections, the group name table), built in memory (≤ 64 MiB, one at a time), audited as `system.export` (for a sync token only when the content changed since the last export audited for it). **Follower**: settings section `sync` (`mode`, `source` = `https://host[:port]`, the sealed `token`, `caPem` = 1–4 PEM trust anchors or the system roots, `intervalMinutes` 5–1440, `sections` with the dependency rule). Transport: https only, TLS 1.2+, the chain and host name verified against `caPem` or the system roots (a leaf re-issued by the same CA keeps working), the token written only after the verified handshake, no redirects, no proxy, the host resolved by the host resolver and link-local, multicast, unspecified, loopback and this machine's addresses refused afterwards (private addresses allowed), 30 s, ≤ 64 MiB, strict decoding. Runs: 1 minute after the start and 1 minute after the mode, source, sections, trust anchor or token changed (a replaced token leaves the document as it is: the loop compares a fingerprint of the stored token), then every `intervalMinutes` (±10 %), plus `POST /system/sync/run` (409 "sync is off" / "a sync is running", 429 within 30 s of a start); one at a time. An export of an unknown format version or a component schema newer than this binary's is refused ("the primary runs PiCache <v> with a newer configuration schema: update this follower"); an export whose `contentSha256`, source and sections equal the last applied one (app_meta `sync.state`) applies nothing. **Apply**: everything is validated first with the API's validators and limits (identifiers, the group upstream rules and bounds against the resulting DNS settings, lists and rule caps, the IP rule cap, 10 000 records and CNAME loops, 256 forwarders and their domain rules, parental schedules and services, the `dns.blockedClients` safety net, a public custom ECS subnet); then one `BEGIN IMMEDIATE` transaction in which `clients`, `filter`, `dnsserver` and `parental` replace their tables through `…ReplaceSynced(ctx, tx, …)` (rows and IDs of the primary; with `clients-and-groups` all four group-linked sections, so the IDs stay consistent; otherwise mapped by group name, an unknown name fails the run; parental overrides and pauses of groups whose name stays are kept); commit; then the syncable settings through `settings.Store.Update` (only those members); then the reloads (clients, matcher and lists: a list keeps its downloaded copy while its URL, kind and format stay, new and changed lists are fetched, removed ones deleted; records and forwarders; parental controls; group upstream sets). An error before the commit keeps nothing of the run; an error of the settings or of a reload after the commit fails the run too, so its state is not stored and the next run applies the export again (the components never stay on the previous configuration while the tables hold the new one). Every failed run sets `lastError` (never the token; the source as its origin). Audited per apply as `sync.applied` (username `sync`, target the origin, `{sections, contentSha256, primaryVersion}`); the configuration lock does not apply (internal update). **Syncable settings** (`dns-settings`): every member of `dns` and `filter` except `dns.serverNames`, `dns.serverNameAddresses`, `dns.encrypted`, `dns.plainDns`, `dns.allowedNetworks`, `dns.allowAllNetworks`, `dns.trustConnectedNetworks`, `dns.routerResolver`, `filter.enabled` and `filter.pausedUntil`; `internal/settings` classifies every leaf (a test fails for an unclassified one). `dns.dnssecMode` and its alias `dns.dnssec` are both synced: a 0.16 primary sends only `dns.dnssec`, which `reconcileDNSSEC` maps (`true` keeps a follower's `passthrough` or `validate`, else sets `passthrough`; `false` sets `off`); a 0.17 primary sends both, consistent, and the mode wins. The forwarders' `validate` flag travels with `local-dns` (a 0.16 primary's forwarders get `false`). Upstreams, fallbacks, local PTR upstreams (`settings.ApplySyncable`), group upstreams and forwarder targets (`legacySyncedUpstreams`) that a primary before 1.0.0 sends with text after `#` that this version refuses are read with that version's meaning, like stored ones (`settings.LegacyUpstream`, 7.4), and logged once the run is applied ("the primary sent an upstream with text after "#" …"). Any other validation error of a section fails the whole run, the other sections included: a run applies everything or nothing. Status `GET /system/sync`; the health check `sync` (while the mode is `follower`) warns while no sync token is stored (a backup restored without secrets: the settings refuse a follower without a token only when a write changes the sync section, so other writes keep working), while the last run failed ("sync from <origin> failed: <error>") or no run succeeded for 3 intervals.

---

## 16. Parental controls (`internal/dns/parental`)

**Model** (table `parental_groups`, component `parental`; at most one row per client group, deleted with the group through `ON DELETE CASCADE`; a group without a row has no restrictions). Per group:

- `blockedServices`: ids of the service catalogue that are always blocked (≤ 256).
- `schedules` (≤ 10): `id` (8 hex, assigned by the server, kept on update), `name` (1–40 characters, no control characters), `enabled`, `days` (0 = Sunday … 6, sorted, unique), `start` and `end` (`HH:MM`, `settings.ParseClock`; `end` ≠ `start`, `end` before `start` = until `end` the next day), `block` `all` (all internet) or `services` (1–256 catalogue ids).
- `safeSearch` `{google, youtube: off|moderate|strict, bing, duckduckgo, ecosia, yandex, pixabay}` (default all off; a stored configuration without it, or with an unknown YouTube level, loads as off): the engines whose restricted mode the group enforces (7.1 step 7c). Stored in the configuration JSON with the services and schedules.
- One override (`override_mode`, `override_until`): `block` = all internet blocked until the time, `allow` = the group's blocked services and schedules lifted until the time; at most 7 days ahead. An expired override is ignored and removed with the next write.
- One **pause** of the group's filtering (`pause_until`, parental migration 2): until the time (at most 7 days ahead) the group's lists and user rules stop applying for its clients — the protection lists excepted, the group's security lists included; the client's other groups still apply. It is independent of the override (both can be active at once) and ends by itself; an elapsed pause is ignored and removed with the next write. Parental controls, safe search and protection lists stay in force (7.1: all groups versus filtering groups).

The **category switches** (`adult`, `gambling`, `dating`, `piracy`, `bypass`) are not stored here: each binds catalogue lists (`categoryPresets` in the filter package: `oisd-nsfw`, `hagezi-gambling-medium`, `shadowwhisperer-dating`, `hagezi-anti-piracy`, `hagezi-doh-vpn-bypass`), and a switch is `on` when every bound key has a list that is enabled and assigned to the group (a list whose `catalogKey`, or an alias of it, is the key); `state` is `off`, `failed` (a bound list is `failed-empty`), `pending` (a bound list has no copy yet) or `active`. Switching on creates a missing list from the catalogue entry (enabled, only this group; never Default) or adds the group to an existing one and enables it (also for its other groups); a list found by the entry's URL or given another category gets the entry's catalogue key and category back; a bound URL that holds an allowlist or the list limit (100) → 409. Switching off removes the group from the bound lists, which stay. A bound list counts for `on` only while it is a blocklist of the switch's category, so a switch never reports a list that is not enforced at step 7a: a bound list recategorised to, e.g., `security` (it then pauses with blocking) or made an allowlist leaves the switch `off` for its groups. `PUT /parental/groups/{id}` applies the switches first (`filter.Engine.SetPresets`, one transaction with the headroom check; memory is updated from that transaction's rows, and the bound lists are brought in line with the database even when nothing changed), audits the created and changed lists at once, then saves the parental configuration; if that fails, `filter.Engine.RevertPresets` undoes the list changes exactly (lists the request created are deleted, changed lists get back their enabled flag, category, catalogue key and membership of the group) and the undo is audited. List writes (create, update, delete, switches) are serialised with their in-memory update. The API fills `GroupControls.categories` from the filter lists (parental never imports filter). The bound lists are protection lists (7.2) while they keep the switch's category: they apply like parental controls, always, whatever the blocking switch says, and are not schedulable.

Only enabled groups apply (identities list enabled groups only). A stored configuration is sanitised when it is read (catalogue ids that left the catalogue and malformed schedules of an edited database are dropped), never rejected.

**Content protection versus lift**: the `allow` override ("lift restrictions") lifts only the group's blocked services and schedules. Safe search and protection lists stay on; they end only by switching them off, disabling the group or removing the client from the group. A user allow rule lifts a protection-list block (like every 7a block) but never safe search.

**Family resolvers** (7.4 "Group sets"): a group's upstream preset or own upstreams are content protection like safe search: neither a global disable or pause of blocking nor the group's pause or `allow` override switches them off; disabling the group or removing the client from it does. A client in several groups gets one resolver (a preset wins, then the lowest group id); the UI warns when a client's groups name different resolvers.

**Service catalogue** (`services.go`, 144 services): a plain Go literal `{id, name, category, domains}`, sorted by category (`video`, `social`, `messaging`, `gaming`, `music`, `ai`, `dating`, `gambling`, `shopping`, `privacy` — VPN and proxy apps —, `software` — app stores only, never update channels —, `hosting` — file sharing and cloud storage —, `news`) and then by name, updated with releases and never downloaded. Domains are subtree matches (the domain and its subdomains). Only well-known, service-specific domains are listed, never shared infrastructure (`googleapis.com`, `akamaihd.net`, `cloudfront.net`, `fastly.net`, `media-amazon.com`, …) nor names whose blocking breaks unrelated things or security: OS and security updates, certificates/OCSP/CRL, time, captive-portal and connectivity checks, identity providers, and vendor domains that also serve other products (only service-specific names below them, e.g. `music.apple.com`, `www.amazon.de`), so blocking one service does not break others; a specific name below a shared domain is fine (`youtubei.googleapis.com`). Every domain was checked in DNS for the release. **Released ids are frozen** and their domain sets only grow (a domain leaves only when it is dead or turned out to be shared, named in the CHANGELOG; a rename would need an alias map applied when configurations are read and validated). A test checks slugs, categories, the order, valid A-labels, public suffixes (`x/net/publicsuffix`, ICANN and private, plus an explicit list of hosting suffixes), duplicates and nesting across services without exceptions, the shared names (no catalogue domain equals one or is a parent of one), the frozen ids and that blocking every service validates and round-trips.

**Safe search** (`safesearch.go`, 7.1 step 7c): a table written from the vendors' documentation (each engine cites its source) maps exact names to engines; `Engine.SafeSearch(qname, groups, now)` returns the target, the label and the group. The groups with any safe search setting are part of the snapshot. A name outside the table costs one map lookup; nothing allocates. Safe search is DNS-based: apps with their own DNS or a VPN get around it.

**Evaluation** (`Engine.Check(qname, groupIDs, now)`, 7.1 step 7a). The groups are taken in the identity's order and the first group that blocks decides (deterministic); within a group:

1. an active `allow` override → the group contributes nothing;
2. an active `block` override → blocked, kind `override` (status `blocked-schedule`);
3. an enabled block-all schedule active now → kind `schedule` (status `blocked-schedule`);
4. the qname's service is always blocked, or listed in an enabled service schedule active now → kind `service` (status `blocked-service`).

The service of a name is found by walking the name and its parent suffixes (at most 127 labels) through a map of all catalogue domains. A client in several groups gets the union of their restrictions; an `allow` override lifts only its own group. Parental controls do not depend on `filter.enabled`, the global pause or the group's pause: they end only with the group's `allow` override, by disabling the schedule or the group, or by removing the client from the group. A user allow rule that applies to the client lifts a block (7.1 step 7a). Reasons (query log, EDE 15): `Kids: Bedtime`, `Kids: blocked by hand`, `Kids: YouTube`, `Kids: YouTube (Homework time)`.

**Time.** Schedules follow the host's wall clock (`time.Local`, as scheduled backups; a Docker container uses UTC unless `TZ` is set). A window starts on each listed day at `start` and ends at `end` the same day, or the next day when `end` is before `start` (Friday 21:00–07:00 ends Saturday 07:00). Activity is decided on the wall clock (weekday, minute of the day): when the clocks go forward, a window that starts in the skipped hour starts at the first valid instant (02:30 → 03:00); when they go back, the repeated hour counts as the clock shows it. Reported instants (`until`, `next`) are `time.Date` in the location with the same rule for the skipped hour.

**Hot path.** `Check` reads an immutable snapshot (`atomic.Pointer`) of the groups that have a restriction (blocked services, an enabled schedule or a block override), the groups with a safe search setting and the pauses (group → until), rebuilt on every write and on `clients.Registry.OnChange` (renames, deletions). If none of the client's groups is in it, `Check` returns without reading the time zone; it never allocates. Cost: O(groups of the client × schedules) plus one suffix walk. `FilterGroups(groups, now)` returns its input slice itself (no allocation) when none of the groups is paused, else a new slice without the paused ones. The `now` of a query is its start time.

**State** (API, computed per request): `blockAll` with `reason` (`override`/`schedule`), the active block-all `schedule` and `until` (block-all windows that follow each other, and a block override that ends inside one, count as one block, at most 7 days); `blockedServices` blocked now (always blocked plus active service schedules; empty while lifted); `lifted`/`liftedUntil` for an `allow` override; `next` = the next start or end of an enabled schedule within 7 days (an end before a start at the same time); `paused`/`pausedUntil` for a pause of the group's filtering (independent of `blockAll` and `lifted`); `timeZone`/`utcOffsetMinutes` of the host.

**Limits.** Parental controls are DNS-based. A device that asks another resolver (hard-coded DNS servers, encrypted DNS in apps or browsers, VPN apps, mobile data) bypasses them (safe search and the category switches too); the category switch `bypass` (the list "HaGeZi DoH/VPN/TOR/Proxy Bypass") for the group and blocking outgoing DNS (53, 853) to other servers on the router reduce that. Blocking starts when an app looks a name up again (clients cache answers), and open connections continue until they reconnect. Devices must be identified as clients (IP or MAC; phones use a private MAC per Wi-Fi network, which stays fixed for that network when it is set to "fixed").

---

## 17. Network check (`internal/app/netcheck.go`)

Answers "do all devices use PiCache?" and explains the two router set-ups that bypass it: the router hands out itself as DNS server and forwards to PiCache (every query comes from the router), and the router announces itself as IPv6 DNS server (devices ask the router over IPv6).

**Sources** (read-only, unprivileged; nothing leaves the host):

- The kernel's neighbour table, read fresh for every check (`clients.Registry.Neighbours`: netlink `RTM_GETNEIGH`, IPv4 and IPv6; `/proc/net/arp` if netlink fails): entries with a link-layer address in state REACHABLE, STALE, DELAY, PROBE or PERMANENT (never INCOMPLETE or FAILED), without all-zero, broadcast and multicast MACs, at most 65 536.
- Queries per address in the last 24 h: `logs.ClientStats` (the statistics behind `GET /stats/clients?range=24h`); the last activity per address and MAC from `clients.Known` (30 days). While logs.db is unavailable or client addresses are anonymised, only `Known` is used (queries of addresses seen within 24 h) and the response says `statsAvailable: false`. Loopback and this machine's addresses are not counted.
- The router: the IPv4 default gateway (`/proc/net/route`) and the IPv6 default gateway (`/proc/net/ipv6_route`, the default route with the lowest metric), the IPv4 gateway's MAC from the neighbour table (else the IPv6 gateway's) and every neighbour address with that MAC; a gateway that is this machine is ignored. Kind: `fritzbox` if the gateway's PTR name (router resolver or local PTR upstreams, cached 10 minutes, 2 s timeout) or the effective local domain is `fritz.box` or below it, `generic` otherwise, `unknown` without a gateway.
- This machine's interface addresses (`dnsserver.Server.HostNetwork`: no loopback, no virtual bridges or tunnels as in 7.1 step 9), classified IPv4, ULA, global, link-local; whether a DNS listener serves IPv6 (bound to `::` or an IPv6 address). `self` lists the addresses of the interfaces of the IPv4 and the IPv6 default route (the lowest metric each, the route snapshot of 6.1) first, then the others by value, so its first IPv4 address and ULA are the ones LAN devices use (0.16.0: the router steps, the checklist and the device guides name them); Tailscale's addresses (`100.64.0.0/10`, `fd7a:115c:a1e0::/48`) are left out of `self` unless they are on such an interface (LAN devices cannot reach them; they still count as this machine's own).
- Refused sources (`dnsserver.Server.RefusedSources`): the ACL drop path (7.1 step 2: the UDP reader decorator and the handler) counts each source address in memory, at most 256 (least recently refused evicted), full IPv6 addresses, loopback excluded, since the start; nothing is persisted or logged per packet. Queries dropped because of `dns.blockedClients` (7.1 step 2a) are not counted here.
- Mode `bridge` when PiCache runs in a container bridge network (as detected for the cache IP): the neighbour data then shows only the bridge, the router is not visible (`kind` `unknown`, or `fritzbox` by the local domain) and the bridge gateway takes the router's place in the forwarding check.

**Checks** (the server computes status and numbers, the UI writes the texts):

| id | ok / info / warn | data |
|---|---|---|
| `router-forwarding` (`container-nat` in bridge mode, with the bridge gateway as router) | with at least 200 queries in 24 h: warn if ≥ 80 % come from router addresses, info from 20 %; ok otherwise | `routerQueries, totalQueries, share (0–1), routerAddresses[]` (most queries first) |
| `ipv6-dns` | ok if the LAN has no IPv6 (no non-link-local IPv6 neighbour and no ULA/global address of this machine) or LAN devices (not the router) asked over IPv6 in 24 h; warn otherwise | `lanHasIPv6, ipv6Queries, ipv6Clients, ula[], global[], routerRdnss[]?` (the DNS servers the IPv6 default router announces in its router advertisements: RAs with router lifetime > 0 whose source is one of the router's addresses; present only while PiCache records router advertisements, i.e. while its own are on (18.5); `[]` when the router announces none), `hostIgnoresRA` (true when this machine has no ULA or global address and the kernel ignores router advertisements on its default-route interface, or on every interface that is up and neither loopback nor virtual without a default route: Linux `net.ipv6.conf.<if>.accept_ra` 0, or 1 while `forwarding` is on; the UI then explains that PiCache cannot tell whether the network uses IPv6) |
| `ipv6-address` | ok without IPv6 in the LAN or with a ULA of this machine; info with global addresses only (they change with the prefix); warn without any | `ula[], global[]` |
| `refused` | warn if the ACL refused sources since the start (e.g. global IPv6 addresses outside `dns.allowedNetworks`), else ok | `sources[]{address, count, last, onLink}` (newest first, ≤ 20; `onLink`: inside a network this machine is connected to, i.e. one `dns.trustConnectedNetworks` would allow), `since`, `trustConnectedNetworks` (the setting; the UI offers to turn it on for refused on-link sources, and the /64 of other sources for `dns.allowedNetworks`) |
| `devices` (not in bridge mode) | info if devices of the neighbour table (router and this machine excluded) did not query in 24 h, ok otherwise | `total, active, inactive, never` |

**Interfaces** (`GET /network/interfaces`, the panel on DNS → Network check): per interface (loopback excluded, at most 64) name, index, MAC, up, operational state, MTU, speed and duplex when known (a speed of −1 or an unreadable file is omitted), addresses, the non-default routes through it (`networks`, the route snapshot of 6.1, ≤ 64), `virtual` (below `/sys/devices/virtual`), rx/tx bytes and errors and the default gateways that use it; read by `internal/hostinfo` below its injectable root plus `netutil`; `[]` on systems other than Linux; the container's own interfaces with `mode:"bridge"`.

**Devices**: neighbour entries grouped by MAC (the router's and this machine's MACs excluded), each with the vendor of its MAC (`oui`) or `macRandomized` for a locally administered address, addresses in the order IPv4, ULA, global, link-local; `name` = the configured client (by MAC or IP, learned MACs included), else the PTR name of one of its addresses, else of another address with the same MAC (7.1 step 5; each check queues PTR lookups, through the same de-duplicated, bounded queue as for querying clients, for neighbour addresses without a name younger than an hour, link-local excluded; names appear with a later check); `lastQuery` = the latest activity of its addresses and its MAC; `queries24h` = the sum over its addresses; `status` `active` (a query within 24 h), `inactive` (older) or `never`. Sorted never, inactive, active, then by address; at most 1024. None in bridge mode.

**This machine and the requester** (0.16.0). `self.dynamic4`: an IPv4 address of the interface of the IPv4 default route with the lowest metric (the route snapshot of 6.1) has a finite valid lifetime (`netutil.HostAddrs` `Dynamic`, netlink `IFA_CACHEINFO`), i.e. a DHCP client configured it, whatever PiCache's own DHCP server does; absent in bridge mode, on systems other than Linux and without an IPv4 default route (or an IPv4 address on its interface). `requester` describes the effective client of the request (2): `local` (loopback or one of this machine's addresses), the `mac`, `name` and `clientId` of the device whose addresses contain it, and its `queries24h` and `lastQuery` as the device list shows them for that device (every address of its MAC: a dual-stack device may open the web UI over IPv4 and ask over IPv6), without a device from the activity of the address (nothing new is recorded); while `logs.anonymizeClientIps` is on `privacy:true` and no counts. It is computed for every request from the cached check's state (the device list, this machine's addresses and the activity per address), never cached itself.

**Caching.** A check is computed at most every 30 s (every 2 s while a scan runs), one computation at a time; starting or finishing a scan invalidates it. The health check `network` (every 60 s) uses the same cache: `warn` while `router-forwarding` or `container-nat` warns ("Most DNS queries come from the router (<ip>): PiCache cannot tell devices apart"), else `ok`.

**Discovery scan** (`POST /network/scan`, admin, audited `network.scan`): one empty UDP datagram to port 9 (discard) of every host address of this machine's private (RFC 1918) IPv4 subnets, not of virtual bridges: subnets of /24 or smaller in full, larger ones only the /24 containing this machine's address; network, broadcast and own addresses skipped; at most 512 addresses; from one unconnected, unprivileged UDP socket, paced at ≤ 200 packets/s. The kernel resolves each address with ARP before sending, so devices that answer ARP appear in the neighbour table; nothing is read back. The scan counts as done 3 s after the last packet. One scan at a time (409), at most one start per 60 s (429); 503 in bridge mode, on systems other than Linux and without a private IPv4 subnet. No IPv6 scan. A running scan ends with the process.

---

## 18. DHCP server (`internal/dhcp`)

Optional, for routers that cannot hand out another DNS server: PiCache hands out IPv4 addresses (DHCPv4) and announces itself as IPv6 DNS server (router advertisements with RDNSS/DNSSL, stateless DHCPv6). It is available on every Linux installation and switched on in the web UI (DNS → DHCP) when needed; it never serves by surprise: off by default, not with `PICACHE_DHCP=off`, only on the configured interface, never in the known-dangerous situations (18.2), and it never becomes the IPv6 default router. No DHCPv6 address leases, no prefix delegation, no DHCP relay support.

### 18.1 Privileges and sockets

- **Nothing is held while `dhcp.enabled` is false**: no UDP 67, no UDP 547, no raw ICMPv6 socket (only the short search of `POST /dhcp/probe` opens UDP 67, 18.3, and the legacy `PICACHE_DHCP=on` holds them from start until the service is built). UDP 546 (the DHCPv6 client port the host's own DHCPv6 client uses) is never bound, like port 68.
- **Markers** in the data directory, kept only by the DHCP service: empty regular files, 0640, created and removed through the data directory's `os.Root` (never through a symbolic link): `dhcp.sockets` exists exactly while `dhcp.enabled` is true, `dhcp.ra` exactly while `dhcp.enabled` and `dhcp.ipv6.routerAdvertisements` are true; both only when DHCP is not opted out, on Linux and not in a bridge network. The service reconciles them after build (every start, a restored backup), every 30 s and after every settings change (including `POST /dhcp/reset`). A failed write is logged (at most hourly) and shown in the status (`markerError` in every state, and `reason` outside `unavailable`); with `restart-required` it means a restart will not open the ports, so the health check fails then, and it warns while DHCP is switched on otherwise. `install.sh` creates both markers once in place of a removed `PICACHE_DHCP=on` (never through a symbolic link), so the first start after such an upgrade still opens what the opt-in opened. They grant nothing the settings do not.
- **At start** (`bindListeners`, before the privilege drop, like the listeners): each marker is checked with `lstat` only (never opened or read; only a regular file counts). With `dhcp.sockets`: UDP `0.0.0.0:67` (`SO_BROADCAST`, `IP_PKTINFO`: the ingress interface of every packet, and replies leave through the configured interface with PiCache's address as source) and UDP `[::]:547` (`IPV6_RECVPKTINFO`, `IPV6_MULTICAST_LOOP` 0). With `dhcp.ra` and `CAP_NET_RAW` in the effective set: the raw ICMPv6 socket for the router advertisements (hop limit 255, an `ICMP6_FILTER` that passes router solicitations (133) and advertisements (134) only, `IPV6_RECVPKTINFO` and `IPV6_RECVHOPLIMIT`, no multicast loopback). It also records, before the drop, whether the process could bind ports below 1024 (euid 0 or `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE` effective) and held `CAP_NET_RAW`; they decide the reason codes. `PICACHE_DHCP=off` opens nothing and ignores the markers (reason code `opt-out`); `PICACHE_DHCP=on` (the opt-in before 0.8.0) opens everything as if both markers existed, and the service closes what the settings do not need right after build, so an installation that used `--with-dhcp` keeps sending router advertisements after an update without a restart.
- **While running** the service opens UDP 67 and 547 itself when `dhcp.enabled` becomes true (or is true after start) and they are not open. Under systemd this needs no restart and no new privilege: `CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE` stays ambient for the process lifetime (as for :53). Permission denied although the process could bind them at start (Docker: the switch to `PICACHE_RUN_AS` cleared every capability) → state `unavailable`, reason code `restart-required`; the marker is written, so the next start opens the ports (PiCache never restarts by itself; the UI offers the restart). Anything else (another DHCP server on this host holds the port; permission denied without the capability at start) → `unavailable`, reason code `socket`, retried with every evaluation (30 s) and settings change while enabled, so stopping the other server is picked up. A failed UDP 547 alone blocks DHCPv6 (`no-socket`).
- **Switching off**: serving stops (withdrawal advertisement with lifetime 0, groups left), then UDP 67, UDP 547 and the raw socket are closed and both markers removed. Switching only the router advertisements off withdraws them, closes the raw socket and removes `dhcp.ra`; since the raw socket opens only at start, switching them on again needs a restart (reason code `restart-required`). Each open socket has one reader goroutine that starts with the open and ends with the close; a socket closed while a packet is handled drops only that packet.
- **Dropping `CAP_NET_RAW`** (fail closed; `app.dropNetRaw` after the privilege drop on every start, whether or not a raw socket was opened): (1) the capability sets of every thread are read (`/proc/self/task/*/status`: `CapEff`, `CapPrm`, `CapAmb`); (2) if any thread holds `CAP_NET_RAW`, or the sets cannot be read, the ambient capability is lowered (`PR_CAP_AMBIENT_LOWER`) and it is cleared from the effective, permitted and inheritable sets on every thread (`syscall.AllThreadsSyscall` with `SYS_CAPSET`; the binary is built without cgo) — an error here is fatal when step 1 showed the capability; (3) opening a raw ICMPv6 socket must now fail (EPERM, EACCES or EAFNOSUPPORT) and, when step 1 was readable, no thread may list the capability any more; otherwise `app.Run` returns "CAP_NET_RAW could not be dropped: …; refusing to run with it" and the process exits; (4) if step 1 could not be read at all (e.g. a kernel without `CapAmb`) but step 3 passed, the raw socket is closed (router advertisement reason code `drop-unverified`), the error is logged and the health check `dhcp` fails ("could not verify that CAP_NET_RAW was dropped") whether or not DHCP is enabled; DNS keeps running. The systemd unit grants the capability and re-allows `capset` (6.2); in Docker the switch to `PICACHE_RUN_AS` clears every capability and the socket opened before stays usable.
- Multicast groups are joined only while needed: `ff02::2` on the raw socket (router solicitations) while advertisements are sent, `ff02::1:2` on UDP 547 while DHCPv6 answers.
- With `PICACHE_DHCP=off`, on systems other than Linux and in a container bridge network (DHCP broadcasts do not reach the container) the service is `unavailable` (reason codes `opt-out`, `not-linux`, `bridge`); the tables exist anyway (backups and restores are the same everywhere), the settings and reservations can be edited, and the names of stored leases are answered until they expire.

### 18.2 States and safety gates

State: `unavailable` (with a reason code: `opt-out`, `not-linux`, `bridge`, `socket`, `restart-required`), `off` (`dhcp.enabled` false; `available` true on a supported installation), `blocked`, `serving`, `error` (a configuration or runtime problem that is not a blocker: no router address because the IPv4 default gateway is not in the subnet, a failed probe). The gates are evaluated at start, every 30 s and on settings changes; while `enabled` is true PiCache serves only if none applies:

- `dynamic-address`: PiCache's own IPv4 address on the interface has a finite valid lifetime (netlink `RTM_GETADDR`, `IFA_CACHEINFO`), i.e. a DHCP client configured it. It cannot be overridden: once the router's DHCP is off PiCache would lose its own address. The host (or container) needs a static address; a reservation in the router is not enough.
- `other-server`: another DHCP server answered a probe (18.3) within the last 10 minutes, or a client's broadcast DHCPREQUEST named another server identifier (option 54) within the last 24 hours (passive detection on port 67, which runs while UDP 67 is open, i.e. while DHCP is switched on; the mandatory probe before serving is the protection before that), unless `dhcp.ignoreOtherServers`. It is checked before serving starts: while PiCache serves on the same interface a newly seen server raises a health warning but does not stop serving. Before serving starts (after enabling, after a restart, after a change of the interface) the service probes and serves only when nothing answered (only a probe that ended after this start attempt began counts, so switching off and on always searches again); until then the state is `blocked` with `other-server` (the health check reports this phase as ok). A search started through the API that finds no server forgets the REQUEST detections of the interface from before it (typically right after the router's DHCP server was switched off); a server that is still active shows up again with the next client's REQUEST. At most 32 detected servers are kept, the least recently seen go first.
- `no-interface`: the interface is missing, down, a loopback or virtual interface (bridges, veth, tunnels as in 7.1 step 9), has no Ethernet address, or has not exactly one RFC 1918 IPv4 address (the subnet served).
- `range`: the range does not lie in the subnet, the subnet is smaller than /30, or no address of the range can be handed out.

The settings API checks enabled and changed settings against the live interface (fields `dhcp.interface`, `dhcp.rangeStart`, `dhcp.rangeEnd`, `dhcp.router`, `dhcp.options.ntpServers[i]`: not the subnet's network or broadcast address); the settings package checks only their form. Health check `dhcp` (only while enabled or available, always after `drop-unverified`): `ok` while off or serving without warnings; `fail` while enabled with reason code `socket` (UDP 67 could not be opened) or `restart-required` with a marker that could not be written, and after `drop-unverified`; `warn` while enabled with reason code `restart-required` ("DHCP is switched on but its ports open only at start: restart PiCache"), while blocked, in `error` or otherwise unavailable, when another server was seen while serving, when router advertisements are enabled but not sent (with their reason code), when another router announces its own DNS server (18.5), or when the markers could not be updated while enabled. Messages and hints name the blocker and never mention an installation option; health warnings produce notifications (15.1). `POST /dhcp/reset` (A) puts the section `dhcp` back to its defaults through `settings.Update` (the normal switch-off path runs), then empties `dhcp_static` and `dhcp_leases` in one transaction and clears the in-memory table, offers, quarantine and names; other-server detections, the last probe, the exchange log and the other IPv6 announcers are kept (observations of the network: the gate keeps protecting after a reset). When emptying the tables fails after the settings were reset, the request fails and `dhcp.reset` is audited anyway (the settings change took effect). `DELETE /dhcp/leases` (A) ends every lease the same way without touching reservations and settings.

### 18.3 Probe

Conflict detection without port 68 (the host's own DHCP client may use it): a DHCPDISCOVER as a relay agent (`giaddr` = PiCache's address, `hops` 1, `chaddr` = the interface's MAC, a random `xid`, a parameter request list) to `255.255.255.255:67` out of the interface. Servers answer relayed requests unicast to `giaddr:67`, i.e. to PiCache's server socket, which hands BOOTREPLY offers with the `xid` that arrive on the interface to the running probe (server identifier, source address, offered address; at most 16). PiCache never answers relayed requests, so its own DISCOVER, which the kernel loops back, is ignored, and offers naming PiCache are dropped too. Offers are collected for 5 s: servers that ping an address before offering it answer late (dnsmasq waits 3 s). The MAC of each server comes from the neighbour table. A probe replaces the earlier probe answers of the interface. It runs on `POST /dhcp/probe` (the configured interface, else the default-route interface; one per 10 s through the API; while DHCP is off UDP 67 is opened for that probe only and closed afterwards — in an installation that opens the ports only at start the search needs DHCP switched on), before serving starts and every 10 minutes while serving; one probe at a time.

### 18.4 DHCPv4

- **Acceptance**: datagrams of 240 to 1500 bytes that arrived on the configured interface (`IP_PKTINFO` ifindex) from `0.0.0.0` or an address of the subnet; `op` BOOTREQUEST, `htype` 1, `hlen` 6, `giaddr` 0 (relayed requests are never answered), a unicast client MAC, a message type. Options: bounded by the packet, repeated options concatenated (RFC 3396), pads skipped, an end option required, anything after it ignored; option overload (52) is never honoured (`sname` and `file` are not parsed). Malformed packets are dropped and counted. At most 50 packets per second in total and 5 per second per client MAC (fixed one-second windows; excess dropped and counted). A failure while handling one packet drops that packet only.
- **Messages**: DISCOVER → OFFER (no answer when the pool is exhausted), or with rapid commit an ACK (below); REQUEST (selecting, init-reboot, renewing, rebinding) → ACK or NAK; a REQUEST naming another server identifier is recorded (18.2) and not answered; DECLINE (of the address the client holds or was offered) → the lease ends and the address is quarantined for 10 minutes; RELEASE → the lease ends (the address stays reserved for the client); INFORM (from an address of the subnet) → ACK with the options, no lease.
- **Only reserved devices** (`dhcp.onlyReserved`, default off): DHCPv4 messages of a client without a reservation (the lookup below) are ignored: DISCOVER, REQUEST in every state and DECLINE get no OFFER, ACK or NAK (logged as `ignored`, `not-reserved`). A RELEASE of a PiCache lease is processed and INFORM answered. Existing leases of such clients are neither renewed nor ended; they expire, and their names are answered until then — so PiCache can serve the known devices next to another server (with `ignoreOtherServers`) without knocking the other server's clients off their addresses. A convenience, not an access control: MAC addresses and client identifiers can be forged.
- **Authoritative for its subnet**: a REQUEST is nak'ed for an address outside the subnet, a reserved address (network, broadcast, PiCache's, the router's, the DNS server's), the address of another client's reservation, an address with an active lease or a pending offer of another client, a quarantined address, an address kept for another client (storage, below), an address outside the range that is not the client's reserved address, and a dynamic address while the client has a free reserved address (so it moves there; logged `move-to-reservation`). An address of the range without any record is acknowledged after the neighbour check (below): a client that got it from the previous DHCP server keeps it.
- **Address choice** for a DISCOVER: the client's reserved address; else its current or previous lease; else the requested address (option 50) if free and in the range; else the lowest free address; when the range is exhausted the address whose lease of another client expired first. An offered address is held for its client for 60 s.
- **Neighbour check**: before offering or acknowledging an address without a lease of the client, PiCache primes it like the clients package does (one empty UDP datagram to its discard port, so the kernel resolves it with ARP) and reads the neighbour table after 5, 10, 15 and 20 ms: another device's MAC quarantines the address for 10 minutes and the next one is tried (at most 4 checks per DISCOVER).
- **Rapid commit** (option 80, RFC 4039; `dhcp.rapidCommit`, default off): a DISCOVER carrying option 80 is answered with an ACK carrying option 80 only while no other DHCP server counts for the interface and `ignoreOtherServers` is off; otherwise with the normal OFFER. The rapid ACK goes through exactly the path of the OFFER and the REQUEST that follows it for the chosen address: address choice with the neighbour check, the pending offer (so an address kept for another client's expired lease is acknowledged as after an OFFER), the request decision, `onlyReserved`, making room, the lease write and the names refresh (logged `rapid-commit`); where a REQUEST would get a NAK it gets no answer.
- **Options** in OFFER and ACK: 1 subnet mask, 3 router (`dhcp.router`, else the IPv4 default gateway, which must be in the subnet; the last one seen is used while the routing table has none), 6 DNS server (`dhcp.dnsServer`, else PiCache's address), 15 domain name, 28 broadcast address, 51 lease time, 54 server identifier (PiCache's address), 58/59 T1/T2 (50 % / 87.5 %), 12 host name (the lease's, never a generated name); only when the client's parameter request list asks for them: 119 domain search (the effective domain `dhcp.domain`, else `dns.localDomain`, first, then `dhcp.options.extraSearchDomains`; DNS wire format without compression; extras that do not fit the reply, or that a later change of the local domain pushed over 255 bytes, are dropped from the end, the domain never), 42 NTP servers (`dhcp.options.ntpServers`, 4 bytes each), 26 interface MTU (`dhcp.options.mtu`, 2 bytes), 252 WPAD URL (`dhcp.options.wpadUrl`, the URL bytes without NUL; every DHCP client that asks uses this proxy configuration, so a change is audited with the value). The INFORM answer carries the same options without lease times. Extra search domains are IPv4 only (not in DNSSL, not in the DHCPv6 domain list). 53 and 54 come first, then the options of the client's parameter request list in its order, then the rest. A reply never exceeds 576 bytes unless the client's option 57 allows more (up to 1500); options that do not fit are left out; replies are padded to 300 bytes. A NAK carries 53 and 54 only.
- **Delivery**: a client with `ciaddr` gets the reply unicast to `ciaddr:68`; everything else, and every NAK, is broadcast to `255.255.255.255:68` out of the interface (`IP_PKTINFO`). No raw sockets, no ARP injection.
- **Client identification**: the MAC (`chaddr`) is the key of a reservation. One lookup decides a client's reservation for the address choice, the REQUEST decision, `onlyReserved`, the host name it holds, its lease time, `Lease.static` and `StaticLease.active`: (1) the reservation whose MAC is `chaddr` (a MAC match always wins); (2) else the reservation whose `clientId` equals the packet's option 61 (colon-separated hex), but only when no active lease and no pending offer of another MAC holds the reserved address and the neighbour check finds no other device on it; otherwise the client is served as unreserved (logged `client-id-conflict`). When a client-ID match is used, an expired lease row of another MAC on the reserved address is deleted in the same write that stores the new lease. A reservation's `leaseSeconds` (0 = the global `dhcp.leaseSeconds`, else 300–604800; infinite leases are left out on purpose: they could never be reclaimed, and a reservation keeps the address anyway) sets the lease time and T1/T2 on its address. Client-ID matching is as forgeable as MAC matching: a convenience, not a security control.
- **Storage** (picache.db, component `dhcp`, own append-only migrations): `dhcp_static(mac TEXT PRIMARY KEY, ip TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE, hostname, comment, client_id (unique while set), lease_seconds, created_at, updated_at)` (≤ 1024; migration 2 added `client_id` and `lease_seconds`), `dhcp_leases(mac TEXT PRIMARY KEY, ip TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE, hostname, client_id, expires_at, updated_at)` (≤ 4096; when full, the lease that expired first makes room). Timestamps are unix milliseconds. Leases are written on every change and survive restarts; expired leases are kept 24 h to hand the same address back and are purged every 10 minutes. Rows an edited database made invalid are skipped at load. Backups include both tables (nothing secret). Reservations can be exported (CSV with a formula guard, or hosts lines) and imported (CSV, hosts, `mac ip [hostname]` lines; validated like single reservations plus batch rules, all or nothing in one transaction, a dry run for the preview; API.md).
- **Host names**: option 12, else the first label of option 81, sanitised to a DNS label (lower-case `a-z0-9-`, space and underscore become `-`, other characters are dropped, at most 63, no leading or trailing `-`; empty → none); names of the form `<a>-<b>-<c>-<d>` (so a device cannot take another address's generated name) and `wpad` and `localhost` (so WPAD cannot be hijacked) count as none. A renewal without a name keeps the previous one. A reservation's host name wins and is reserved for its client (a reservation may name `wpad` or `localhost` explicitly; a name of the generated form must be its own address's). A name held by another active lease is not used for the second one (`nameConflict` in the API); a lease keeps its name while it is active, free names go to active leases in MAC order.
- **Exchange log**: the last 200 handled exchanges in memory (lost on restart): DHCPv4 packets that passed parsing and the rate limits while serving and were answered, nak'ed, processed (DECLINE, RELEASE) or ignored by a rule (`onlyReserved`, a REQUEST for another server, pool exhausted); DHCPv6 information requests answered; router solicitations answered with an advertisement. Malformed, rate-limited, foreign-interface or relayed packets, packets while not serving and periodic advertisements are only counted. `GET /dhcp/log` (API.md).

### 18.5 IPv6 announcements

The router advertisements and stateless DHCPv6 need DHCP enabled and available, a usable interface and a **stable ULA** of PiCache on it (not temporary, not deprecated, not tentative; a statically configured one first; global addresses change with the prefix and are never announced), otherwise they are `blocked` (`no-interface`, `no-ula`, `no-raw-socket` or `no-socket`). The IPv4 blockers (`dynamic-address`, `other-server`, `range`) do not stop them. The router's own DNS announcement should be switched off where possible, otherwise devices may keep asking the router. The router advertisements' status has a reason code while they are not available: `dhcp-unavailable`, `restart-required` (enabled; the raw socket opens at the next start), `no-cap-net-raw` (no `CAP_NET_RAW` at this start, also reported while the option is off: an older unit or container configuration), `drop-unverified` (18.1), `socket`; while the option is off and `CAP_NET_RAW` was held at start they count as available (switching them on needs one restart).

- **Router advertisement** (package `ra`): ICMPv6 type 134 code 0; current hop limit 0; M = 0, O = 1 while DHCPv6 answers; **router lifetime 0** (PiCache never becomes a default router; the router keeps doing SLAAC and routing); reachable time 0; retransmission timer 0; options: source link-layer address (the interface's MAC), RDNSS (25; lifetime 1800 s; the ULA), DNSSL (31; lifetime 1800 s; the domain in DNS wire format, padded to 8 bytes; left out without a domain). No prefix information, no MTU, no route information.
- **Sending**: to `ff02::1` out of the interface (`IPV6_PKTINFO`; the kernel picks the link-local source), hop limit 255. 3 initial advertisements 16 s apart, then one every random 200–600 s. A router solicitation received on the interface (hop limit 255, code 0, at least 8 bytes, a link-local or unspecified source, well-formed options, no source link-layer address with the unspecified source; not from an address of this machine, such as PiCache's own solicitation of the search below reflected by a bridge) leads to a multicast advertisement after a random 0–500 ms, not sooner than 3 s after the previous one; it merges into an unsolicited advertisement that is due anyway. A new announcement (another address, domain or interface) restarts the initial burst.
- **Withdrawal**: when the advertisements are switched off, the interface, address or domain changes, or PiCache stops, one advertisement with RDNSS and DNSSL lifetime 0 is sent (best effort).
- **Stateless DHCPv6** (package `dhcpv6`): information requests (type 11) that arrived on the interface from a link-local address, to `ff02::1:2` or a link-local address, at most 50 per second, get a REPLY (type 7) with SERVERID (a DUID-LL from the interface's MAC), the client's CLIENTID (echoed), DNS_SERVERS (23, the ULA), DOMAIN_LIST (24, the domain) and INFORMATION_REFRESH_TIME (32, 3600 s). Every option is bounds-checked (packets of 4 to 1500 bytes; DUIDs of 3 to 130 bytes; a repeated identifier is malformed); requests for another server (a foreign SERVERID) or with an IA option, other message types and malformed packets are ignored and counted. No addresses are leased; relay messages are never answered.
- **Other IPv6 announcers**: PiCache looks for other routers and DHCPv6 servers that announce DNS when its IPv6 announcements start (without delaying them) and every 10 minutes while they run; one search at a time, listening 3 s (`ipv6.lastSearch`). DHCPv6 part (no new socket; UDP 546 is never bound): from UDP 547 a Relay-Forward (type 12, hop count 0, link-address = the announced ULA, peer-address = PiCache's link-local address on the interface) carrying RELAY_MSG with an Information-Request (type 11, random transaction ID, CLIENTID = PiCache's DUID-LL, ELAPSED_TIME 0, ORO 23 and 24) and an INTERFACE_ID option, to `ff02::1:2` port 547 out of the interface — only an Information-Request (no Solicit, no IA, no rapid commit), so it never creates a binding. Servers answer with a Relay-Reply (type 13) to PiCache's address on port 547; one that arrives on the interface, carries exactly one level of RELAY_MSG with a Reply of the search's transaction ID and names the search's peer-address is handed to the search (source address, SERVERID, DNS_SERVERS); every other Relay-Reply is ignored and counted, as are answers with PiCache's own DUID or from an address of this machine. Servers that do not answer relayed messages are seen only through the M/O flags of router advertisements. The UDP 547 reader runs while the socket is open; while DHCPv6 answering is off it only routes search answers. RA part (only while the raw socket is open, i.e. while PiCache's own router advertisements are on; otherwise `lastSearch.ra` is false): one router solicitation (type 133, code 0, hop limit 255, source link-layer address = the interface's MAC) to `ff02::2`; the ICMPv6 filter passes type 134 too, and **every** router advertisement received on the interface is considered (passive): hop limit 255, link-local source, code 0, at least 16 bytes, every option bounds-checked (length > 0, inside the packet; at most 8 DNS addresses kept); at most 50 are parsed per second (excess dropped); those from an address of this machine or with the MAC of one of its interfaces are ignored. Records (at most 32 in total, least recently seen evicted): `ra` — an RA announcing DNS (RDNSS lifetime > 0) or DHCPv6 (M or O) creates or updates the record of its source, one announcing neither removes it, RDNSS addresses with lifetime 0 are removed at once, and the record expires max(router lifetime, longest RDNSS lifetime) after its last RA, at most after 1 h; `dhcpv6` — one per server that answered a search, replaced by the next search of the interface, expiring 10 minutes after the search. A record warns (`conflict`) while PiCache sends router advertisements or answers DHCPv6 when it carries a DNS address that is not an address of this machine (so a router that announces PiCache's ULA, as DEPLOYMENT recommends for the FRITZ!Box, never warns) and was seen at least twice (two RAs, or two searches in a row); M/O flags alone never warn (the UI shows them as "offers DHCPv6"). Each record lists which of its DNS addresses are addresses of this machine (`ownDns`), so the UI names only the others. Conflicts show in the DHCP page and as a health warning `dhcp` ("another router announces its own DNS server (<addr>) …", the fix being on the router), which notifies like every health warning. The network check (17) reports the default router's RDNSS (`routerRdnss`) from these records.
- **Status**: the advertisements' state and reason code, the announced address, the last one sent, the count and the solicitations answered, the last error; DHCPv6 replies and ignored packets; the other announcers and the last search.

### 18.6 Names of leases

With `dhcp.registerHostnames`, every active lease with a host name (and a domain) answers `<host>.<domain>` (A → the lease address) and the PTR of the lease address (→ `<host>.<domain>`) in step 7 of the pipeline (7.1) as part of the local zone, with TTL min(300 s, remaining lease); local records win (same name, a matching wildcard, or the auto-PTR of the same address). With `dhcp.generateNames` (default on) an active lease without a usable host name, or whose name another client holds (`nameConflict`), answers the generated name `<a>-<b>-<c>-<d>.<domain>` of its address instead (A and PTR, same TTL, local records win). Generated names are only DNS answers: never sent in option 12, never the lease's host name and never the clients registry's name (`Lease.dnsName` carries it with `nameGenerated`). Bare single labels are not answered in step 7; while `dns.domainNeeded` is on, step 11a answers a bare `<host>` (A, AAAA, HTTPS, SVCB, ANY) as `<host>.<localDomain>` from local records and then these lease names (7.1), except `wpad` and `isatap`, which only local records answer. The names are an immutable snapshot rebuilt on every lease change (one map lookup per query). The clients registry uses a lease's DNS name (else its host name) as the first name source of the address and of the device's other addresses (7.1 step 5), so the query log and client lists show them; `POST /dns/lookup` traces `DHCP lease name`. The network check (17) reports `dhcp.serving` (the router's IPv4 DNS steps then do not apply) and `dhcp.routerAdvertisements`.

### 18.7 Limits

DHCPv4 50 packets/s (5/s per MAC), DHCPv6 50 packets/s, other routers' advertisements parsed 50/s, one answer to router solicitations per 3 s; pool at most 4096 addresses; 4096 leases, 1024 reservations, 32 other servers, 32 other IPv6 announcers (8 DNS addresses each), 16 servers per probe, 200 logged exchanges; one probe at a time, 5 s, one per 10 s through the API; one search for other IPv6 announcers at a time, 3 s, every 10 minutes; pending offers 60 s; quarantine 10 minutes; expired leases kept 24 h; imports at most 256 KiB and 1024 rows; repeated errors are logged at most hourly.

---

## 19. Encrypted DNS (DoT and DoH for clients)

**Settings** (`dns.encrypted {dot, doh, serverName}`, `dns.plainDns`; API.md): the server name is the name devices use for DoT and DoH and a name of PiCache itself (recommended `picache.<localDomain>`, which the local CA covers). It is trimmed, lower-cased, without a trailing dot; required while DoT or DoH is on; a host name of at least two labels and at most 189 characters (so `<ClientID>.` still fits), never an IP address, a special-use name (`localhost`, `invalid`, `onion`, `arpa`; names below `home.arpa` are allowed), the local domain or a parent of it, and (checked by the API against the running system) a search domain or a parent of one. Plain DNS can be switched off only while DoT or DoH is on, and the API accepts that only while an enabled protocol has a bound listener; a restore that turns plain DNS off without one is staged with a warning (`dnsWarning`).

**Serving** (per protocol): on, at least one listener bound (DoT: `PICACHE_DOT_LISTEN`; DoH: `PICACHE_WEB_TLS_LISTEN` or `PICACHE_DOH_LISTEN`; a plain web listener behind a proxy never counts) and the certificate usable (no fallback, not expired). `app` rebuilds an immutable snapshot (`dnsserver.EncryptedState`: serving, ports, the leaf's IP addresses) on settings, listener and certificate changes and every tick; the DNS server reads it with one atomic load per query.

**DoT** (`PICACHE_DOT_LISTEN`, default `:853`): `netutil.LimitDNSListener` (DNS ACL, 32/1024, the heaviest client gives way at the total) → a switch that closes connections while DoT is off and closes the open ones when it is switched off → TLS (ALPN `dot`, the certificate of 2) → miekg's DNS server: the queries of a connection are answered one after another, in order (pipelining without reordering; queries sent ahead wait in the socket), the handshake and the first message within 10 s, a started message within the idle time of 30 s, every reply written within 10 s, 1024 queries per connection. Each query checks `dns.encrypted.dot` again. The ClientID is the first label of an SNI `<ClientID>.<serverName>` with a valid label; any other SNI (none, the server name, another name) completes the handshake without one. Apple devices and Android's Private DNS use port 853 only.

**DoH** (RFC 8484, `/dns-query` and `/dns-query/<ClientID>`): one implementation (`dnsserver.ServeDoH`): DoH off → 404; a method other than GET or POST → 405; browsers across sites (`Sec-Fetch-Site` `cross-site`/`same-site`, a foreign `Origin`) → 403; the DNS ACL on the source → 403 (counted as refused; before the ClientID and the message are read, so a source outside the allowed networks gets nothing parsed and learns nothing about its request); an invalid ClientID → 400; GET (`?dns=` base64url) or POST (`application/dns-message`, ≤ 64 KiB) else 400/415/413; a message that does not unpack, has QR set or no question → 400; then the pipeline with protocol `doh` (a drop resets only the stream; rate limited → 200 REFUSED; more than 64 requests of a client key in flight → 429 with `Retry-After: 1`, beyond 4096 keys or 4096 queries in flight → 503). Replies keep the query's ID and question case and carry `Cache-Control: private, max-age=<smallest TTL>` (0 without records and for rcodes other than NOERROR/NXDOMAIN); errors carry `no-store`. On the web listeners the middleware of 12 applies first (web ACL, host allowlist with the server name, no HTTPS redirect for these paths, CrossOriginProtection); DoH is answered only for an effective scheme of https (404 on plain HTTP without a trusted proxy that sends `X-Forwarded-Proto: https`), a peer that is not trusted but sends `X-Forwarded-For`, `Forwarded` or `X-Real-IP` gets 403, and the source is the effective client, so a TLS-terminating proxy in `web.trustedProxies` can forward `/dns-query` to the plain web listener. The dedicated listeners (`PICACHE_DOH_LISTEN`, default off) chain like DoT into an own HTTP server (headers 5 s, which also bounds the handshake, read 10 s, write 15 s, idle 30 s, headers ≤ 16 KiB, 32 HTTP/2 streams) that serves only the DoH paths (a bare 404 otherwise); the source is the TCP peer and proxy headers get 403.

**Pipeline**: DoT and DoH queries run the pipeline of 7.1 like TCP (health probe, ACL, blocked sources and ClientIDs, rate limit, identification with ClientIDs, …); the plain-DNS gate (3a) does not apply to them; replies are padded (step 15); the query log records the protocol and the ClientID; answered queries are counted since the start (`GET /dns/encrypted`).

**Plain-DNS switch** (step 3a): closed only while `dns.plainDns` is off **and** a protocol serves. Otherwise plain DNS keeps serving everyone (availability first), the health check `encrypted-dns` fails and an ERROR is logged once per change of that state.

**ClientIDs**: one DNS label (`^[a-z0-9]([a-z0-9-]{0,61}[a-z0-9])?$` after lower-casing) from the DoT SNI or the DoH path (never from Host or SNI of DoH), never from UDP or TCP. Clients list them as identifiers `clientid:<ClientID>` (one client per ClientID); `dns.blockedClients` accepts `clientid:` entries. Identification: 7.1 step 5 (a ClientID never changes the client of a device identified by address or MAC and never unblocks). Seen data: 11. `POST /dns/lookup` evaluates a query with `dnsClientId` as a DoT/DoH query.

**DDR**: 7.1 step 6 (`_dns.resolver.arpa` SVCB, verified discovery only with a certificate that has one of the answered addresses: the local CA's leaf, on devices that trust the CA).

**Certificates**: the certificate of 2 serves every TLS listener; the server name is an own name (and `*.<serverName>` while DoT is on); Android's Private DNS and most browsers need a publicly trusted certificate for the server name (a wildcard needs DNS-01), the local CA works only on devices that trust it.

**Apple configuration profiles** (`GET /dns/profile.mobileconfig`, `POST /dns/profile-links`, `GET /dns/profile-links/{token}`; API.md): an unsigned property list (`com.apple.dnsSettings.managed`; DoH with `ServerURL` or DoT with `ServerName`; optional `ServerAddresses`; on demand only on the listed Wi-Fi networks, else everywhere), identifiers stable per instance, protocol and ClientID; downloads only over HTTPS or on this machine; links (in memory, 15 minutes, at most 32) point at `https://<serverName>` on the HTTPS web listener.

**Limits**: no QUIC, DNSCrypt or HTTP/3 listener; no access from outside the DNS ACL (devices away from home use a VPN: Tailscale's 100.64.0.0/10 is allowed by default, a WireGuard network is added to `dns.allowedNetworks`); DoT answers the queries of a connection in order (no parallel answers on one connection).
